64. **2026-11-10, Throughput_7 (slim): OptiX is faster than our slim kernel, so there is no OptiX overhead to report.** On the same rays (71 and 142 SMs worth), OptiX finishes whole rays in 0.52 to 0.74 of the time our slim kernel needs for the first query only (random rays on 6.2 and 186 MB BVHs; 1.9 to 2 queries per ray), and in 0.2 of the time on a coherent one triangle ray. Per query OptiX is 2.6 to 3.6 times faster. So the earlier 11 to 22 times gap was our harness. Later ablations (Finding 78) showed that most of the remaining gap was our own load and store instructions, not an RT core limit: they cut it from 3.5 times to 1.6 times.
65. **2026-11-10, SM to GPC map.** The clock64 counter is one counter per GPC, so SMs can be grouped by reading it everywhere at once: 12 GPCs, GPC = (SM id / 2) mod 12, eleven with 6 TPCs and one with 5 (71 TPCs, 142 SMs). Consecutive blocks of a launch land on different GPCs.
66. **2026-11-10, random ray plateau: the L2 sector rate and a per GPC limit.** A plain pointer chase on a 6 MB table reaches 38 L2 sectors per SM cycle at 142 SMs (ncu: L2 at 99% of peak) and no more; load latency stays flat at about 280 cycles until the load nears that rate and then rises as Little's law says. One GPC moves at most about 6 sectors per SM cycle however many of its 12 SMs load, so 12 SMs packed in one GPC deliver half of 12 spread SMs. The slim random ray runs put 32 sectors per cycle on the L2, about 80% of the plain load ceiling (the RT core's own 45 sectors per ray plus our result stores), which explains the plateau; OptiX, without our stores, goes about twice as far.
67. **2026-11-10, Latency_3 and Latency_4: what a node, a leaf and a triangle cost.** A traversal that visits everything, with L2 warm and L1 cold, costs about 934 cycles plus 343 per node (one L2 round trip), 113 per leaf and 52 per triangle. Children of a node are handled in groups of three. An extra triangle in an existing leaf costs about 45 cycles against 156 for an extra leaf with one triangle; a leaf holds at least 4 triangles. (Correction 2026-11-16, F163: the 934 is the intercept of a regression for a cold L1 and includes about one L2 line per query, the OptiX instance node, that is not counted as a node. The fixed cost of a query with every line in L1 is about 740 cycles, the figure the model uses; Finding 25.)
68. **2026-11-10, Memory_2: the node cache is the SM's L1.** Everything the RT core fetches is found again in a second pass while the footprint is below about 60 KB, half of it is lost at about 100 KB and all at about 700 KB; with shared memory reserved it cannot hold even a few KB. No larger separate cache shows up.
69. **2026-11-10, Memory_3: bytes per query.** A full traversal fetches about 176 bytes of header plus 128 per node visited and 43 per triangle tested; random rays in 100,000 and 3 million triangle scenes fetch 1.4 to 2.2 KB per ray.
70. **2026-11-10, Traversal_1: visit order and early exit.** Children and leaves are visited by distance along the ray, not by memory order; inside one leaf the triangles are tested in storage order and the first hit found is returned, so the first returned hit is not always the closest even in one leaf. Rays are cut at tmax: boxes beyond it are never visited (latency falls linearly with the part of the scene the ray reaches).
71. **2026-11-10, Traversal_4: the RT core matches exact arithmetic except on the edge itself.** Rays one float32 step or more off a triangle edge or vertex are classified exactly as in exact arithmetic; rays exactly on an edge follow an ownership rule (two edges count, the third does not, one vertex counts) and offsets of denormal size count as zero. OptiX gives the same answers because it uses the same unit. A shared edge between two triangles was not tested.
72. **2026-11-10, The performance model.** A simple per ray model (queries per ray, RT L1 sector requests, L2 fills and DRAM bytes as inputs; work that does not depend on the number of warps, latency hidden by warps, and a queueing term in warps) fitted on one SM data predicts held out ray orders and scenes on one SM to about 11 to 19 percent mean error. Fitted costs are consistent with the direct measurements: about 660 cycles per warp query matches the 740 cycle fixed cost, and about one L1 sector per cycle matches a plain load. With the TPC scaling and L2 sector ceiling terms it predicts whole GPU throughput of random rays to about 20 to 26 percent, with an overshoot of 15 to 50 percent beyond 71 SMs, and one coherent 64 triangle scene to 16 percent. It fails on the one triangle coherent case (about six times too slow): the queueing term was learned on random rays and there is no measured per node unit cost for coherent rays yet. The model takes measured per ray descriptors as input; it does not yet predict them from a scene.
73. **2026-11-10, Throughput_7 with the resume loop inside the kernel.** Our direct kernel now runs the whole ray (the same traversal group repeated in a loop in the kernel until the ray has no pending work, exactly as OptiX's own traversal loop rebuilds the next query) and every checked lane equals the CPU closest hit, with no hang. OptiX on the same rays is still faster: about 1.8 to 2.6 times on random rays at 71 to 142 SMs and 4 to 5 times on a coherent single triangle scene. So the earlier gap was not host round trips. Remaining differences from OptiX's own launch are one ray per thread, a launch of about 130,000 rays, and the input loads of our loop; the comparison says nothing yet about the RT core itself.
74. **2026-11-10, plateau check.** With the RT core's own traffic only (no plain loads), spreading active SMs over the 12 GPCs instead of packing them into as few GPCs as possible changes whole ray throughput by only 0 to 10 percent (packed slower below 36 SMs, equal at 60); the per GPC limit seen with plain loads (about 6 sectors per cycle) is not reached because the RT core asks for only about a sixth of that per SM. These launches are short (about 0.1 ms), so this is not a steady state test. Grouping random rays by the subtree they hit (an oracle key) lowers whole kernel L2 sectors per ray by 24 to 38 percent and RT L1 sector requests by 16 to 28 percent but raises whole GPU rate only 8 to 9 percent on a 6 MB scene (not at all on a 186 MB scene at all SMs, where DRAM utilization is about 60 percent in both orders), because at the plateau the L2 is at 70 percent (interleaved) or 47 percent (grouped) of its plain load ceiling and the rate of our kernel is set by costs that grouping does not change.
75. **2026-11-10, steady state (long launches) changes the OptiX comparison.** With a persistent kernel (each thread traces many rays, launches of millions of rays lasting 3 to 17 ms) every lane of every run equals OptiX and the CPU. On random rays our direct kernel stops scaling at about 36 SMs and reaches 825 rays per microsecond (small scene) and 726 (large scene), while OptiX reaches 3,000 and 1,760: OptiX is 3.6 and 2.4 times faster, and 10 times on one triangle. The earlier 1.8 to 2.6 was therefore not a launch effect. ncu shows OptiX with half our resident warps (13 against 27 per SM), more registers (113 against 72), about the same number of RT queries in flight (9 to 10 per SM), and 0.6 times our L2 traffic per ray. Our warps spend most of their time throttled in the memory instruction queue. Removing a third of our own harness traffic gives 32% more, which leaves a 2.8 times gap; why our queries complete more slowly at the same concurrency is not yet explained.
76. **2026-11-10, the plateau tests hold at steady state.** Packing active SMs into few GPCs costs 0 to 10% against spreading them (same as with short launches), and ray grouping by hit subtree gains 8 to 13% for our kernel but 2.0 to 2.2 times for OptiX on the same rays. One SM saturates at about 54 cycles per ray on one triangle from 4 warps, far above the one shot unit cost of 7.4 cycles, so the unit is not what limits our kernel.
77. **2026-11-11, what stands and what does not.** The one SM results (latency per level, cache behavior, TPC scaling on coherent rays, per element costs) stand. The whole GPU random ray throughput of our direct kernel, and the plateau seen in it, is limited by our own memory traffic (warps stalled in the memory instruction queue, 1.7 times the L2 sectors per ray that OptiX moves), so it is not a measure of the RT core; the earlier plateau explanations (Findings 62, 63, 66, 74, 76) describe our kernel, not the hardware. Key result: grouping rays by the subtree they hit gives OptiX 2.0 to 2.2 times, but our kernel only 8 to 13 percent, so the locality idea is strong on an efficient pipeline. The grouping key is an oracle, so the next test is a realistic sort (Morton code of ray origin and direction) on OptiX. Plan: future whole GPU throughput tests use OptiX with controlled ray order; our direct kernel is for single SM microbenchmarks.
78. **2026-11-11, most of the gap between our direct kernel and OptiX was our own load and store instructions.** In the persistent kernel on random rays (100,000 triangles, all 142 SMs), loading the lane invariant input words once instead of for every ray gave 66% more throughput, writing one coalesced 16 byte result per ray instead of 13 scattered words gave 17% more, and limiting the kernel to OptiX's 13 warps per SM gave none (but removed the memory instruction queue stalls, 14 stalled warps to about 1). All three together gave 2.3 times, which leaves OptiX 1.6 times faster instead of 3.5 times. Our kernel executes about 5 times fewer instructions per ray than OptiX, so instruction count is not the cause. The gain came with almost no change in L2 sectors per ray, so what mattered was the number of memory instructions through the SM, not the traffic. The remaining gap matches our 1.4 times more L2 sectors per ray. Every ray in every run still equals OptiX's answer. This confirms Finding 77: the whole GPU random ray rates of the direct kernel measure the kernel, not the RT core.
79. **2026-11-11, a Morton sort of the rays recovers the whole 2x that the oracle grouping gave OptiX; OptiX's own reorder (SER) recovers none of it.** On 8.4 million random rays per launch, sorting rays by a Morton code of their origin (4 to 5 bits per axis, a few rays per cell) gives OptiX 2.2 times on a 100,000 triangle scene and 2.5 times on a 3 million triangle scene, the same as or more than the oracle grouping by hit subtree (2.2 and 2.4 times). Adding a coarse direction code on top gives up to 2.5 and 2.6 times; sorting by direction first is poor. Coarse sorts recover less (2 bits per axis: 82% and 30% of the oracle gain on the small and large scene). The sort cost on the host is not included. Using OptiX's reorder call (optixReorder) with the same codes as hints, on random order, makes OptiX 7 to 18 percent slower instead of faster, so ray order has to be fixed before the launch.
80. **2026-11-11, how deep the subtree grouping has to go.** Grouping rays by the subtree they hit, with oracle keys: on the small scene grouping at the first tree level gives 1.3 times, the second level 2.0 times, the third 2.2 times; on the 3 million triangle scene (8.4 million rays) 1.2, 1.5 and 2.0 times, against 2.4 times for the full key. A scene that is bigger needs finer groups for the same gain.
81. **2026-11-11, the number of queries per ray can be predicted from the BVH on a CPU.** A CPU traversal over the decoded BVH (nearest child first, the RT core returns after every leaf that improves the hit, and the ray is resumed whenever anything is still pending) predicts queries per ray of 1.85 and 1.98 against 1.88 and 2.00 measured on the 100,000 and 3 million triangle scenes, and the same count for 94% of individual rays (within one query for more than 99.8%). If a pending entry is dropped when the new hit already makes it useless, the prediction is clearly worse, so the hardware does resume for such entries. The replay is not exact: which triangles sit in which leaf is not decoded and had to be matched by boxes (the closest hit matches OptiX on 97% of rays). Nodes, leaves and triangles per ray are about 13 / 5 / 9 (small scene) and 16 / 6 / 10 (large scene), which with the measured bytes per element gives 10 to 18 percent less memory traffic than measured. A check of the per element cycle costs against measured time per ray is still missing.
82. **2026-11-11, a returned query can carry unfinished work from different tree levels.** In a 100,000 triangle scene, 8 of 32 random rays came back from their first query with pending node pointers at two different depths at once (for example depth 3 and 4, or 1 and 3), which the one level test scenes never showed. So the RT core does keep unfinished siblings at several levels. Observation from one warp; which returned words are entries and which are cursor words is only partly decoded, and the final hits of that run were not compared with the CPU.
83. **2026-11-11, net Morton speedup with a GPU sort: it pays on large incoherent launches only, and only if the rays are not copied.** OptiX, closest hit, a GPU key generation (Morton code of the origin, 4 to 6 bits per axis, or a coarse direction code) and a CUB radix sort of 12 to 24 bit keys, timed together with the trace (10 launches, median; every sorted result equal to the unsorted one for every ray, no mismatches in any run). Launch sizes 1 million, 8.4 million and 33.5 million rays, scenes with 100,000 and 3 million triangles. Cost of the sort: key generation 0.05 / 0.4 / 1.6 ms and the sort itself 0.04 / 0.3 / 1.5 to 2.1 ms at the three sizes, together about half the trace time of the small scene at 8.4 million rays and less than the trace time of the large one. Copying the rays into sorted order and the results back costs much more than the sort (random 32 byte gathers and scatters: 1.6 and 1.8 ms at 8.4 million rays, 6.4 and 8.1 ms at 33.5 million). Random rays, speedup over the unsorted launch, trace alone / sort plus trace without copies / everything including both copies: 100,000 triangles 2.0, 2.4, 2.9 / 1.35, 1.4, 1.6 / 0.68, 0.52, 0.55; 3 million triangles 1.7, 2.4, 3.8 / 1.4, 1.7, 2.7 / 0.81, 0.76, 1.09. So sorting pays (above 1 even with the copies) only for 33.5 million rays on the large scene; without the copies (rays generated in the shader from their index, results written in place) it pays from about 1 million rays on, and the gain grows with the launch size and the scene size. Ray sets with structure: (a) camera rays from a pinhole in raster order are already coherent (a shuffled launch is 1.6 to 3 times slower), sorting them never pays (trace alone 0.9 to 1.05 times, net 0.5 to 0.6 of the raster launch without copies); (b) bounce rays (origin at the primary hit, random direction, in pixel order) gain 1.06 to 1.33 times from sorting the trace alone but lose once the sort is paid (0.7 to 0.8 without copies; they beat a shuffled order by 1.2 to 1.8 times); (c) short radius queries (random origin, random direction, length 0.05 or 0.2 of the scene width): length 0.2 gains like random rays (net without copies 1.25 to 1.4 on the small scene, 1.4 to 2.9 on the large one), length 0.05 costs so little to trace that the sort only pays on the large scene from 8.4 million queries (1.0, 1.6 and 2.5 times). Best key everywhere is the plain origin code (4 or 5 bits per axis); direction first codes are worse on random rays and only match it on camera rays. Rule of thumb: sort when the traced rays start in random places, the launch is millions of rays and the scene is bigger than the L1 and L2 can hold, and avoid physically moving the ray records.
84. **2026-11-11, Finding 82 checked against the CPU: unfinished work at several levels is what a stack does, but the exact entries returned are not reproduced.** The first query of the 32 random rays of Finding 82 was replayed on the CPU (nearest child first, depth first, return at the first leaf that improves the hit). The CPU stack holds pending nodes at two or more depths in 18 of the 32 rays at that point (the RT core returned two depths in 8, since it returns at most two entries). Of the 36 node pointers the RT core returned, 22 are among the CPU's pending nodes at that return; the returned set equals the top of the CPU stack in 15 rays (9 of them with nothing pending) and the nearest entries by entry distance in 11. So the claim that the RT core keeps unfinished siblings at several levels holds, while which two entries it hands back is not predicted by this simple model: the return point, the order in which entries are kept, or the decoding of the returned words differs. Run on saved data from the existing checked group, no new TTU run.
85. **2026-11-11, BVH_4 host built and checked without the RT core: OptiX instancing with real transforms matches a CPU reference.** A scene of four instances of one 20,000 triangle GAS with rotation, uniform scale and translation, traced with OptiX through an instance acceleration structure, agrees with a CPU float64 reference on hit, instance id, primitive and barycentrics for all 728 hits of 1,024 random rays (identity transforms: 735 of 735; one t differs by more than 1e-4). In the acceleration structure the instance level stores each instance's object to world matrix and, in its leaf record, its world to object matrix next to the instance slot and the child GAS root pointer. Not done: running the checked traversal group on an instance level with transforms. The two pointer words the group takes (instance node and start node) are not known for a real instance level, so that needs a dump of OptiX's own launch for an IAS first.
86. **2026-11-11, grouping depth against the node cache: the gain follows whether the nodes resident on one SM fit the L1.** For the three grouping depths of Finding 80 (8.4 million random rays in sorted launch order, CPU replay of every ray over the decoded BVH, nodes are 128 bytes): the nodes touched by all rays of one group are almost the whole tree for the coarse groups (small scene, 904 KB of nodes: 675 KB per group at depth 1, 394 KB at depth 2, 159 KB at depth 3; large scene, 28 MB of nodes: 5.5 MB, 1.4 MB, 383 KB), far above the roughly 60 KB node cache; but the nodes that most rays of a group share (at least 5% of its rays) are only 2 to 9 KB (the upper levels). The better measure is what one SM holds at once: 13 resident warps (the OptiX average from ncu) taken from a launch in sorted order, which come from 1.07 / 2.0 / 7.3 different groups (depth 1 / 2 / 3, small scene; 1.07 / 2.0 / 10 on the large scene; up to 1.3 / 4.9 / 25 and 36 at the hardware limit of 48 warps) because consecutive warps go to different SMs. The distinct nodes those 416 rays touch: small scene 151 KB, 75 KB and 76 KB at depths 1, 2, 3 (speedups 1.34, 2.02, 2.17 in Finding 80); large scene 509 KB, 272 KB and 248 KB (1.19, 1.53, 1.95). So the speedup rises while the resident working set falls toward the L1 size and flattens once it is only a little above it; on the large scene even depth 3 leaves a resident set four times the L1, which matches its speedup still being below the full key's 2.4 times. Each SM sees about as many groups as warps once groups are small (depth 3), so the upper levels shared inside a group are counted several times: the working set per SM is not the sum over groups but is not much smaller than it either.
87. **2026-11-11, TPC_1 first test: the placement works, but OptiX cannot be run beside a plain compute kernel, so the comparison is not made yet.** A persistent plain CUDA compute kernel can be pinned by SM parity: with 4 blocks of 384 threads per SM (every resident thread slot used) on the odd SMs only, all 71 odd SMs hold their 4 blocks and no block stays on an even SM; with 2 blocks per SM on all SMs both parities hold 2 each. Alone, the arithmetic kernel completes 7.6 million block iterations in 150 ms with 2 blocks on every SM and 4.4 million with 4 blocks on the 71 odd SMs (62,000 against 54,000 per SM). But an OptiX launch issued while such a kernel is running did not start until the compute kernel ended (the first launch took the whole 2.2 to 2.7 seconds the compute kernel ran, in both the shared and the odd placement, and with every shared memory carveout setting), after which launches ran at the normal 3 ms; so in one process OptiX and a plain kernel are serialized by something other than free SM resources (not found). Consequently no RT throughput against compute throughput comparison and no node cache interference measurement exist yet. The remaining way is our own traversal kernel (an ordinary CUDA launch) next to the compute kernel in one process, which needs the setup3 host extended and its patched binaries checked again; not built.
88. **2026-11-11, oracle bound for bounce rays: even perfect grouping by the answer gains only 1.2 to 1.3 times over pixel order, and a plain origin Morton sort already gets most of it.** OptiX, closest hit, bounce rays (origin at the camera hit point, random direction, no normals; Finding 83's set), launch order varied, trace time only (10 launches, median, every result equal to the pixel order result for every ray, no mismatches). Oracle = rays sorted by the subtree of their own hit (known only after tracing, so it is a bound, not a method). Speedup over pixel (natural) order, with the shuffled order for reference. Small scene (100,000 triangles), 10.2 million bounce rays: Morton of the origin 1.10 to 1.11, oracle depth 1 / 2 / 3 / full 1.14 / 1.23 / 1.27 / 1.27 (shuffled 0.52). Large scene (3 million triangles), 11.1 million bounce rays: Morton 1.15 to 1.16, oracle 1.04 / 1.16 / 1.25 / 1.27 (shuffled 0.46); 2.8 million rays: Morton 1.11 to 1.13, oracle 1.04 / 1.11 / 1.20 / 1.21. So the whole room a treelet queue could win over pixel order on bounce rays, even with free and perfect grouping, is about 1.2 to 1.3 times, and about 10 to 15 percent over a plain origin Morton sort; the grouping itself would have to cost less than that, and Finding 83 already showed that the sort does not pay for bounce rays. Against a shuffled (incoherent) launch the same groupings are worth 2.1 to 2.7 times, which is the case where grouping matters.
89. **2026-11-11, grouping depth 4 on the 3 million triangle scene: 2.22 times, and still about 3.5 times more nodes per SM than one L1 holds.** Random rays, 8.4 million, OptiX, sorted by the oracle subtree at depth 1 / 2 / 3 / 4 / full: 1.13 / 1.44 / 1.85 / 2.22 / 2.31 times faster than the random order (small scene: 1.37 / 2.00 / 2.13 / 2.16 / 2.17); depth 4 has 22,557 groups of about 370 rays on the large scene. Nodes touched by the rays resident on one SM (13 warps, CPU replay, 128 byte nodes) at depth 1 / 2 / 3 / 4: 509 / 272 / 248 / 210 KB on the large scene against roughly 60 KB of node cache, and for the two SMs of a TPC together 882 / 386 / 345 / 300 KB against about 120 KB of two L1s. At the hardware limit of 48 resident warps per SM the sets are 3 to 4 times larger (713 KB per SM and 978 KB per TPC at depth 4). Small scene: 151 / 75 / 76 / 75 KB per SM and 191 / 100 / 102 / 102 KB per TPC. So the speedup keeps rising on the large scene while the resident set falls from 500 KB to 210 KB, long before it reaches the L1 size; the L1 fit is a trend, not a threshold, and the two L1s of a TPC do not change that (the TPC set is only 1.4 to 1.7 times the SM's, not a doubling to 2x, because both SMs hold related groups).
90. **2026-11-11, Throughput_9 (RT capacity of one SM): the RT core accepts all 1,024 lanes of an SM at once; latency rises from the second warp on and grows with warps more than with lanes; at saturation about 160 lanes are in service; a one lane warp waits as long as the full warps beside it; the second SM of a TPC adds about 1.4 times on cheap queries and nothing on random ones.** One block on one SM, all warps released together by a barrier, one query per lane, idle lanes exit before the query, 30 launches (median), warps per SM 1 to 32, 1 / 8 / 16 / 32 active lanes per warp; every lane 0 result of 2,170 warp results equals OptiX (hit, primitive, t), masked lanes untouched, no fault in any run. Cheap scene (one triangle, one coherent ray): the most warps in flight at one moment equals the number launched at every point, up to 32 warps and 1,024 lanes, so no limit on queries in flight was found at the SM's full thread capacity (the queue is inside the RT core, not a refusal). Latency of a warp (cycles, unloaded = one warp alone: 1,295 / 1,320 / 1,428 / 1,649 for 1 / 8 / 16 / 32 lanes): it is above unloaded from the first added warp (x1.08, 1.10, 1.13, 1.20 at two warps) and there is no sharp knee in lanes in flight: at the same 64 lanes in flight it is x1.20 with 2 full warps, x1.38 with 4 warps of 16 lanes and x1.63 with 8 warps of 8 lanes, so warps count more than lanes. A fit over all 48 points: latency = 1,620 + 32 cycles per extra warp + 4.2 cycles per lane in flight (rms error 270 cycles). Throughput (active lanes per cycle) keeps rising to the largest launch: 0.097 for 32 full warps (0.082 at 16 warps, 0.044 at 4), 0.062 for 32 warps of 8 lanes, 0.0078 for 32 warps of 1 lane; throughput times unloaded latency (Little) gives about 160 lanes in service at 1,024 in flight for full warps (135 at 512, 96 at 256, 73 at 128), so capacity in this sense is a little above 128 and never close to 1,024: the rest of the lanes wait in the queue (loaded latency 4 times unloaded). Random rays in the 100,000 triangle scene: saturated rate about 0.010 to 0.013 lanes per cycle (10 times lower than the cheap query), Little about 140 to 180 lanes, same picture; the latency baseline is noisy there because a warp waits for its slowest lane and different warps carry different rays. Mixed warps (alternating 32 and 1 lanes, or 32 plus three of 1): a one lane warp is not served ahead of the full warps; with 32 warps at 1:1 a full warp takes 5,610 cycles and a one lane warp 5,352 (alone: 6,540 and 3,088), and at 1:3 4,382 and 4,254. So the queue looks first come first served per warp and a one lane warp pays about the full warp's latency whenever full warps are in flight; whether a warp waits until all its lanes fit was not separated from that (capacity was never exceeded in a way that refused a warp). Both SMs of one TPC (SMs 0 and 1, other blocks exit): cheap queries at 32 warps of 32 lanes, 0.0646 + 0.0697 = 0.134 per cycle against 0.097 for one SM alone (1.38 times; latency 9,452 against 6,540 cycles); with 1 lane warps the pair sums to twice one SM (no contention); random rays in the 100,000 triangle scene at saturation: pair total 0.0099 against 0.0109 for one SM alone, no gain from the second SM. (Both blocks used the same ray set, so they share cache lines.) This agrees with Throughput_8: one traversal unit per TPC, which two SMs share, and the SM side limits cheap queries to about 0.1 lanes per cycle.
91. **2026-11-11, BVH_4 dump of an OptiX launch through an instance level (IAS), read from code only: the first query of an IAS traversal takes one root pointer word instead of the instance node and GAS root words; not yet observed running.** A one triangle GAS inside an IAS with one instance (rotation about z, scale 0.9, translation) was launched with OptiX in a pipeline that allows single level instancing (memdump launch --ias; 417 of 4,096 rays hit; the IAS handle has low bits 0x4, the GAS handle 0xC), and the pipeline code below our kernel was read back (64 KB window, same method as the GAS dump; private dump in notes/). That pipeline holds only two traversal sequences (the GAS only pipeline holds four plus the main one). The longer one is the same shape as the GAS traversal (open with the dual flag, fuse count 15, then stores of the ray words 0, 0x40, 0x50 and 0x80, then 0x100, 0x180, 0x190 and 0x200, go, the same six loads) but without the two extra ray copies (0xC0 and 0xD0) of the GAS sequence. In it the stores 0x100, 0x180 and 0x190 are executed under one predicate and 0x200 under its opposite. Reading the straight line code before it, the predicate selects the 0x200 store on a fresh query: that store carries a zero word pair and one pointer, which is the IAS base plus the 64 bit offset in the IAS header at +0x20 (0x700 in our one instance IAS; the GAS header has its root offset at the same place, 0x280) with 0x10000000 added to the pointer's high word. So on that path no instance node (the 0x100 word of the GAS case) and no separate root word is needed. The 0x100, 0x180 and 0x190 stores sit with the resume path: the code after the loads builds them from words the RT core returns (shifted and masked copies of two returned words), which fits the RT core handing back an instance pointer when it reaches an instance, with the SM side then continuing; that is an inference. The second, shorter sequence (open without the dual flag, fuse count 6: one predicated store at 0, one at 0x280, go, three loads) follows a handle type test on the low six bits (type 0xD) and a load of 16 bytes at +0xB0 of the node it points to, so it looks like the instance record handling; what it does was not determined. Not settled: which of the paths the live launch takes (ncu shows only the raygen kernel's own instructions, no TTU opcodes, so the executed path cannot be counted that way), and none of this has been run with our own TTU code. The next step that would settle it is one TTU group copied from the IAS sequence run in our own kernel over the IAS from the same process, which needs Jack's go ahead and a new check of the sequence.
92. **2026-11-11, TPC_1 with the checked traversal group (one process, two streams): compute on the odd SMs leaves RT work on the even SMs untouched if it is arithmetic only, but a compute kernel that uses the L1 or the memory system costs the RT side 40 to 98 percent; the two SMs of a TPC share the node cache.** RT: the checked group in a persistent loop kernel (one RT block of 20 warps per SM, 640 threads), each lane tracing 96 rays (about 5.4 to 10.8 million rays per launch), compared against OptiX for every ray (hit, t, primitive): all 6 launch sets of 12 configurations in each of two scenes correct, no fault, no hang (stage 0 ran first for every compute placement; the placement was checked there: compute blocks 2 per SM on every SM, or 4 per SM on odd SMs only and none on even SMs). Compute: a plain kernel on a second stream of the same process (the first attempt used OptiX beside it, which Finding 87 showed does not work). Rates are against the same RT or compute work alone. RT alone: random rays in the 100,000 triangle scene 798 rays per microsecond on all 142 SMs and 780 on the 71 even SMs only (98 percent from half the SMs: one traversal unit per TPC, so the odd SMs add nothing); one coherent ray 1,313 and 1,235. Split (RT on even SMs, compute on odd SMs), RT speed against RT alone on the even SMs and compute speed against compute alone, random / coherent: arithmetic compute 0.95 and 1.02 (compute 0.89 and 0.95); compute that re-reads a 32 KB region per block (L1 resident) 0.42 and 0.44 (compute 0.61 and 0.60); compute that streams 1 GB 0.03 and 0.16 (compute 0.94 and 0.81). Shared (both on every SM): arithmetic compute 0.14 and 0.17 (compute 0.98 and 0.99, issue slots go to the compute warps, an SM side effect, not an RT core one); L1 compute 0.58 and 0.73 (compute 0.42 and 0.38); streaming compute 0.01 and 0.05 (compute 0.95 and 0.94). Node cache interference: an L1 hungry kernel on the odd SMs alone, with no work on the even SMs but the RT blocks, takes the RT blocks on the even SMs down to 0.42 to 0.44 of their speed even with a one triangle BVH, and the RT work in turn takes the L1 compute on the odd SMs down to 0.60; arithmetic compute on the sibling does neither. That is what one would expect if the RT unit of a TPC fetches nodes through both L1s of the pair (Memory_5), so a compute kernel on the sibling SM competes for the RT unit's node cache and the RT unit's fetches compete with that kernel's data. Memory heavy compute slows the RT side by queueing in the L2 and DRAM path (random rays 0.03). So placing RT work on one SM of each TPC and compute on the other is free only when the compute does not use the L1 or memory bandwidth; for a compute kernel with a real working set the sibling SM is not an independent resource. Caveats: one run per configuration (6 launches, median), the compute rate during RT launches is iterations over the host window around each launch (includes the host's synchronisation time), the L1 compute kernel spills 48 bytes of stack per thread at the forced 24 registers (extra local memory traffic), and compute alone was measured in a 300 ms window before the RT launches in the same run. [2026-11-11, queue 6 (Finding 101): about half of the L1 and memory effects on coherent rays, and part of those on random rays, are harness effects of our kernel; arithmetic, shared SM issue and the random ray L2 and DRAM numbers are not.]
93. **2026-11-11, TPC_1 repeated five times: the Finding 92 numbers hold, with small spread.** Same checked traversal group, same setup, five separate runs of every configuration (interleaved), every ray checked against OptiX each time (no wrong result, no fault). RT speed against RT alone on the even SMs, median (range), random rays / coherent: arithmetic compute on the odd SMs 0.98 (0.94 to 1.02) / 1.05 (1.02 to 1.05); L1 heavy compute 0.42 (0.42 to 0.44) / 0.44 (0.44 to 0.45); streaming compute 0.03 (0.03) / 0.16 (0.16). Both on every SM: arithmetic 0.13 / 0.18, L1 0.56 / 0.75, streaming 0.01 / 0.05. Run to run spread is 1 to 5 percent, far below the effects. One note: with arithmetic compute beside it, coherent RT runs 1.05 times faster than RT alone on the same 71 SMs (it matches the all-SM rate), so the "alone on 71 SMs" reference is about 5 percent slow in that scene; the effects below are larger than that. [Harness status: see Finding 101; the repeatability stands, the L1 numbers are partly harness.]
94. **2026-11-11, compute intensity sweep on the sibling SMs (1, 2 or 4 resident blocks): what slows traversal is memory system traffic, not how much arithmetic or how many warps.** RT on the even SMs, compute on the odd SMs, five runs each, RT speed against RT alone, random rays / coherent. Arithmetic compute: 0.99, 0.98, 1.01 / 1.05, 1.03, 1.05 for 1, 2, 4 blocks (no effect at any intensity; the compute side runs at 0.80 to 0.99 of its own alone speed). Shared memory only compute: 0.91, 0.90, 0.89 / 1.04 (small effect on random rays). Compute reading a 32 KB region per block from L1 (no spills): 1.01, 1.01, 0.75 / 1.04, 1.04, 0.80 (nothing at 1 or 2 blocks, a quarter lost at 4). Compute reading a 32 MB region (L2 resident): 0.29 at every block count / 0.77 at every block count. Compute streaming a 1 GB buffer: 0.03 at every block count / 0.16. So one block per SM of L2 or DRAM traffic already costs the full amount: the L2 and DRAM path is saturated by a single block, and the RT unit's node fetches, which go through the same path, queue behind it. Random rays suffer much more than coherent ones because their nodes come from L2 (coherent ones mostly do not). [Harness status: see Finding 101; arithmetic, random ray L2 and DRAM rows are not harness effects; the coherent L1, L2 and DRAM rows are partly harness.]
95. **2026-11-11, node cache separation (option b): the L1 effect is mainly traffic through the sibling's L1, not capacity, and for coherent rays the RT unit does not use the L1 at all.** (1) Carveout and working set on the sibling compute kernel (4 blocks): the RT slowdown does not follow the compute's working set size. 8, 16, 32, 64 and 128 KB per block give 0.70, 0.71, 0.75, 0.69, 0.61 of RT speed on random rays, and 0.63, 0.63, 0.80, 0.81, 0.79 on coherent rays; the smallest windows (32 KB total on the SM, which fit any L1) hurt coherent rays most, and there the compute itself runs fastest (more loads per second). Two blocks of a 32 KB window (64 KB total, no cache pressure) cost nothing; four blocks cost a quarter. So the cost follows how many L1 requests per second the sibling issues, a bandwidth effect, more than how much of the cache it uses. Shrinking the sibling's L1 (carveout to shared memory, 8 KB reserved per block) with the 32 KB, 4 block compute kernel that spills to local memory: random 0.42 to 0.51, coherent 0.42 to 0.61 (less L1 use, and traffic goes to L2 instead); with the 128 KB window kernel: random 0.64 to 0.47, coherent 0.80 to 0.64 (worse with a smaller L1, which is capacity). The carveout change alone with arithmetic compute costs nothing (0.94 / 1.05). The earlier L1 compute kernel (spilling 48 bytes of stack per thread, which doubles its L1 traffic) is much harsher (0.42) than the non spilling window kernel (0.75 to 0.80) with the same 32 KB window, which supports the request rate reading. So the capacity part is real but secondary (about 0.1 to 0.2 of RT speed); the rest is request traffic. (2) ncu on RT alone (it serializes kernels, so it cannot be run beside compute; sector counts summed over all L1s, RT = all L1 sectors minus load/store ones): random rays in the 100,000 triangle scene: 82 L1 sectors per ray from the RT unit, hit rate 0.31 with RT on 71 SMs, 0.28 on all 142 SMs, 0.19 with 32 KB of shared memory reserved on the RT SMs (their L1 shrunk; the odd SMs' L1s unchanged). So the RT unit's node fetches do use the L1 and its hit rate falls when the L1 shrinks. One coherent ray (one triangle BVH): zero L1 sectors from the RT unit per ray, yet L1 heavy compute on the sibling still cuts RT speed to 0.44. So for coherent rays the slowdown is not node cache sharing at all: it is something else the siblings share (the RT unit's interface to the SMs, or L1 bandwidth for the ray and result traffic of the RT blocks themselves). This revises the reading in Finding 92: the node cache is shared (Memory_5), but node cache interference is not the whole story, and for coherent rays it is not the cause. [Harness status: see Finding 101; the coherent rays reading (L1 effect without any RT L1 sector) is explained mostly by our kernel's own loads and stores, so the capacity and request rate analysis of the random ray rows is the part that stands.]
96. **2026-11-11, bounce rays on a scene far larger than the L2: the oracle gain does not grow.** OptiX, closest hit, 11.2 to 11.3 million bounce rays (origin at the camera hit point, random direction), soup scenes of 8 million and 16 million triangles whose acceleration structures are about 0.52 GB (5.4 times the 96 MB L2) and 1.04 GB (10.8 times); earlier 3 million (186 MB, 1.9 times). Speedup over pixel order, trace time only (10 launches, median, every result equal for every ray): origin Morton (4 / 5 bits) 1.14 / 1.17 at 8M and 1.12 / 1.16 at 16M (3M: 1.15 to 1.16); oracle grouping by the ray's own hit subtree, depth 1 / 2 / 3 / full: 1.05 / 1.12 / 1.21 / 1.28 at 8M, 1.01 / 1.09 / 1.18 / 1.28 at 16M (3M: 1.04 / 1.16 / 1.25 / 1.27). So the best possible grouping gains 1.28 times over pixel order at every scene size; it does not grow with the scene. What does grow is the gap to a shuffled launch: pixel order is 2.2, 2.5 times (8M, 16M) faster than shuffled (2.1 at 3M, 2.3 here), and the oracle 2.8 and 3.2 times. Net of costs (GPU key generation and sort, ray gather, result scatter, as in Finding 83): the best Morton setting (4 bits per axis) takes 5.4 ms at 8M and 5.7 ms at 16M against 2.5 ms for pixel order, so 0.46 / 0.45 of pixel order speed (the trace itself is 2.2 to 2.4 ms, a gain of 0.3 ms that the 3 ms of sort work swamps), and 1.01 / 1.26 of a shuffled launch (only the 16M scene gains over a shuffled launch). Conclusion: for bounce rays a treelet or sorting scheme has at most about 1.3 times to win over pixel order at any size we tried, and must cost under about 0.5 ms for 11 million rays to pay; its main value is against incoherent (shuffled) launches, and that value grows with the scene.
97. **2026-11-11, coherent interference test: the slowdown comes from the sibling's L1 request traffic acting on our own kernel's memory instructions, not from capacity or the RT core, and it nearly disappears when the RT kernel's loads are L1 allocating.** The default traversal kernel contains no L1 allocating load (its ray and constant loads and its result stores bypass the L1), contrary to the earlier suggestion that this variant needed building; the lean build is the L1 allocating one (some ray loads allocate in L1, and it stores 4 result words instead of 14). Three builds of the same checked traversal group (base, hoist = constants loaded once, lean) ran on the even SMs beside a compute kernel on the odd SMs (4 blocks), 3 repetitions, every ray equal to OptiX. Coherent rays, RT speed against the same build alone (alone: 1,235 / 1,549 / 1,926 rays per microsecond for base / hoist / lean): arithmetic compute 1.02 / 1.00 / 1.05; shared memory only compute 1.05 / 0.97 / 0.91; compute re-reading a 1 KB window per block from L1 (4 KB on the SM, no capacity pressure at all) 0.63 / 0.50 / 1.05; 32 KB window 0.79 / 0.62 / 0.91; the original spilling L1 kernel 0.44 / 0.35 / 0.88. Random rays (alone 775 / 1,386 / 1,071): arithmetic 1.00 / 0.92 / 1.01; shared memory only 0.91 / 0.69 / 0.90; 1 KB window 0.72 / 0.41 / 1.02; 32 KB 0.75 / 0.44 / 0.69; spilling L1 0.42 / 0.24 / 0.53. Spread between repetitions is 1 to 5 percent. Reading: a sibling that only hits its own L1 (1 KB window) still halves a build whose memory instructions bypass the L1, so cache capacity is not the shared resource, and neither are issue slots (arithmetic compute: no effect) or the shared memory pipe (shared memory only compute: no effect on coherent rays). The faster the RT kernel (hoist), the larger the loss. The lean build is immune for coherent rays and with the small window on random rays. Caveat: lean differs from base in two ways (L1 allocating loads and 4 stores instead of 14), which were not separated, so which of the two matters is open; the candidate is a path for L1 bypassing requests that the two SMs of a TPC share. Consequence for Finding 92: part of the "RT core slowed by a sibling" effect is a property of our harness kernel's own memory instructions, so it must not be read as an RT core property without the lean control.
98. **2026-11-11, an LRU model of a 60 KB node cache fed with replay reuse distances gets the order and saturation of the grouping speedups right but not their size, and no single cache size or constant fits both scenes.** CPU replay of 8.4 million random rays over the decoded BVH, stream of one SM (13 resident warps served round robin by traversal step, SM s holds warps s + 142 j of the grouped launch order, two windows, 142 SMs), reuse distance histograms for random order, oracle grouping depth 1 to 4 and the full key, on the 100,000 and 3 million triangle scenes. Miss rate of a fully associative LRU of 60 KB (480 lines of 128 bytes), nodes only, lanes of a warp asking the same node in one step counted once: 100,000 triangles 0.76 (random), 0.41, 0.13, 0.13, 0.14, 0.14; 3 million: 0.94, 0.87, 0.70, 0.59, 0.52, 0.47. Predicting the speedup as the ratio of miss rates (no fitted constant) gives, against the measured 1.37, 2.00, 2.13, 2.16, 2.17 (small scene) a prediction of 1.8, 5.9, 5.7, 5.5, 5.5, far too high, because the measured speedup saturates near 2.2 while the miss rate keeps falling, and for the large scene 1.08, 1.35, 1.60, 1.81, 2.00 against 1.13, 1.44, 1.85, 2.22, 2.31 (0.82 to 0.95 of measured; 1.01 to 1.16 when identical requests of a warp are not merged). A fraction of the time that does not depend on misses (one fitted constant) fits one scene well (rms log error 0.03) but is 0.36 to 0.39 on the small scene and about 0 to 0.07 on the large one, and applied to the other scene gives errors of 0.2 to 0.8 (log). Sweeping the cache size, the miss ratio prediction is closest to the measurements at 15 to 30 KB (rms log error 0.29 to 0.34 over both scenes) and worse at 60 KB (0.58) and 120 KB; adding the leaf slots as one line each (a stand in for triangle blocks) fits the small scene at 60 KB within 0.90 to 1.0 of measured but underpredicts the large one (0.59 to 0.92). Reading: the working set story of Findings 86 and 89 is supported in direction (miss rate falls with depth, faster on the large scene, which is also still falling at the full key), but a node cache miss count is not a sufficient model of the speed: the small scene is limited by something else once the miss rate is low (random rays gain 2.2 times, then the rate is flat), and the cache size and line model (the hardware cache, its node and triangle line layout, whether the TPC shares it) are not known well enough to calibrate. Caveats: cold (first touch in the window) accesses count as misses, 20 to 70 percent of accesses at random order on the large scene; the window length and warm up were not varied; the replay agrees with OptiX's closest hit on 97 percent of rays; timing equal weight steps stand in for time.
99. **2026-11-11, lean split: neither of lean's two changes alone removes the sibling effect on coherent rays; each removes about two thirds of it, together they remove all of it. For random rays with a 32 KB sibling window nothing removes it.** The lean build of Finding 97 changed two things at once: its ray loads allocate in the L1 and it stores 4 result words instead of 14. Two more builds of the same checked traversal group separate them: loads only (L1 allocating ray loads, 14 stores) and stores only (default loads, 4 stores). All four builds ran on the even SMs beside a compute kernel on the odd SMs (4 blocks), 3 repetitions, every ray equal to OptiX (144 runs, none wrong, none unstored). RT speed against the same build alone, coherent rays (one triangle), median: sibling that only hits its own 1 KB window per block: default 0.62, loads only 0.91, stores only 0.90, both (lean) 1.05; 32 KB window: 0.78, 0.89, 0.87, 0.91; the original spilling L1 kernel: 0.43, 0.66, 0.57, 0.88. Random rays in the 100,000 triangle scene: 1 KB window 0.72, 0.91, 0.89, 1.02; 32 KB window 0.74, 0.69, 0.72, 0.70 (no build is immune); spilling L1 kernel 0.42, 0.47, 0.48, 0.54. Arithmetic sibling: 0.95 to 1.05 for every build and scene. Shared memory only sibling: 1.02 / 0.93 / 1.04 / 0.90 (coherent), 0.91 for every build (random). RT rate alone on 71 SMs (coherent / random, rays per microsecond): default 1,235 / 780, loads only 1,535 / 950, stores only 1,423 / 844, both 1,924 / 1,063. Reading: both the number of L1 bypassing loads and the number of stores of our own kernel make it sensitive to the L1 request traffic of the sibling SM, and the two add up; removing either leaves a third of the loss, removing both leaves none for coherent rays with a small window. So the coherent rays loss of Findings 92 to 95 is a property of our harness kernel's memory instructions. The random ray loss with a 32 KB window (0.70 in every build) and with the spilling kernel (0.47 to 0.54) is not removed by lean, so something real remains there (capacity of the shared L1 or the node cache, or L1 and L2 bandwidth); it is smaller than the 0.42 of Finding 92. Caveats: 3 repetitions (spread 1 to 4 percent); the builds differ in speed alone, so "against the same build alone" compares different baselines; the stores only and loads only builds were not given a version that also hoists the constants.
100. **2026-11-11, the performance model with its miss inputs taken from the replay reuse distance histograms: closer than the LRU only miss ratio over both scenes without any new fit (rms log error 0.36 against 0.58), but it never predicts more than 1.4 times where 2.2 to 2.3 times is measured.** The inputs of the performance model that depend on the ray order (L1 sectors per ray, L1 fill sectors per ray, DRAM bytes per ray) were replaced by values from the CPU replay histograms of Finding 98 (one SM stream, 60 KB LRU, nodes only, identical requests of a warp merged): lines requested per ray and misses per ray from the histogram, converted to sectors with one constant (7.5 sectors per line request, chosen so that random rays in the 100,000 triangle scene give the 82 L1 sectors per ray that ncu measured, Finding 95), DRAM bytes only from requests that leave the SM's histogram horizon times the share of the acceleration structure that does not fit the L2 (zero for the small scene), queries per ray and 13 resident warps as before, and the fitted coefficients of the model unchanged (fit on one SM Memory_4 data of our own kernel). Predicted speedup over random order by key (random, depth 1, 2, 3, 4, full), small scene: 1.00, 1.10, 1.26, 1.33, 1.37, 1.37 against measured 1.00, 1.37, 2.00, 2.13, 2.16, 2.17; large scene: 1.00, 1.09, 1.20, 1.28, 1.35, 1.40 against 1.00, 1.13, 1.44, 1.85, 2.22, 2.31. Root mean square log error: the performance model 0.39 (small), 0.33 (large), 0.36 over both; LRU only miss rate ratio (Finding 98) 0.81, 0.12, 0.58; LRU only ratio of misses per ray 1.05, 0.07, 0.74. So v1 is wrong by a similar factor on both scenes (it flattens the gain), while the LRU ratio is nearly right on the large scene and wildly high on the small one (5.5 times against 2.2). Fitted variants: the LRU ratio with one fitted constant (the share of time that does not depend on misses) gives 0.25 when fitted on the small scene and applied to the large one and 0.74 the other way; the LRU ratio with its cache capacity fitted over both scenes reaches 0.24 in sample (a 7 KB cache for the per ray version); refitting the three miss coefficients of the v1 form on one scene and applying them to the other gives 0.17 (small to large, with the scaled variant 0.21) and 0.62 (large to small), 0.45 over both. Reading: with reuse distance inputs the model keeps the right order of the keys, the right saturation on the small scene and the right growth with depth on the large one, which the miss ratio alone does not get on the small scene; it understates the size because its fitted terms are dominated by traffic and queueing terms that do not change with grouping (one fill sector costs it 5.1 cycles divided by the 13 warps, so removing 53 of 62 fill sectors saves about 20 cycles of roughly 170 per ray), and these were fitted on one SM of our own kernel, where grouping gains only 8 to 37 percent. It is a better stated model than the pure LRU ratio but not a better predictor than the LRU ratio on the large scene. Caveats: the conversion constant comes from one ncu number; the DRAM input is a crude stand in; the histograms are from the replay, not the hardware; only 12 points (two scenes, six keys) so the fitted variants have almost no degrees of freedom.
101. **2026-11-11, F92 to F94 redone with the lean build (5 runs each, 250 runs, none wrong, none unstored): which numbers were harness effects.** RT speed against the same build alone (split: RT on the 71 even SMs, compute on the odd SMs; shared: both on every SM), random rays in the 100,000 triangle scene / coherent rays (default build in Findings 93 and 94 in brackets). Split: arithmetic compute 1.00 / 1.05 (0.98 / 1.05); original spilling L1 compute 0.55 / 0.88 (0.42 / 0.44); compute that streams 1 GB 0.02 / 0.35 (0.03 / 0.16). Shared: arithmetic 0.09 / 0.11 (0.13 / 0.18); spilling L1 0.60 / 0.73 (0.56 / 0.75); streaming 0.01 / 0.04 (0.01 / 0.05). Intensity sweep (1, 2, 4 blocks per sibling SM): arithmetic 1.05, 1.01, 1.01 / 1.04, 1.03, 1.05 (1.01 / 1.05 before); shared memory only 0.89, 0.90, 0.87 / 0.90, 0.90, 0.90 (0.91 / 1.04); 32 KB window from L1 1.01, 0.97, 0.70 / 1.04, 1.04, 0.91 (1.01, 1.01, 0.75 / 1.04, 1.04, 0.80); 32 MB window (L2 resident) 0.22, 0.23, 0.23 / 0.89, 0.89, 0.90 (0.29 / 0.77); 1 GB streaming 0.02 at every count / 0.36, 0.36, 0.35 (0.03 / 0.16). Compute speed is unchanged by the build. Harness effects (they shrink when our kernel stops using many bypassing loads and stores): the coherent ray losses from L1 compute (0.44 to 0.88 for the spilling kernel, 0.80 to 0.91 for four blocks of the 32 KB window), from L2 resident compute (0.77 to 0.89) and about half of the one from DRAM streaming compute (0.16 to 0.35); and on random rays the 1 KB window loss of Finding 97 (0.72 to 1.02) and a quarter of the spilling kernel loss (0.42 to 0.55). Not harness effects (equal or worse with the lean build): arithmetic compute on the sibling costs nothing; arithmetic compute on the same SM costs RT 0.09 to 0.11 (issue slots); on random rays the 4 block 32 KB window loss (0.70), L2 resident compute (0.22, a little worse than 0.29), DRAM streaming (0.02), the shared L1 loss (0.60); on coherent rays DRAM streaming still leaves 0.35 and the shared memory only sibling 0.90 (also seen in Finding 97 for the lean build), which are probably queueing in the memory path and the SM to RT unit interface rather than anything of ours. What remains of the shared node cache story: random rays lose 30 to 45 percent to an L1 using sibling (0.55 to 0.70), which is a real effect of the sibling SM, consistent with a shared L1 or node cache or the L1 request path; coherent rays lose almost nothing to a sibling that stays in its L1 (0.91 to 1.05 with the lean build). The headline of Finding 92 (L1 heavy sibling cuts RT speed to 0.42 to 0.44) was a property of the default harness kernel; for the lean kernel it is 0.55 on random and 0.88 on coherent rays. Caveats: the lean kernel is faster alone (1,064 / 1,934 rays per microsecond on 71 SMs, against 780 / 1,235), so it is also more exposed to anything that adds a fixed latency; the lean variant stores only 3 result words, which are all the check needs; one scene per ray type.
102. **2026-11-11, real scenes (Bistro, San Miguel, Power Plant, Lucy), path tracing rays: grouping gains are small, 1.0 to 1.1 times in most views and up to 1.6 times for deep bounces in two views; the best Morton sort never pays net of its cost (0.2 to 0.5 of pixel order speed); the traversal share of a frame is about half with a minimal shader.** Scenes (OBJ or PLY, downloaded to /dev/shm only, never to git; licenses: Bistro CC BY 4.0, San Miguel CC BY 3.0, Power Plant UNC non commercial research use, Lucy Stanford research use with credit): Bistro exterior and interior merged 3.85 million triangles, San Miguel 2.0 9.96 million, Power Plant 12.76 million, Lucy 28.06 million plus a two triangle ground plane (so that bounce rays can hit it). OptiX acceleration structure buffer (default build flags, no compaction, as in earlier findings) against the 96 MB L2: Bistro 239 MB (2.5 times), San Miguel 619 MB (6.4 times), Power Plant 792 MB (8.3 times), Lucy 1,742 MB (18 times). Rays: 4096 by 2048 camera rays in pixel order (depth 1), then cosine weighted bounces from the real closest hits (geometric normal, offset origin), misses end the path (depth 2 is the first bounce); two automatic views per scene (random probe views, the 70th and 95th percentile of the median hit distance among views with at least 80 percent hits) and one fixed view of Lucy (statue on its plane, sky around it). Every order's result equals the pixel order result for every ray (34 scene, view and depth cases of 8 orders each, 0 wrong). Trace time speedup over pixel order, closest hit, 10 launches median, depth 1 / 2 / 3 / 4: Morton of the origin with 6 bits per axis, close view: Bistro 1.00 / 1.01 / 1.10 / 1.16, San Miguel 1.05 / 0.99 / 1.01 / 1.03, Power Plant 1.00 / 1.03 / 1.19 / 1.24, Lucy 0.98 / 0.89 / 0.92 / 0.85; wide view: Bistro 1.00 / 1.04 / 1.39 / 1.60, San Miguel 1.03 / 1.00 / 1.01 / 0.98, Power Plant 1.01 / 1.04 / 1.18 / 1.26. Morton of the origin (4 bits) plus the direction (2 bits per axis) is a little better on bounces (Bistro wide view 1.19 / 1.46 / 1.63, Power Plant 1.16 / 1.21 / 1.29) and about equal to the oracle grouping by hit subtree (full key): Bistro close 0.99 / 1.05 / 1.11 / 1.10, wide 0.97 / 1.30 / 1.46 / 1.63; San Miguel close 1.00 / 0.98 / 1.05 / 1.06, wide 1.00 / 1.03 / 1.04 / 1.06; Power Plant close 0.93 / 1.25 / 1.29 / 1.31, wide 0.91 / 1.25 / 1.24 / 1.28; Lucy 0.97 / 0.91 / 1.01 / 1.01. Depth 1 gains nothing anywhere, and the fifth depth continues the trend. A shuffled launch is slower than pixel order by 1.0 to 1.7 times (depth 1: 1.09 to 1.72; bounces 1.0 to 1.5). Net of costs (GPU key generation, sort, gather, scatter, as in Finding 83): the best Morton variant runs at 0.18 to 0.56 of pixel order speed at every depth of every scene, so over a whole frame of 4 to 5 depths no gain is left (1.00 with a pixel order fallback), while the trace time summed over depths with the best Morton per depth is 1.16 / 1.37 times faster for Bistro (close / wide), 1.08 / 1.07 for San Miguel, 1.17 / 1.16 for Power Plant and 0.95 (worse) for Lucy; the oracle bound gives 1.07 / 1.39, 1.02 / 1.03, 1.18 / 1.18, 0.95. Important limit of this measurement: a launch of 8.4 million rays takes only 0.67 to 0.91 ms (9 to 12 billion rays per second), because the ray records (32 bytes read) and results (32 bytes written) need about 0.69 ms of memory traffic on their own: the same launch with every ray's tmax set to 0 (no traversal work) takes 0.99 to 1.03 times the real trace time at depth 1 in the close views (the launch is bound by ray and result traffic, traversal is hidden behind it, so no grouping can help) and 0.4 to 0.8 times at depths 2 to 5 (fewer rays, more traversal per ray). Grouping therefore matters only when the traversal work per ray is large enough to show above that traffic, which happens for bounces in scenes with long rays. Traversal fraction of a frame (closest hit plus shadow rays to a fixed light direction, over closest hit plus shadow plus shading), with a minimal shading kernel (one triangle fetch, normal, cosine direction, the next ray and shadow ray records and an accumulator write, 1.4 ms for 8.4 million hits) summed over depths 1 to 5: Bistro 0.52 close / 0.63 wide, San Miguel 0.48 / 0.47, Power Plant 0.56 / 0.55, Lucy 0.54 (depths 1 to 4); with a shading kernel 3 times as expensive 0.23 to 0.36, with 10 times 0.08 to 0.15; the closest hit rays are 57 to 68 percent of the traversal time (the rest are shadow rays); the traversal share rises with depth as rays and hits thin out (Bistro wide view 0.51 at depth 1 to 0.70 at depth 5). Reading: on real scenes with these views, sorting does not pay for the bounce rays of a wavefront on this GPU (for 8.4 million rays key generation, sort, gather and scatter together cost about 2.2 ms, the radix sort alone 0.4 ms, against a trace of 0.4 to 1.4 ms), the potential (oracle) gain is 1.0 to 1.1 times for the compact scenes and up to 1.3 to 1.6 times where bounce rays travel far (Power Plant, Bistro wide view), smaller than the 2.2 times of the random ray soup tests (Finding 80), and the RT core's share of a frame is about half with a light shader and a fraction with a realistic one. Caveats: one frame per scene and view, 1 sample per pixel; views are chosen by a heuristic, not by artists; no materials, textures or alpha tests (geometry only), so shading cost is a stand in with a sensitivity, not a measurement; trace times include ray and result traffic; the oracle key needs the answer and is a bound; the Lucy ground plane and fixed view were our choice; BVH sizes are uncompacted buffer sizes.
103. **2026-11-13, 4070 Ti (a second chip, 60 SMs, 30 TPCs, 48 MB L2, same driver): the harness runs unchanged apart from a per GPU configuration, and OptiX's traversal code on this driver is the same as on the RTX 6000 Ada.** One detected configuration per GPU (SM count, L2, driver, OptiX library, block placement) replaced the constants of the RTX 6000 Ada (142 SMs, 71 TPCs, GPU 1) in the host program, the run gate, the memory reader and the pointer chase; a plain CUDA probe checks the block to SM placement rule (block b on SM 2b for the first half of the SMs, then the odd ones) before anything is run, and it holds on the 4070 Ti. Reading the live OptiX pipeline code on the 4070 Ti showed the 19 instruction traversal group, the predicate setup and the code before it byte for byte equal to the RTX 6000 Ada copy, so the already checked group ran as is. The first traversal on this chip (one warp, one triangle) returned the same hit and result words as before. Many bench scripts and the model still carry the old constants (listed in the log).
104. **2026-11-13, 4070 Ti: the performance model predictions written beforehand (committed before any measurement) were right for everything that happens inside one SM or one TPC, and wrong where the L2 size mattered.** Matched within 10%: pointer chase L1 41, L2 285 and DRAM about 655 cycles (the L2 hit latency on 60 SMs and 48 MB is the same 285 cycles as on 142 SMs and 96 MB); latency of one traversal 1,612 cycles warm and 2,593 cold (against 1,641 and 2,632), 782, 827 and 867 cycles for a second trace at one, two and three levels (exactly as before), warm and cold depth sweeps within 4%; one SM throughput at 1 to 20 warps within 3% (saturated steady state 6 to 10% faster than before); two sibling SMs 1.03 to 1.05 times one SM with long queries and two SMs in different TPCs 1.99 to 2.01 times; whole chip with long coherent queries 29.8 times one SM at 30 SMs (one per TPC) and 31.1 at 60 (the second SM of a TPC adds 4%, as on 71 and 142); the node cache keeps every node up to about 60 KB and loses half near 100 to 115 KB; shared memory reservation shrinks L1 from about 90 KB to about 20 KB; the sibling's L1 serves a trace at the same 782, 827 and 867 cycles and another TPC at 1,569, 1,863 and 2,169 (5 to 9% below the old values). Missed: a cyclic pointer chase over 64 MB was predicted at 350 to 600 cycles and measured 658 (DRAM level): a cyclic walk bigger than an LRU like L2 gets almost no hits, and v1 has no capacity term for that (40 MB still hits at 286, 48 MB gives 424, 56 MB and up 658). The size of the slowdown from shared memory use (Memory_1) could not be compared: it depends on the ray pool, and ours gave 1.4 to 3.2 times against 1.9 to 2.5 before. Random ray plateaus (L2 and per GPC ceilings) were not predicted and not measured here.
105. **2026-11-13, 4070 Ti: short coherent queries let both SMs of a TPC add 38%, long ones only 4%.** One triangle queries (about 1,600 cycles) with 32 warps per SM scale to 30.0 times one SM at 30 SMs and 41.3 times at 60 SMs, while 64 stacked triangle queries scale to 29.8 and 31.1. A single SM on the short queries gives about 0.10 rays per cycle and a TPC pair about 0.14, so one SM does not saturate the shared traversal unit there; the traversal unit's capacity per TPC is about 0.14 rays per cycle on short queries. This agrees with the earlier sibling result on short queries (1.25 to 1.4 times, Finding 32) and says that the 4 percent of Finding 62 belongs to queries long enough to saturate the unit.
106. **2026-11-13, the performance model gets an L2 capacity term: for a cyclic access pattern the L2 hit rate falls from 1 to 0 over a narrow band around the L2 size, and with it the 64 MB chase miss of Finding 104 is predicted.** The term (cyclic access: hit rate 1 up to the L2 size times (1 minus d), 0 from the L2 size times (1 plus d), linear between; random access: L2 size over working set) is fitted on the RTX 6000 Ada's chase data, which hits at 1 to 64 MB (292 to 298 cycles) and reaches DRAM at 512 MB (652): that only bounds the band, d between 0 and 1/3, so d = 1/6 is used. Checked on the 4070 Ti (48 MB L2) without refitting: 24, 32 and 40 MB predicted 285 (measured 284 to 286), 56, 64, 96 and 128 MB predicted 658 (measured 658 to 661), and the L2 sized point at 48 MB predicted 471 against 424 measured (11% high; measured hit rate 0.63). The 4070 Ti only bounds d to at most 1/6, and the single partial point does not fix it, so the ramp's width and shape are not identified: points between 40 and 56 MB are missing. The formula for random access is a lower bound on the slowdown and does not describe a cyclic walk. A real ray stream is neither: its hit rate comes from the reuse distance histogram (Finding 100); the capacity term is the bound for cyclic access (code: model/l2_capacity.py, table in model/results_4070ti.md).
107. **2026-11-13, 4070 Ti (48 MB L2): bounce rays with oracle grouping, the gap over a plain Morton sort does not grow with the smaller L2 (prediction written first, committed before the run).** OptiX, closest hit, 10 to 11 million bounce rays on the soup scenes of Findings 88 and 96 (100,000 / 3 million / 8 million / 16 million triangles, acceleration structures 0.14 / 3.9 / 10.8 / 21.6 times the L2 here, against 0.07 / 1.9 / 5.4 / 10.8 times at 96 MB), every launch order giving the pixel order result for every ray. Speedup over pixel order, trace only: oracle grouping (by the ray's own hit subtree, full key) 1.26 / 1.30 / 1.29 / 1.31 (RTX 6000 Ada: 1.27 / 1.27 / 1.28 / 1.28), origin Morton 1.09 to 1.19, so the oracle is 1.15 / 1.13 / 1.11 / 1.10 times better than the Morton sort (6000 Ada 1.14 / 1.09 / 1.09 / 1.10): the gap over Morton does not grow with the smaller L2, nor with scene size. What differs on this chip: the shuffled launch is penalized less near the L2 size (pixel order 1.57 and 1.91 times faster than shuffled at 100,000 and 3 million triangles, 2.17 and 2.32 at 8 and 16 million; the prediction said 2.0 to 3.0), the trace takes 2.1 to 2.4 times longer (60 against 142 SMs), and net of costs (GPU key generation, sort, gather and scatter, about 1.5 to 1.9 times slower than on the 6000 Ada) the best Morton sort delivers 0.41 / 0.50 / 0.51 / 0.53 of pixel order speed (6000 Ada 0.45 to 0.46): still never worth it for bounce rays, and a bit less bad on the big scenes. One chip and one ray set per scene, differences under about 0.04 are inside launch to launch noise (one 16 million Morton setting read 1.07 against 1.20 in the net run). The real scenes of Finding 102 were not rerun (files are not on this machine). Details: model/results_bounce_4070ti.md.
108. **2026-11-13, 4070 Ti, first energy test: NVML's total energy counter works (100 ms updates), but the energy of a query is below what our harness can resolve.** The counter agrees with the power draw (92 to 125 W under load, 7 W idle on the second card) and updates every 100 ms. Per query energy was measured as the difference between the traversal build and the same build without TTU instructions, with one block on each of 60 SMs, 1 or 16 warps, back to back launches for 3 seconds each, clocks fixed at 2865 to 2880 MHz after a warm up run, three repetitions, depth sweep 1 / 144 / 2,500 / 20,736 triangles, from L1 (second query in a kernel), L2 (warm, first query) and DRAM (after a 96 MB read flush). The harness without traversal already takes 97 to 98% of a launch's energy (about 0.2 microjoules per query above idle), a traversal query adds 0.5 to 3% (2 to 24 nanojoules, as large as the 1 to 2.7% run to run spread, which the 100 ms counter granularity on a 3 second window explains), no dependence on depth or on the memory level can be claimed, and the one visible number (about 0.02 microjoules for an L2 sourced query at 1 warp, the same at every depth) equals the card's power times the extra latency of the traversal, so it is waiting cost, not node fetch cost. A usable energy test needs a persistent kernel where the RT work dominates, windows of 10 seconds or more, and power above idle over query rate (not done). Details: model/results_energy_4070ti.md.
109. **2026-11-13, 4070 Ti: energy per bounce ray follows time per ray; grouping rays saves about as much energy as it saves time, and sorting on the GPU costs more energy than it saves.** OptiX closest hit, 11 million bounce rays on the 8 and 16 million triangle soups, NVML total energy over 10 second windows of back to back short launches (idle window before and after each, 3 repetitions in rotated order, SM clock 2826 to 2865 MHz, every result equal to the pixel order result, spread under 1.1 percent). Pixel order costs 121 nanojoules per ray at 8 million triangles (127 at 16 million), 8.3 and 7.9 million rays per joule, at 280 W. Trace only, a Morton sort of the origin is 1.16 times faster and uses 1.21 times less energy per ray (the card draws 4 percent less power, 269 against 280 W, with fewer memory misses); the oracle grouping is 1.28 to 1.29 times faster and uses 1.29 to 1.30 times less energy, at the same power as pixel order. Net of the sort (key generation, radix sort, gather, scatter) the Morton pipeline runs at 0.47 of pixel order speed and 0.65 of its energy efficiency, because the sort kernels draw about 204 W against 280 W for traversal, so energy loses less than time but still loses: sorting bounce rays does not pay in energy either. Subtracting in process idle power (32 to 35 W) changes the ratios by 0.01 to 0.03. So the answer to whether energy per ray improves more than time is: slightly for the Morton trace (21 against 16 percent), no for the oracle bound (equal), and for the whole sort pipeline it falls by less than time does. Caveats: whole card energy, one chip, soups only (the real scenes are not on this machine), the card runs near its power limit and clocks differ by up to 1.4 percent between configurations. Details: model/results_energy_bounce_4070ti.md.
110. **2026-11-13, 4070 Ti (48 MB L2): the L2 capacity ramp of a cyclic walk is sharp and starts at the L2 size, not symmetric around it: full hits at 44 MB, 63 percent at 48 MB, none at 52 MB.** Pointer chase with L2 caching, 3 repetitions each, repeatable to 0.3 percent: 40 MB 286 cycles, 44 MB 287 (hit rate 0.995), 48 MB 424 (0.63), 52 MB 656 (0), 56 MB 656 (DRAM level 656). The symmetric ramp of Finding 106 (width fixed at one sixth of the L2 size, chosen from triton's bounds) is wrong at both new points (it predicts 378 cycles at 44 MB and 565 at 52 MB), and no straight ramp that is 1 at 44, 0.63 at 48 and 0 at 52 exists, so the walk loses its hits between about 45 and 52 MB: a straight ramp starts between 45.4 and 47.9 MB and ends between 48.5 and 52 MB. The model now uses hit rate 1 up to 0.97 times the L2 size and 0 from 1.05 times (46.5 to 50.5 MB here); it is fitted on these points, so they are no longer a check, and its shape between 44 and 48 MB is an assumption (no point at 46 or 50 MB). Whether the band scales with the L2 size (93 to 101 MB on the RTX 6000 Ada) is untested: triton has no chase point between 64 and 512 MB. Code: model/l2_capacity.py, table in model/results_4070ti.md.
111. **2026-11-15, 4070 Ti, BVH format: the byte after each child box in an instance acceleration structure is the instance's 8 bit visibility mask; leaves advance by at most 3 blocks for triangles but reach the 3 bit field maximum of 7 for custom primitives.** Built in our own process and copied out with a plain memory copy. An IAS of 30 instances with random masks holds exactly those 30 masks, one per child slot; three instances with masks 0x0F, 0xF0 and 0x01 give slots 0, 1 and 2 those values (the mask is also stored in a table next to the header and, shifted by one bit, in the instance record). Triangle slots carry 0xFF in that byte whatever the geometry flags and custom primitive slots carry 0 (no per geometry mask). The slot type byte is more than a leaf flag: for triangles the top bit means an any hit program may run (the disable any hit geometry flag clears it), custom primitives and instances use a different pattern (bit 6 set), and the low 3 bits are the number of 128 byte blocks the leaf advances: 0 to 2 in soups, grids and clustered triangles, 3 at most in a scene of large thin triangles, and 0 to 7 for custom primitives, where the field maximum is reached in 207 of 80,610 slots at 100,000 boxes. A node has at most 12 slots and up to 13 blocks of triangles (35 or more of custom primitives). The header's triangle counter is exactly 1.15 times the triangle count in three of four large scenes (1.142 at 1,000), which fits a 15 percent spatial split budget (duplicated references; not checked on hardware, and it contradicts the early no duplicates reading), and one 128 byte block holds on average 3.4 to 3.8 references. Not decoded: the middle bits of the triangle type byte. Details: notes/bvh_format.md (private), ttu/bench/BVH_6.
112. **2026-11-15, 4070 Ti, payload and attribute size (Throughput_10): payload values cost registers, not spills; the cost appears as a step in occupancy when a thread passes 128 registers.** OptiX closest hit, 1 million rays on a 100,000 triangle soup, payload values 0 to 32, attribute values 0 to 8, triangles and custom primitives, random and coherent rays. Each payload value adds about one register (103 registers at 0 values, 122 at 16, 138 at 32; attributes 115 to 148) and the kernel uses 24 bytes of local memory stores per ray (triangles) at every payload size, so nothing spills (the 255 register limit is far away) and the local traffic belongs to the trace call itself, not to the payload. The registers matter through occupancy: past 128 registers per thread an SM holds 3 instead of 4 blocks of 128 threads (13.6 to 10.3 active warps), which costs 3 percent of the speed for random rays and 18 percent for coherent rays; the step is at 16 payload values with 8 attributes and 32 payload values with 2 attributes. Custom primitives show the same step, and 66 bytes of local stores and 34 of loads per ray (random) from the intersection program call. Details: model/results_payload_4070ti.md.
113. **2026-11-15, 4070 Ti: the performance model predicts the grouping speedups of bounce rays from the scene alone, to about 10 percent, with nothing fitted on this chip.** Inputs per launch order came from a CPU replay over the decoded OptiX BVH (queries per ray, lines requested and L1 misses per ray from reuse distances, 60 SMs, 48 MB L2); the model's parameters were fitted on the RTX 6000 Ada (one SM data) and one conversion constant (sectors per line request) was set on triton's random ray set. For the 100,000 and 3 million triangle soups the predicted speedups over pixel order of six launch orders (shuffled, origin Morton, oracle grouping at three depths and in full) differ from the measured ones by 0.087 and 0.103 in root mean square log error (about 9 and 11 percent); the same recipe on triton's random rays had 0.36. The shuffled order is predicted 9 to 19 percent too mild, the Morton order 5 to 8 percent too fast, and the deep oracle groupings 8 to 14 percent too low (the model sees no gain from depth 2 to the full key, the chip shows 5 to 10 percent). Caveats: the prediction script was written after the measurements existed (no parameter was changed after seeing the result, but this is not pre-registered), two scenes, one chip, trace only (no sort cost), and the replay's queries per ray (1.43 and 1.55) were not measured on this chip. Details: model/results_scene_prediction_4070ti.md.
114. **2026-11-15, 4070 Ti, IAS harness (BVH_4): the instance level traversal group can now be built, shape checked and its inputs verified, but it has not been run.** The setup host builds one instance of the triangle structure (identity transform) under an instance structure, traces through it with OptiX, and prepares the register image of the instance level group: the instance structure's root offset field reads 0x700 in our own process (as read from the code dump in Finding 91), the handle carries type 4, and OptiX's answer through the instance level agrees with the CPU reference for hit, miss and back facing rays. The two ray header words of the group were computed by running the straight line code in front of it on its raw fields: both low words equal those of the single level path (flags NONE, mask 255; shadow flags add the same 0x800), while the second word differs from the single level path and is not verified by hardware. The shape checker passes 28 checks on the group (store and load offsets, the three negated and one plain predicate, fuse count 15, close bit on the last load only) and fails when one predicate is removed. Only the no TTU twin ran (1 and 4 warps, three rays, inputs intact). Whether the hardware accepts the first query words is untested.
115. **2026-11-15, why the RT core loses for RAGRT stage 1 (model argument, nothing run on RAGRT).** RAGRT sends one ray per query slice into a scene of one triangle per codeword and records every codeword above a threshold with an any hit program, so a ray costs one query to the RT core per selected codeword plus one. Using the measured query cost (about 7.4 cycles per query on one SM at best, 1,640 to 2,315 cycles of latency), the round trip of a program the RT core cannot run (850 to 900 cycles, measured for custom primitives, assumed for any hit) and the cost of brute force on the CUDA cores (about 0.04 cycles per codeword and ray for a 3D dot product), the RT core wins only if less than about 0.5 percent of the codewords pass the threshold (0.26 percent with a program issue cost, 0.04 percent for incoherent rays); at 256 codewords that is fewer than 1.4 hits per ray. At a fixed selectivity the ratio does not improve with more codewords. Predictions that can fail, and what is not measured, are in the draft section (notes) and model/ragrt_case.py.
116. **2026-11-15, RTNN on the 4070 Ti, predictions committed first (ca7620e): five of six times inside the predicted ranges, time is linear in candidates per query, one prediction wrong by a factor 19.** Uniform point clouds of 100,000 and 1,000,000 points, radius search, three radii each. Candidates per query (points whose box contains the query) were computed exactly on the CPU; the hardware's own count from a recorder build was 10 percent higher (54.2 against 49.2 and 69.6 against 63.1: the box test is conservative). Search times (synchronized) 0.22 to 6.4 ms against ranges built from the query rate; rms log error of the point predictions 0.51; the miss is the case dominated by a fixed cost (output buffer fill and launch, 0.17 and 1.18 ms) that the model did not have. After subtracting that baseline (after the fact) time ratios between radii match the prediction (6.2 against 6.1, 7.5 against 6.5) and one candidate costs 4 to 9 cycles per SM equivalent: the throughput cost of a custom primitive candidate is at the bottom of the bracket, so the 850 to 900 cycle round trip of Finding 49 is a latency that concurrency hides, not a throughput cost. Query and point order: unsorted costs 4.0 times on the 1,000,000 point cloud (predicted at least 1.5, right) and 22.9 times on the 100,000 one (predicted at most 1.2: wrong, a 2 MB structure is far outside the SM's 60 KB node cache, Finding 5 applied wrongly). RTNN's partitioning and sort machinery adds 0.8 s of host time on these clouds with no gain in search time. The first set of runs timed only the asynchronous launch call and was discarded. Details: model/results_rtnn_4070ti.md.
117. **2026-11-15, pre registered check of the performance model on random rays (4070 Ti), predictions committed first (a93c71d): rms log error 0.33 over 18 points, 0.12 on a real scene.** Two new soups (300,000 and 1,500,000 triangles) and Bistro Exterior (2.83 million triangles, re downloaded, not in git), 8.4 million random rays, six orders each. Soups: the model predicts 1.4 to 1.5 times for Morton and deep grouping where 2.2 to 2.35 is measured (error 0.42 and 0.38, always the same sign, mean signed error -0.28); Bistro: 1.22 to 1.32 measured, error 0.12 (only 35 percent of its random rays hit anything), with the Morton order wrongly predicted to lose (0.95 against 1.22).
