Why random rays were harder (0.36 on triton, 0.33 here, bounce rays 0.09 to 0.10): the error relative to the spread of the measured log speedups is the same (0.55 triton, 0.56 here, 0.33 bounce), but bounce rays gain only 1.1 to 1.3 times; and the model's miss coefficients were fitted on our own harness kernel whose grouping gain is 8 to 13 percent, so it flattens the 2 times effect OptiX shows on both chips. Not refitted. Details: model/results_random_check_4070ti.md.
118. **2026-11-15, BITTU: compute mode tool written, not yet validated on a TTU run; OptiX recorder mode ran on RTNN.** Compute mode (NVBit 1.8.1): decodes the hidden fused group from the checked binary's bytes with the EoSS SM89 tables (field positions generated from the tables, opcodes cross checked against our sequence file), refuses anything that is not one complete group, probes before the open and after the wait that follows the group record clocks (with a second back to back probe to measure probe cost), the ray registers and the load destinations per thread. It built and ran on the no TTU twin (finds no hidden instructions, as expected); the run on the TTU binary was not made (stage 1 runs await approval), so decoding, probe cost and agreement with the harness clock are untested. Recorder mode (OptiX, RTNN only): clocks around the trace call and a counter in the intersection program; per warp lane efficiency (candidates summed over lanes divided by 32 times the warp's maximum) is 0.78 and 0.82 with sorted queries and 0.74 and 0.78 unsorted (median max over mean candidates 1.2 to 1.35); per warp trace time divergence is always 1.0 (the clock after the trace is read after the warp reconverges, so it cannot show it). The recorder slows the launch 1.1 to 5 times (the counter updates are dependent global memory read modify writes), so its counts are valid and its timings are not.
119. **2026-11-15, BITTU compute mode on a TTU run (4070 Ti, the checked single level group, 1 warp, 5 launches): decoding, ray and result capture are exact; its timing is not usable.** The tool decoded the hidden 19 instruction group from the binary's bytes with the EoSS tables (fuse count 17, the six predicated stores, six loads, close bit on the last load only), read the eight ray registers (origin 0 0 -1, tmin 0, direction 0 0 1, tmax 1e16) and the 22 result registers (hit word, t, barycentrics: the values of the known load map) in all 32 lanes, with full warp masks and the instance count right in every launch; results of the instrumented run equal the plain run. Times: the harness clock measures 1,613 cycles for the group without the tool; with it the probe to probe window is 11,335 cycles (stable to 0.2 percent over 5 launches) and one probe costs about 1,450 cycles (two back to back probes), so subtracting one probe overhead leaves 9,900: wrong by a factor 6. The window contains the tail of the first probe and the head of the second, which save and restore a full register file and are far more than one probe's clock to clock gap, and the group itself may run slower inside. The first version used managed memory and its first touch cost 1.8 million cycles in the first probe (fixed by device buffers). Counts and data yes, latencies no; a usable timer needs probes that read the clock without a register spill, or the harness's own clock reads.
120. **2026-11-15, refitting the miss terms of the performance model on OptiX data fixes the random ray flattening but not bounce rays.** Only the three miss coefficients were refitted (on soup 300,000 random rays and bounce rays on the 100,000 soup; scene based CPU replay inputs unchanged). The Ada fit had put all weight on the L1 sector term and none on fills or DRAM; the refit puts it on the fill term. Test scenes (not in the fit): soup 1,500,000 random rays 0.375 to 0.105, Bistro 0.117 to 0.078, bounce rays on the 3 million soup 0.103 to 0.187 (worse); training sets 0.419 to 0.065 and 0.087 to 0.098. So a single set of coefficients does not cover both ray kinds. RTNN could not be rechecked (no replay inputs for its structure); the refit raises the weight of misses, which is the direction of the 22.9 times unsorted result, but that is no test of it. Script model/refit_optix.py.
121. **2026-11-15, correction to Finding 118: the flight recorder costs 7 to 15 percent of kernel time, not 1.1 to 5 times.** The earlier numbers were the host timer around the launch, which includes the recorder's own host work (allocation, copies, report). With event timing around the launch alone: 100,000 points 0.356 ms plain, +11 percent recording every warp, +8 percent with 1 in 8 and 1 in 32 warps; 1,000,000 points 3.41 ms, +14.5 percent, +10.8 percent, +10.2 percent. Sampling barely helps because the cost is mostly the clock reads and stores of the recorded warps plus the larger launch parameters, not their count: lane efficiency is the same to 0.003 at every sampling rate (0.78 and 0.815).

122. **2026-11-15, BITTU timing fixed with an empty probe pair: the corrected group time equals the harness clock to within 1 percent at depths 1, 2 and 3.** The probes were placed around a block of no-ops of the same length in the twin build without the group (same probe code, same register lists, same offsets) and its probe to probe window was subtracted from the window of the real run. Depth 1, 2, 3 scenes of Latency_2, one warp, four timed launches each. Window with the group 11,297 / 11,588 / 12,080 cycles, window around no-ops 9,775 / 9,733 / 9,917, difference 1,522 / 1,855 / 2,163; the harness clock (its own two clock reads, no tool) gives 1,613 / 1,930 / 2,234 for the group and 80 for the empty slots, so group minus empty slots is 1,533 / 1,850 / 2,154: the corrected tool time is within 11, 6 and 9 cycles of it (0.7, 0.3, 0.4 percent). The overhead is nearly constant but not exactly: the empty window is 9,733 to 9,917 (2 percent spread across the three scenes, 0.4 percent between two runs of one scene), and the clock to clock cost of one probe is 1,441 to 1,459 cycles. Single probe subtraction (Finding 119) was wrong because a probe's save and restore code lies inside the window; the empty pair contains the same two. The tool can now time a group provided a twin build with no-ops in the group's place exists; for OptiX's own kernels the twin does not exist (not applicable there, they are not instrumentable, Finding 60).

123. **2026-11-15, one model for both ray kinds: a reuse feature plus warp level sharing gives a held out error of 0.08, against 0.13 to 0.15 for the separate miss coefficient models.** The miss part of the performance model was rebuilt from the CPU replay's reuse distance histograms of one SM's node fetch stream (identical requests of a warp merged) and fitted once, on the same training sets as Finding 120 (soup 300,000 random rays and bounce rays on the 100,000 soup, 12 points), then tested on the 18 points of random rays on the 1.5 million soup, random rays on Bistro and bounce rays on the 3 million soup. Variants (rms log error of the predicted speedup over the natural order, held out): the Finding 120 structure with its fixed part scaled by one free factor 0.148; the hard share of fetches with reuse distance under 480 lines (the 60 KB node cache) times the fetches 0.148; the same with a gradual hit probability 1 over 1 plus (distance over capacity) to a power (capacity and power fitted) 0.132; and that gradual share with the miss cost per fetch divided by the number of lane fetches per warp fetch (how many lanes share a node request) 0.080 with the exponent fitted (0.85) and 0.077 with the exponent fixed at 1 (four parameters). Fitted capacity 475 lines, which is the 480 lines of the 60 KB node cache nobody told the fit about, and a power of 2.1. The sharing ratio differs by kind: about 1.1 for random rays in natural order, about 1.9 for bounce rays, 1.4 for Bistro, and rises to 1.5 to 2.3 when rays are grouped. Without it the model over predicts the gain of bounce rays (1.5 against 1.3 on the 3 million soup) and under predicts random rays (1.9 to 2.0 against 2.3), which is the bias that Finding 120 could not remove. Leaving each of the five sets out in turn (fit on the other four): rms 0.074 over all 30 points, worst 0.108 (random rays on the 1.5 million soup); the fitted capacity and power move a lot between these fits (capacity 365 to 1,580 lines), the errors do not. Caveats: five scene and ray sets, five parameters, the DRAM term gets zero weight in every fit (D inputs are crude), and the sharing term is an empirical description (miss latency of requests several lanes share overlaps), not a measured mechanism. RTNN is tested separately (Finding 126). Code model/reuse_feature.py, output notes/q11/reuse_feature.txt.

124. **2026-11-15, per term breakdown of the one model on 4070 Ti OptiX launches: the total speedup error (rms 0.06) is much smaller than the errors of its terms, so the fit hides compensating errors; the total error shows no trend with scene size or BVH depth but a small one with lane sharing.** For 20 of the 30 validation points (random rays on three scenes, bounce rays on two, 4 to 5 orders each) the launch was profiled with ncu (one launch, caches and clocks left alone) and the per ray counts compared with the replay inputs of the model: L1 requests of the RT unit (all L1 sectors minus the load store pipe), L1 fills, DRAM bytes. Natural order L1 requests are right to 0.03 in log on four of five scenes (93.2 against 92.7 predicted on the 300,000 soup) and 1.4 times too high on Bistro (24.1 predicted, replay counts more fetches than the hardware makes for rays that leave or miss the scene: only 35 percent hit). Across orders the predicted change in L1 requests has rms log error 0.17 (mean +0.08: the model over predicts the number of requests of grouped orders, e.g. 66 against 52 for Morton on the 300,000 soup, 28 against 12 on Bistro) and the change in fills has 0.50 (mean -0.27): for bounce rays the replay says fills fall to 2 to 8 sectors per ray and the hardware shows 5 to 14, because every launch also carries about 4 to 5 sectors per ray of ray and result traffic that the model has no term for. The DRAM term is nearly absent in the measurement (5 to 20 bytes per ray beyond the ray fetch, against 50 to 320 predicted for the 1.5 million soup and Bistro). Queries per ray and triangles per ray have no counter on this GPU (no ray counters in ncu on AD104), so they are compared only through the earlier direct harness check (Finding 81: queries 1.85 and 1.98 predicted against 1.88 and 2.00 measured), not for these scenes. Total speedup error by group (mean signed, predicted minus measured in log): BVH under 10 MiB -0.01, 10 to 100 MiB -0.05, over 100 MiB -0.02; estimated depth under 5 -0.01, 5 to 6 -0.05, over 6 -0.02; lane sharing under 1.3 (random rays) -0.05, 1.3 to 1.7 (Bistro) -0.07, over 1.7 (bounce rays) +0.01. Correlations of the error with log BVH size -0.17, with depth -0.18, with measured speedup -0.18, with lane sharing 0.46. The pattern is a mild under prediction (mean -0.03) on large random ray scenes (1.5 million soup -0.09, Bistro -0.07) and a mild over prediction on the largest bounce set with the Morton order (+0.14, 1.32 against 1.15). Reading: the fit is good because its miss cost absorbs the unmodelled launch traffic and the DRAM and request errors; the terms are not individually right. Code ttu/bench/Morton/term_ncu.py, model/term_breakdown.py; table and figures in notes/q11.

125. **2026-11-15, RTNN on the 4070 Ti: a CPU replay of its custom primitive BVH reproduces the hardware's per query counts, and the earlier 22.9 times and 3.98 times for unsorted against sorted (Finding 116) are not reproduced: a rebuilt binary with event timing gives 1.37 and 1.60.** The replay was extended to the box (AABB) primitives of RTNN's acceleration structure (a point query in a radius search is a ray of length 1e-16; the tree was decoded from a dump of the structure the process built). Per query, 100,000 points radius 4 and 1,000,000 points radius 2: the replay enters 22.6 and 27.9 internal nodes and 43.0 and 54.4 leaf slots, tests 53.9 and 69.3 primitives that sit in those slots, and 49.2 and 63.1 of them contain the query point (the exact count). The recorder (a counter in the intersection program) counts 54.24 and 69.65 calls on the hardware: 1.006 times the primitives in the entered leaf slots in both cases and 1.10 times the exact count. So the box test of the hardware is on the leaf slot, and every primitive of an entered slot gets its intersection program called; the 10 percent surplus of Finding 116 is the leaf slot granularity (1.2 primitives per slot), not a rounding error. Queries per ray (returns to the SM) are between 43 and 54 (one per entered slot with a candidate plus one) and 50 to 64 (one per candidate plus one). Lane efficiency (candidates summed over lanes over 32 times the warp maximum, recorder): sorted 0.777 and 0.815, unsorted 0.737 and 0.776; mean per warp maximum candidates 70 and 86 sorted, 74 and 90 unsorted. Times of the search launch (CUDA events, median of 5): 100,000 points 0.369 ms sorted, 0.504 ms unsorted (1.37 times); 1,000,000 points 3.49 ms and 5.59 ms (1.60 times); per warp trace time in the recorder grows 1.59 and 1.58 times unsorted. Correction of Finding 116: its process timer for the unsorted configuration read 10.6 and 16.6 ms against 0.46 and 4.2 ms sorted; the rebuilt binary (same sources plus recorder, CUDA 12.8, same flags as far as recorded, but not the same build) reads 1.6 ms for the whole search step and 0.50 ms for the kernel in the unsorted configuration at 100,000 points. The extra 9 to 12 ms of the earlier numbers is not a property of the query order; it looks like a one time cost in the first launch of a process (the unsorted configuration has no earlier launch, the sorted one has an initial traversal launch), but the earlier binary is gone and the cause is not verified. The sorting effect on this hardware is therefore 1.4 to 1.6 times, and the sentence in Finding 116 about 22.9 times must not be used. Code model/replay/rtnn_prep.py, replay_aabb.cpp, reuse_aabb.cpp; patched RTNN diff in notes (not committed: third party code).
126. **2026-11-15, the one model on RTNN: the reuse feature points the right way but under predicts the sorting gain (1.1 to 1.26 predicted against 1.37 and 1.60 measured), and the model with the fixed part for a query that returns to the SM 50 times is far from the absolute time.** From the replay: the share of node fetches with reuse distance beyond the 60 KB node cache is 0.14 (sorted) against 0.84 (unsorted) for 100,000 points, 0.13 against 0.95 for 1,000,000 points (cold share 0.13 against 0.13, and 0.12 against 0.60); lane sharing falls from 1.5 to 1.1 when unsorted. With the model fitted only on triangle launches (Finding 123, nothing refitted) and q taken as the number of returns per ray, the predicted unsorted over sorted time is 1.09 and 1.10 (nodes only), 1.20 and 1.20 (nodes and leaf slots as fetches), with fewer returns (one per entered slot with a candidate) 1.25 and 1.26; measured 1.37 and 1.60. Log errors -0.09 and -0.24 at best. With q fixed at 1 (as if the custom primitive returns cost nothing) the model predicts 2.35 and 2.33 (leaf slots included), too high: the answer depends on how much of the time is the per candidate return, which the model's fixed part does not know (it is fitted on 1 to 4 returns per ray and gives 1,000 to 1,400 cycles per ray where 520 to 560 cycles per query per SM are measured). The ratio direction (sorted faster, more for the larger structure) is right, the 22.9 times is not predicted and, per Finding 125, is not there. This is one configuration pair on one GPU with one set of radii; the model's custom primitive fixed cost would have to be refitted before it could be used here (Finding 116 gives 4 to 9 cycles per candidate per SM equivalent). Code model/rtnn_model.py, output notes/q11/rtnn_model.txt.

127. **2026-11-15, refit of the model's fixed part for custom primitives on RTNN radii: held out error of the absolute time falls from about 1.0 to 0.4 to 0.6 (log), the unsorted over sorted ratio error from 0.24 to 0.10 to 0.15, and the refit fails when it must extrapolate to the smallest radius.** Twelve configurations on the 4070 Ti (100,000 points radius 2, 4, 6 and 1,000,000 points radius 1, 2, 3, each sorted and unsorted, launch time by CUDA events, median of 5; replay and recorder inputs as in Finding 125). Measured cost 132 to 1,272 cycles per query per SM; primitives in entered leaf slots per query 10.8 to 228 (the recorder's hardware count equals the replay's for radius up to 4 on 100,000 points and 2 on 1,000,000, and is lower at the two largest radii, 105 against 228 for 1,000,000 points radius 3: the ray terminates after 50 results, the replay does not model that). Before: the triangle fit's fixed part (1,000 to 1,400 cycles at 55 returns) gives log errors of 0.3 to 1.9 on the absolute cycles, largest at large radii. After: fixed = c0 + cq x q (q = primitives in entered slots plus 1), two parameters least squares on two radii of one point set, tested on the third (six splits): mean error 0.98 before, 0.63 after (nodes only miss term); fitted c0 50 to 420 and cq 1.8 to 8.8 cycles per return, in line with the 4 to 9 cycles per candidate per SM equivalent of Finding 116. The refit is good when the test radius lies between or above the fit radii (0.34 to 0.59 for radius 4, 6 on 100,000 and 2, 3 on 1,000,000) and bad when it lies below (0.86 and 1.08 for the smallest radius: c0 is then poorly determined). Fitting on all three radii of one size and testing on the other size: 0.335 and 0.310 (before 1.22 and 0.94); with leaf slots counted as fetches 0.357 and 0.296. Ratio of unsorted over sorted time, held out |log error|: 0.236 before, 0.143 after (0.100 with a free miss scale, which fits to 1.1 to 1.6); the 1.37 and 1.60 of Finding 125 are then predicted to within 0.05 to 0.2 in log. So a custom primitive cost of about 5 cycles per return plus 100 cycles fixed is the refit, but the number of returns needs the K = 50 early termination to be right at large radii. Code model/rtnn_refit.py, output notes/q12/rtnn_refit.txt.

128. **2026-11-15, inventory of the real workload code on io: of the four named codes only RT-BarnesHut is here; our own growing radius kNN program runs after three fixes; TrueKNN (RT-kNNS Unbound), Arkade and Mochi are not available.** The Arkade archive in the home directory is a 45 byte file (not an archive). No TrueKNN or Mochi sources were found. RT-BarnesHut (OWL based, with a built binary and sample outputs) builds from source here (OptiX 8.1) after setting the body count constant (the original is compiled for 100 million bodies) and the GPU index (the original hard codes GPU 1, which is the display GPU on io); it needs treelogy format input files (none are in the directory, one was generated). The home directory kNN program (a growing radius, one round per doubling, custom primitive version of the TrueKNN idea; it is our own code, not the paper's) failed on io for three independent reasons, each fixed in a patched copy only: CUDA was not initialised before OptiX (OPTIX_ERROR_CUDA_NOT_INITIALIZED), the intersection program reported hit distance 1e-6 beyond the ray length 1e-16 so every hit was discarded and no neighbour was ever found (the program ran to its 15 round limit with every query active), and the acceleration structure memory was freed right after the build while it was still traversed (an illegal memory access once the input exceeds a few thousand points). A fourth defect remains by design: the termination test counts candidates inside the cube of the radius, not the sphere, so the neighbours returned are the nearest among cube candidates, not guaranteed nearest. Diffs are in notes (third party and home directory code are not in git).
129. **2026-11-15, RT-BarnesHut on the 4070 Ti, predictions committed first (67366ab): traversals and lane efficiency predicted exactly, time outside the bracket for 100,000 bodies and inside it for 1,000,000.** Bodies in a Gaussian disc, opening angle 0.5. The program's loop (one traversal per visited tree node, the rope walk) was predicted by a CPU walk of the program's own tree: 158.3 and 223.0 traversals per body; the recorder (a loop counter per body in the device code) counts 158.32 and 222.98, and lane efficiency (sum over lanes over 32 times the warp maximum, launch order = tree order) 0.877 and 0.909 predicted against 0.877 and 0.9087 measured (equal to three digits: the loop count is exactly the visit count of the standard walk, a hit or miss event per node). Launch times (synchronised, median of 3, the original timer did not wait for the launch): 10.6 ms (bracket 0.78 to 6.76, point 2.1) for 100,000 bodies and 74.3 ms (bracket 11 to 95, point 29.7) for 1,000,000: 100 and 50 cycles per traversal per SM against the bracket's 7.4 to 64. The shading work (850 to 1,020 leaf interactions per body, each a dependent load pair) is the part the bracket did not include; per warp loop time is 9.1 million and 10 million cycles. Compaction opportunity: lane efficiency 0.88 to 0.91 means at most 1.10 to 1.14 times from regrouping lanes, and the program already orders bodies in tree order. Nodes and primitives per traversal were not predicted (no first hit replay mode with the real ray sequence yet). Files: model/predictions_bh_4070ti.md, model/predict_bh.py.
130. **2026-11-15, growing radius kNN on the 4070 Ti (200,000 uniform points, K 16, initial radius 0.004, doubling), predictions committed first (92115c2): candidates per query and lane efficiency predicted to 1 percent and 0.04, launch time under predicted by 3.5 to 15 times, the last round's active set wrong (5,411 predicted, 83 measured).** The CPU replay of each round's acceleration structure (dumped after the OptiX build, no search launch) and rays gave, per round 1 to 4, primitives in entered leaf slots 1.134, 1.919, 8.145, 56.0 (hardware candidates 1.135, 1.930, 8.207, 56.27 measured, ratio 1.0 to 1.007 as in Finding 125) and lane efficiency 0.514, 0.427, 0.553, 0.744 (random order) and 0.545, 0.453, 0.588, 0.786 (Morton order) against measured 0.513, 0.427, 0.554, 0.745 and 0.544, 0.453, 0.589, 0.786. The refit fixed part (Finding 127) predicted launch times of 0.19 / 0.20 / 0.28 / 0.73 ms (random order; sum 1.47 ms) and measured 0.65 / 0.99 / 3.5 / 11.1 / 0.59 ms (sum 16.8 ms); Morton order predicted 1.14 ms in total and measured 16.1 ms. Per candidate cost 430 / 384 / 321 / 150 cycles per SM (RTNN: 5 to 9): this program's any hit shader inserts every candidate into a 16 entry sorted result heap in global memory and its raygen reads 32 heap words per query afterwards; neither is in the model (which was fitted on a program whose intersection program only writes results), and the cause was not isolated by a variant. The active set rule was wrong: the program stops a query when its heap holds K candidates from the cube around the query, so only 2,340 queries stop in round 3 and all but 83 in round 4, where the replay's sphere based rule predicted 5,411 still active in round 5 (the round 5 time prediction is therefore for the wrong launch size). Morton ordering of the queries saves 4.5 percent of launch time (16.1 against 16.8 ms) here, because the point set is only 3.2 MB of boxes at 200,000 points. Compaction opportunity (the question for a TrueKNN style design): lane efficiency per round is 0.51, 0.43, 0.55, 0.74, 0.68 in random order, time weighted 0.675; perfect regrouping of lanes by work would give at most 1.48 times of launch time (1.41 times in Morton order), and rounds 1 to 3 (0.4 to 0.6) carry 30 percent of the time but have few candidates, so their cost is the fixed per query cost, which compaction does not touch. The sum of the launches is 16.8 ms of a 166 to 170 ms search (host side BVH rebuilds, copies and my duplicate check dominate). Not TrueKNN itself: the paper's code is not available. Files: model/predictions_knn_4070ti.md, model/predict_knn.py.

131. **2026-11-15, SM callback term, measured not fitted: the per program cost of the any hit, intersection, closest hit and miss code on the SMs, from ncu instruction and memory counts and microbenchmarked costs, explains the time of three programs that the traversal only model missed by 1.5 to 15 times.** Counts per SASS function (ncu source counters on the OptiX launch: warp calls, warp instructions per call, L1 tag requests, local accesses, L2 sectors, and the launch's L1 hit rate for global loads) and three costs from a plain CUDA microbenchmark on the 4070 Ti (0.25 SM cycles per warp instruction, 1.00 per L1 tag request, 2.9 per scattered L2 sector); the form (the larger of issue and memory time per call, with a no overlap sum as a variant) was committed before any comparison with launch times. The counts are large and different per program: the RTNN intersection program is 76 warp instructions and 26 to 57 tag requests per call, the kNN any hit shader 210 to 240 instructions and 400 to 1,400 tag requests per call (its heap lives in global memory; 50 to 60 stores per call), RT-BarnesHut's closest hit and miss programs 630 to 880 instructions and 530 to 580 tag requests per call (a node's 32 particles are read through divergent loads, about 3.3 tag requests per load, and 46 local stores per call for the payload). Results (log ratio predicted over measured, rms): RTNN, 12 configurations, held out splits as in Finding 127: radius splits 0.63 before (two fitted parameters), 0.50 with the term and one fitted parameter (0.32 with the no overlap form), size transfers 0.32 before and 0.35 after (0.28), but the sorted over unsorted ratio error gets worse (0.05 to 0.18 before, 0.3 to 0.5 after) because the replay miss term and the callback term both carry the sorting effect; the measured callback cost alone correlates 0.96 to 0.98 with the 12 measured cycles per query, the replay's primitives in slots 0.88 and its miss term 0.78. Growing radius kNN rounds 1 to 4 (a program not used in any fit): 2.11 before, 0.56 with the term (0.52 no overlap form); the any hit heap cost is the 6 to 15 times that Finding 130 could not explain. RT-BarnesHut: 1,000,000 bodies 74.3 ms measured, 74.3 predicted (before 30.3, error 0.00 against 0.90); 100,000 bodies 10.4 ms measured, 5.6 predicted (before 2.2): at 3,125 warps the launch is not throughput bound, the term is a throughput floor. Post hoc variants (chosen after seeing the above, so not tests): no miss term, and a scale on the callback term fitted on RTNN only (scale 1.36, c0 98): RTNN held out 0.26 to 0.21, kNN rounds 1 to 4 0.37, ratio error 0.19. The scale above 1 means the throughput form underestimates the callback cost by about a third (latency not fully hidden). Not covered: round 5 of the kNN (83 queries, 3 warps, far from throughput bound), the miss term of the callback loads at a different cache state, programs with a callback on the RT unit side only. Files: model/callback_counts.py, callback_cost.py, cbcost/cbcost.cu, callback_eval.py, results_callback_cost.md, results_callback_term.md, results_callback_counts.md.

132. **2026-11-15, replay fixes, compaction test, Arkade and Mochi on the 4070 Ti.** (1) kNN stop rule: the program inserts every candidate the hardware hands over (its heap starts at the largest float), so a query stops when its replay candidates (primitives in entered leaf slots) reach K, not on exact cube or sphere counts; with that rule the replay gives 197,815 active queries in round 4 (measured 197,660) and 87 in round 5 (measured 83), against 5,411 before (model/knn_rounds.py; the exact cube count rule of model/knn_active.py gives 127 and is also wrong). (2) Compaction on the growing radius kNN: lane efficiency is 0.43 to 0.74 per round, but ordering the queries by the previous round's candidate counts moves it only by 0.01 and time by under 1 percent, and ordering them by this round's true counts (an oracle, lane efficiency 0.9996 in every round) leaves launch time unchanged or 1 to 3 percent worse (3.56 against 3.47 ms in round 3, 11.05 against 10.95 in round 4). The 1.48 times ceiling of Finding 130 is not reachable by regrouping lanes: the time is set by the per candidate callback's memory traffic (a lane's heap lives in global memory, divergent loads cost the same with or without idle lanes), not by the warp maximum. A persistent kernel refilling lanes was not built: lanes inside one trace call cannot be refilled, and the oracle shows perfect balance buys nothing. (3) RT-BarnesHut first hit replay (real ray sequence of the program's loop, 158.32 and 222.98 traversals per body equal to the recorder; first hit termination): 3.90 nodes, 1.39 leaf slots, 2.88 triangles per traversal at 100,000 bodies, 4.59, 1.36, 2.69 at 1,000,000. The committed prediction of the RT unit's L1 sectors per traversal (19.6 to 22.4, 4 sectors per node) was WRONG: ncu measured 3.18 and 4.39, and the 1M over 100k growth is 1.38 against 1.14 from the replay. (4) Arkade builds and runs (OWL, OptiX 8.1; sample01-knn; its distance test compares against the radius squared for every norm, so the L infinity build almost never accepts a neighbour; I used the L2 build). On synthetic 2D data: candidates per query and lane efficiency predicted (10.91 / 54.02 / 10.62 against 10.90 / 53.86 / 11.24, lane efficiency within 0.003 for two configurations and 0.08 for 1,000,000 points), launch time wrong by 100 to 200 times (23 to 32 ms measured against 0.11 to 0.25): the intersection program calls pow on doubles for the L2 distance, 87 of its 170 warp instructions are double precision, and the launch is long scoreboard bound at 32 percent warp occupancy. The callback term of Finding 131 with a measured double precision cost (19 SM cycles per warp instruction) gives 3.7 ms: it is a throughput floor and misses latency bound callbacks by 7 times. (5) Mochi builds with OWL (sample23, flags -DBOX_WIDTH=1.0 -DRENDER=0 -DCOUNT_COLLISIONS=0 -DNUM_PARTICLES=100000) and runs 10 frames on the 100,000 particle file; predictions and measurements per frame were not done (time stepped simulation, BVH rebuilt each frame, needs per frame dumps). Files: model/knn_rounds.py, model/results_bh_replay_4070ti.md, model/results_arkade_4070ti.md, model/predict_arkade.py.

133. **2026-11-15, per call latency of the SM callbacks measured with clock64 probes inside the callback programs (RTNN, growing radius kNN, RT-BarnesHut, Arkade; 4070 Ti): the latency under load is 1.3 to 16 times the latency of the same program alone, so latency times calls over warps in flight predicts the time only when the loaded latency is measured, and then it is nearly a restatement of the measurement.** Probes (code in a patched copy, form fixed in model/q14/FORM.md and committed before the comparison): each callback program reads the SM clock at entry and exit, one lane per warp call adds the elapsed cycles and one call to a per warp counter; the raygen program records its start, the cycles before and after the trace and its end. Probe cost on the launch time: kNN 0 to 2 percent (18 percent on the 83 query round), RTNN 6 to 31 percent (a short program called 400,000 to 9 million times), RT-BarnesHut 4 percent (1,000,000 bodies) and 19 percent (100,000), Arkade within noise; an empty program costs about 125 cycles per call. Latency per warp call, loaded against one warp per SM (1,920 queries, same code): RTNN sorted 765 to 840 against 575 to 660 cycles (1.3 times), RTNN unsorted 1,200 to 1,400 against 790 to 1,340 (1.0 to 1.6), kNN rounds 1 to 4 (intersection program with the any hit inside) 62,000 / 42,000 / 42,000 / 24,000 against 12,600 / 9,300 / 8,400 / 5,800 (4.1 to 5.0), RT-BarnesHut closest hit or miss 36,000 against 22,000 (1.6), Arkade 128,000 to 367,000 against 20,000 to 23,000 (6 to 16). Warps in flight (ncu) are 13 to 15 for RTNN, kNN and Arkade (110 to 114 registers per thread) and 24 to 25 for RT-BarnesHut; summed warp time over 60 SMs times the clock gives implied warps in flight within about 20 percent of the ncu value in nearly every launch (the exceptions are the 100,000 body RT-BarnesHut launch, 18 against 24, because of its first launch cost, and the 83 query kNN round). Error of the callback busy time (log ratio predicted over measured, rms over the launches): the throughput floor of Finding 131 gives 0.34 (RTNN), 0.55 (kNN rounds 1 to 4), 0.28 (RT-BarnesHut), 1.99 (Arkade); max(floor, unloaded latency x calls / warps in flight) gives 0.24, 0.55, 0.28, 1.93 (the unloaded latency is below the floor for kNN, RT-BarnesHut and Arkade, so it adds nothing); the loaded latency gives 0.10, 0.07, 0.22, 0.08, which is a consistency check, not a prediction. Launch time: RTNN held out radius splits 0.64 (floor), 0.66 (unloaded latency), 0.58 (loaded); size transfers 0.44, 0.45, 0.40; kNN rounds 1 to 4 0.42, 0.43, 0.07; RT-BarnesHut 1,000,000 bodies -0.05, -0.05, +0.04 and 100,000 bodies -0.59, -0.59, -0.50; Arkade -1.95 / -1.97 / -3.16 with the floor and -0.42 / -0.20 / -0.47 with the loaded latency. Post hoc (chosen after the numbers, not a test): loaded latency = unloaded latency + (warps in flight - 1) x k x floor cost per call; k = 0.37 from RTNN (median) predicts RT-BarnesHut within 0.05 (its k is 0.42 to 0.44) and RTNN sorted, but not RTNN unsorted at 1,000,000 points (+0.23 to +0.38), kNN (k 1.4 to 1.7, error -0.8 to -1.0) or Arkade (k 4.6 to 14.5). So queueing proportional to the issue and memory cost explains the callbacks that are throughput like (RT-BarnesHut, sorted RTNN) and not the ones with a dependent chain of memory accesses. In the kNN program a bisection of the any hit shader (clock probes, program variants) shows the cost is in its insertion loop: load plus two stores (no loop) 1,100 cycles per call and launch 0.14 ms, full program 55,000 cycles per call and launch 0.68 ms in round 1; the cause inside the loop (a global store followed by a dependent load of the same address, 15 times) is a hypothesis. Files: model/q14/cb_latency.py, FORM.md, parse_logs.py, model/results_latency_term.md.

134. **2026-11-15, time breakdown per workload (share of summed warp time between raygen start and end, probes on): callbacks 25 to 40 percent of the time in RTNN, 69 to 84 percent in the growing radius kNN, 62 to 83 percent in RT-BarnesHut and 63 to 77 percent in Arkade; the part outside traversal and callbacks is under 2 percent except the first launch of RT-BarnesHut.** Callbacks = outermost callback program (RTNN, kNN, Arkade: intersection program, which contains kNN's any hit; RT-BarnesHut: the optixInvoke of the closest hit or miss); traversal = trace window minus callbacks (RT-BarnesHut: the optixTraverse windows); other = the rest of the raygen program. RTNN (12 configurations): callbacks 25 to 34 percent sorted and 32 to 40 percent unsorted, traversal 60 to 73 percent, other 0 to 2 percent. kNN rounds 1 to 4: callbacks 69 / 77 / 84 / 84 percent, traversal 28 / 22 / 15 / 16 percent (round 5, 83 queries: 72 and 28). RT-BarnesHut 1,000,000 bodies: callbacks 83, traversal 11, other 6 percent (loop glue 3, first touch prologue 3); 100,000 bodies: callbacks 62, traversal 7, other 30 percent, of which 28 percent is the time from raygen start to the loop, a first launch cost (same program second launch: prologue 1.6 against 9.3 billion warp cycles; launch 6.4 to 7.1 ms second against 10.1 to 10.8 ms first; at 1,000,000 bodies the first launch is 3 to 7 ms longer than the second). The model error of Finding 133 for the 100,000 body case (-0.59) falls to about -0.19 when compared with the second launch (5.6 ms predicted against 6.4 to 7.1 ms). Arkade: callbacks 63 to 77 percent, traversal 21 to 33 percent, other 1 to 4 percent, but see Finding 136: its callback window is not the program's own work. Caveat: shares of warp time equal shares of launch time only at constant warps in flight; the probes add 0 to 31 percent to the launch time (Finding 133). Files: model/results_latency_term.md (all rows).

135. **2026-11-15, RT-BarnesHut RT unit L1 sectors per traversal (3.17 and 4.39 measured, 19.6 to 22.4 predicted in Finding 132): requests of the lanes of a warp for the same node in the same traversal step are merged, which brings the prediction to +2.7 percent (100,000 bodies, post hoc) and -12 percent (1,000,000 bodies, held out).** The counter (l1tex sectors minus the LSU pipe sectors) has no request count: every L1 request on this launch is an LSU request, and the texture pipe count is 0. Replay of the real ray sequence in warps of 32 bodies in launch order (model/q14/warp_share.cpp): per raw lane traversal 3.90 node fetches, 1 instance node and 1.39 leaf slots; merged across lanes at the same iteration and step 0.534 nodes, 0.036 instance nodes and 0.491 leaf slots (lane sharing ratio 7.3 for nodes); at 4 sectors per node and 2 per leaf slot that is 3.26 sectors against 3.17 measured. Merging at the same iteration at any step gives 2.94; an ideal cache of everything a warp ever fetched gives 0.19, so the unit merges simultaneous requests, it does not remember a warp's history. At 1,000,000 bodies (three windows of 1,000 warps, the ray dump of the whole launch is 7 GB) 3.86 predicted against 4.39 measured; the window to window spread of merged nodes (0.59 to 0.81) is about 15 percent. Not explained: the growth from 100,000 to 1,000,000 bodies is 1.38 measured and 1.18 predicted; the leaf slot size (1.8 sectors per merged leaf slot fits the first case exactly). The failed prediction of Finding 132 assumed no merging; the earlier model of Finding 123 (lane sharing as raw over merged fetches) already carried this effect for the other workloads. Files: model/results_bh_sectors_4070ti.md, model/q14/warp_share.cpp.

136. **2026-11-15, Arkade distance function in float instead of double: no speedup (0.83 to 1.01 times), identical neighbours; the double precision pow is NOT the cause of the 100 to 200 times time gap (Finding 132 withdrawn on this point).** Three builds of the distance in the intersection program (L2 norm, 2D synthetic data, 100,000 points and queries, radius 0.005 and 0.0112, and 1,000,000 points with 100,000 queries, radius 0.0016; CUDA event time of the second launch, median of 5, probes off): the original (std::pow of a float and an int, which is a double precision pow) 21.5 / 30.4 / 24.3 ms; powf in float 25.8 / 30.6 / 26.7 ms; a plain float square (no pow, no double precision instruction left in the PTX) 25.3 / 31.7 / 24.1 ms. The 16 neighbours of every query are the same set in all three builds (0 differing queries of 100,000), the same ids with distances differing by at most 1.2e-7 relative. A bisection of the intersection program: an empty program (returns at once) gives the same launch time, 23.1 ms against 23.1 ms for the full program; double precision distance without the result list 22.7 ms; float square without the result list 22.3 ms; result list scan without write 21.3 ms. So the time is set by the traversal and callback hand off path of this program (OWL: instance level around the custom primitive group; results written to host pinned memory, where the raygen part outside the trace window is 27 percent of warp time with the empty program), not by the arithmetic or the result list in the intersection program. The probe cycles per call (360,000 for the full program, 20,000 for the empty one, against 125 for an empty program in the kNN code) therefore mostly measure time between two clock reads that is not the program's own instructions; the cause of that was not found (an instruction fetch or hand off stall is a guess). Consequence for the model: the Arkade rows of Finding 133 (loaded latency matches by construction) say nothing about the program, and the SM callback term cannot be tested on Arkade until the base cost of its launch is explained. Files: model/results_arkade_float_4070ti.md, model/q14/ark_variants.py; the patch diffs are in notes/q14/diffs (not in git). CORRECTION 2026-11-16 (Finding 151): that test was run with the pinned host output, whose 20 ms write hides the 1.8 ms of the distance function; with a device buffer on triton the float versions are 11 times faster, so Finding 132 was right on this point and Finding 136's withdrawal is itself withdrawn.

137. **2026-11-16, hand off term for custom primitives (875 cycles per warp call of the intersection program, from Finding 49, not refitted): large gain for RTNN, none for the growing radius kNN, none for Arkade; and the Arkade launch is NOT explained by it (the empty intersection build needs about 520 times more per call).** Form fixed and committed before any comparison (model/q15/FORM_handoff.md): T_hand = 875 x warp calls / (60 x warps in flight), added to the callback floor of Finding 131 (the round trip is outside the program's own instructions). RTNN, 12 configurations, c0 refit on the other radii of the same size as before: held out radius splits 0.64 (floor only) to 0.13 (warp calls from the probe count), 0.38 (warp calls predicted by the replay from candidates over 32 times lane efficiency, no run needed); size transfers 0.44 to 0.27 and 0.32; the fitted fixed part c0 falls from 184 to 74 and 112 cycles per query. The hand off term is 59 to 115 percent of the measured callback window time in RTNN, so it is as large as the program's own cost there. A per lane version (875 per candidate, not per warp call) fails (1.53, c0 driven to 0), and max(floor, hand off) is worse than the sum (0.54). Growing radius kNN rounds 1 to 4: 0.42 before, 0.53 (per warp call), 0.38 (per lane): no gain, the term is 1 to 4 percent of its callback time. Arkade (three configurations): 2.42 before, 2.39 after; the term is 0.04 to 0.17 ms against 22 to 30 ms measured. Arkade with an empty intersection program (100,000 points, radius 0.005, probes on, median of 3): 20.8 ms, 105,089 warp calls (the same as the full program), the term gives 0.040 ms (log error -6.25), and the measured time implies about 454,000 SM cycles per warp call at 15.3 warps in flight, 518 times the 875. So the Arkade base cost is not the BVH_3 round trip; it stays unexplained (instance level, host pinned output, or something else in OWL's path: untested). Honest reading of the RTNN gain: it was written down as "a little better or unchanged", the result is much better; the term is partly correlated with what c0 (a per query constant) absorbed before, only 12 launches and one fitted parameter test it, and the 0.13 uses the measured warp call count while the replay version (0.38) is the real prediction. Files: model/q15/handoff.py, model/results_handoff_term.md (run on the rebuilt Arkade empty variant, logs in notes/q15 and notes/q14/logs, not in git).

138. **2026-11-16, kNN any hit heap insertion: the cost is a chain of dependent global memory accesses made much slower by uncoalesced traffic under load; a register heap in the same program is 4.0 times faster over rounds 1 to 4 with identical results, and the model moves the same way but predicts only 2.4 to 2.5 times.** The original insertion keeps each query's 16 entry heap (128 bytes, one line per lane) in global memory and walks it with scalar loads, stores and reloads of the same entries, up to 15 steps (the heap starts at the largest float, so each of the first 16 insertions walks all of it). Fix, in a copy only (diff in notes/q15/diffs, not in git; the host copy only writes each round's heap out): load the whole heap of the lane with eight independent 16 byte loads, do the same insertion in registers with fully unrolled constant indices, store eight 16 byte vectors back. Final heaps are bit identical to the original in all five rounds (200,000 / 200,000 / 200,000 / 197,660 / 83 queries). Launch time (probes off, median of 3, CUDA events): round 1 0.661 to 0.165 ms (4.0 times), round 2 0.979 to 0.225 (4.3), round 3 3.502 to 0.692 (5.1), round 4 11.02 to 2.94 (3.75), round 5 (83 queries) 0.615 to 0.378 (1.6); rounds 1 to 4 total 16.2 to 4.0 ms. Probe cycles per any hit call, loaded: 55,000 / 36,000 / 36,000 / 23,000 to 4,600 / 2,900 / 2,900 / 2,800; one warp per SM (unloaded): 11,000 / 7,900 / 6,900 / 5,300 to 2,400 / 1,700 / 1,500 / 1,500. So the original is a dependent chain of about 15 global round trips (11,000 cycles is about 15 times two L2 latencies of 300) even alone on the SM, and under 15 warps in flight it is 5 times longer (queueing of uncoalesced requests: L1 tag requests plus local accesses per call 490 to 1,180 originally, 170 to 250 with the fix); the fix removes both, loaded over unloaded drops from 5.0 to 1.9. Registers per thread 110 to 126, warps in flight unchanged (14.2 to 15.3). Not separated by a controlled test: a variant with the chain but coalesced access (or the reverse) was not built, so the split between chain length and contention is only argued from the unloaded and loaded probe numbers. Model (callback counts re-measured by ncu for both builds, nothing refitted): predicted speedup of rounds 1 to 4 total 2.38 times with the floor of Finding 131, 2.54 with max(floor, unloaded latency), 2.44 with the hand off term added, 4.31 with the loaded latency of the new build (a consistency check, the latency is measured on the build it predicts); measured 4.02. Per round rms log error of the speedup 0.74 / 0.63 / 0.60 / 0.18. The direction is right, the size is not: the throughput floor does not see a latency chain, so it predicts only the cut in memory requests. Consequence for the paper: the callback term has to be driven by a latency measured under load (Finding 133) and cannot come from instruction counts alone for programs with dependent global memory chains. Files: model/q15/knn_fix_model.py, model/results_knn_fix_4070ti.md.

139. **2026-11-16, triton: the instance level (IAS) traversal group runs on hardware and returns the same hit, miss and back facing answers as OptiX.** One warp, one ray, one launch each, no fault, inputs intact. The first query words computed from the straight line code in front of the group (Finding 114) were accepted. Hit gives result flag 1, miss gives 0 with an all ones marker in the first result pair, back facing differs from front facing only in the sign of one result word. The driver on triton changed to 580.196.07 first; the live OptiX traversal code (single level group, instance level prelude and group) is byte identical to the old driver's, so the same bytes were used, and the driver is now on the validated list.

140. **2026-11-16, triton: TTUCCTL.IVALL (invalidate) between two sessions of one warp puts the second session back to the cost of a first session in a fresh launch, not to a flushed L2 cold start.** One warp, no fault. Median cycles for one session: second session without TTUCCTL 782 (one triangle), 867 (2,500 triangle grid), 909 (20,736); second session after TTUCCTL 1,592, 2,205, 2,544; first session of a launch with L2 warm 1,595, 2,205, 2,577; first session after a 256 MB L2 flush 2,543, 3,887, 4,638. So the invalidate removes the whole saving of the second session (the traversal unit side cache of nodes and primitives, and the setup state), and does not touch L2 (it stays about 950 to 2,100 cycles cheaper than a flushed L2). It cannot separate unit cache from L1 data cache, since a new launch also starts with those empty.

141. **2026-11-16, triton: the large base cost of an empty Arkade intersection program is the result write to pinned host memory, not the ray tracing cores, the launch size or the timer.** The build with an empty intersection program (Finding 137: 20.8 ms on the 4070 Ti against 0.04 ms predicted) takes 5.25 ms by CUDA events on triton (142 SMs). The kernel itself, from the first warp start to the last warp end by the global timer, is 4.9 ms; with the same program writing its results to a device buffer instead of pinned host memory it is 0.13 ms (the full program: 6.0 ms against 2.0 ms). So 4 to 5 ms of every run, independent of the program, is the 12.8 MB of results (100,000 queries x 16 entries x 8 bytes) leaving through PCIe (Gen4 x16) at about 2.6 to 3 GB/s, roughly a tenth of the link's rate; warps finish steadily over the whole span (median end at half the span) while their traversal results were ready early. Launch dimensions are 100,000 x 1 (3,125 warps, one wave: about 22 warps on each of the 142 SMs, all started within 0.01 ms), so occupancy is not the cause. Timer: the original timer is a host clock around the first launch call, which also synchronizes inside the call and includes one time start costs (7.4 ms against a 4.9 ms kernel); CUDA events around the same call add 0.2 to 0.9 ms and come in steps of about 1.05 ms on this machine (plain CUDA events are exact here, so this comes from the launch path). Not settled: why coalescing the stores per warp does not simply help (empty build slower, 7 to 10 ms; full build faster, 3.8 ms); the mechanism inside the pinned write path is not separated. On the 4070 Ti the same effect would need about 20 ms for 12.8 MB, 0.6 GB/s: a device buffer build there is the direct test, not run. Counters could not be read (ncu is now blocked on triton).


142. **2026-11-16, triton: TTUCCTL.IVALL also empties the L1 data cache, and the traversal unit's node cache is partly the L1 (ordinary loads that fill L1 shorten a cold traversal by 17 to 26 percent, not more).** All in one launch of one warp, ordinary loads added around the validated two session sequence, 30 launches each, no fault. (b) Ordinary data: load 8 lines per lane (a 4 byte load per lane), run a second TTU session or TTUCCTL, reload the same data and time the reload. Reload after the first loads, with or without a TTU session in between: 64 cycles (L1 hits); reload with no earlier load (L1 cold, L2 warm): 338; reload after TTUCCTL: 335 (one line per warp) and 634 against 641 cold and 553 for hits (256 lines per warp). The same loads with no TTU code in the build give the hit time, so the flush comes from TTUCCTL and not from the sessions. Loads that do not allocate in L1 always take the L2 time (335). So TTUCCTL invalidates L1 data too, which closes the open point of F140. (a) Warm the BVH with ordinary loads (all four 32 byte sectors of every 128 byte line of the whole GAS; a single 4 byte load per line first gave no effect at all, because the traversal reads whole nodes: a test flaw, corrected) and then traverse, one session timed, cycles: one triangle / 144 triangles (miss ray) / 500 / 1000 (miss ray): cold in a fresh launch with warm L2 1596 / 1263 / 1947 / 1540; after non L1 loads (warms L2 and the TLB, not L1) 1594 / 1263 / 1947 / 1547 (no effect, L2 was warm); after L1 allocating loads 1331 / 1263 / 1432 / 1295; warm second session in the same launch 782 / 679 / 827 / 721; TTUCCTL then L1 allocating loads then the second session 1332 / 983 / 1431 / 1293 (TTUCCTL alone: 1592 / 1264 / 1947 / 1540, loads that skip L1 after it: no change). With the L2 flushed by a 256 MB read just before the launch: cold 2531 / 1879 / 3240 / 2578, after non L1 warm loads 1914 / 1590 / 2287 / 1866 (so these loads recover the L2 part, 620 to 950 cycles), after L1 allocating loads 1912 / 1588 / 2273 / 1864 (same: no further gain). So: the traversal unit reads through lines that ordinary loads leave in L1 (265 to 515 cycles saved when L2 is warm), but L1 alone does not give the second session's level (782 to 827): about 300 to 600 cycles of the second session's saving need something only an earlier TTU session fills (the unit side state TTUCCTL removes). Not explained: the 144 triangle miss ray shows no gain from L1 loads before the first session (1263) but 280 after TTUCCTL (983); and the L1 gain disappears after the L2 flush kernel. Whole GAS warmed, so lines the ray does not touch also sit in L1 (the scenes are 2 to 69 KB, under L1 size). Counters could not be read (no ncu on triton), so L1 hits of the unit are inferred from time only. Code: build_patch --prewarm / --l1t / --sectors / --bypass, check_seq (l1t), setup3 --thrash-gas / --thrash-lane. Data notes/q17/.

143. **2026-11-16, mean value analysis (closed queueing network) of the loaded callback latency, from unloaded costs and warp counts only: with the floor as the only issue demand it predicts almost no queueing (it misses by the same amount as no model); the data are matched by one constant, an issue demand of about twice the floor, for RTNN, kNN and RT-BarnesHut alike (post hoc), not for Arkade.** Network per SM (model/q17/mva.py, form committed first in model/q17/FORM_mva.md): the warps in flight (ncu, 13 to 25) circulate between one queueing station for issue (demand = the floor cost per warp call of Finding 131) and a delay (everything else of the unloaded warp cycle per call: memory, traversal unit, 875 cycle hand off). Exact mean value analysis, 21 launches of Finding 133 (4070 Ti data). Version 1 (all of the rest a delay): predicts loaded latency barely above unloaded (the issue server is 2 to 17 percent busy at 14 warps): rms log error predicted over measured 0.27 RTNN (unloaded latency alone 0.30), 0.78 kNN (1.54), 0.39 RT-BarnesHut (0.47), 1.99 Arkade (2.44). Version 2 (the whole callback is one server per SM, an upper bound): 2.25 RTNN, 1.14 kNN, 2.70 BH, 0.55 Arkade. My written expectations were wrong in three places: version 1 does not fit RTNN sorted and BH under 0.2 (0.25 to 0.4 low), version 2 over predicts kNN by 3 times, not within 2. The one prediction that held: Arkade is closest to version 2. Post hoc diagnostic: the issue demand multiplier m (demand = m x floor) at which version 1 reproduces each measured loaded latency is 1.8 to 3.5 for RTNN sorted and 0 to 2.3 unsorted, 1.9 to 2.1 for all four kNN rounds, 1.8 to 1.9 for both RT-BarnesHut sizes, 4.7 to 15 for Arkade. One constant m = 2 for everything gives rms log error 0.16 over the 18 non Arkade launches (RTNN 0.19, kNN 0.04, BH 0.09) against 0.45 for version 1 and 0.45 for the post hoc k rule of Finding 133 (RTNN 0.16, BH 0.05, kNN 0.92: that rule needs a separate k per program). Chosen after seeing the data, not a test; the held out test is a different program or chip (4090 predictions can carry m = 2). What it would mean: either the floor (instruction counts times microbenchmarked costs) underestimates the issue side demand of a callback call by about two, or a second resource in proportion to the floor is shared (for example the TPC's other SM or the L1 pipe); not separated. What is not in the network because the data do not give it: per call request counts per level (L1, L2, DRAM), the traversal unit demand (two SMs share one unit, so 2 W customers there) and the hand off station as queues. Arkade: on triton with a device result buffer the same callback latency per call is 84,000 cycles against 165,000 with the pinned buffer (22 warps per SM, 142 SMs, full program, 3 runs each; summed warp time 9.1 against 25 billion cycles), so about half of the 6 to 16 times of Finding 133 came from the pinned output traffic sharing the memory system with the callbacks and the other half is still unexplained (no unloaded triton latency yet). Files: model/q17/mva.py, model/results_mva.md.

144. **2026-11-16, correction and addition to Finding 141 (Arkade base cost): the table's numbers are kernel times by the GPU global timer, the "1.05 ms steps" belong to CUDA events around the launch call (a different line), and the model's floor for the device buffer run is 0.23 low on triton.** Timer: every cell of the F141 table (4.9 / 6.0 ms pinned, 0.13 / 1.97 ms device buffer, empty and full program) is the span from the first warp start to the last warp end by %globaltimer in the raygen program, 5 runs per cell, spread under 5 percent (pinned full 6.0 to 6.4). The CUDA event times around the launch call (1.0536, 2.1033, 5.25, 6.30 ms) are 1, 2, 5 and 6 times 1.0536 ms against kernel spans of 0.13, 1.97, 4.9 and 6.0 ms: the events report the kernel span rounded up to a multiple of 1.0536 ms, so they are useless below a few ms on this machine. Model against the device buffer kernel (model/predictions_arkade_devout.md, committed before the io run; the triton column is the io model scaled by SM count, post hoc): triton full 1.57 ms predicted against 1.97 measured (log error -0.23, down from -1.95 for the pinned launch of Finding 133 on the 4070 Ti); triton empty 0.017 against 0.131 (a fixed launch and warp start cost of 0.1 ms that the model does not have); io 3.71 predicted (4.65 by scaling the triton run) and not measured. The io run (pinned against device buffer, to confirm that the 20.8 ms is the PCIe writes) is NOT done: io cannot be reached from triton without a password (not worked around); notes/q17/arkade_io.sh is ready, one command on io, validated on triton. On triton the callback latency per call of the full program falls from 165,000 cycles (pinned) to 84,000 cycles (device buffer), see Finding 143.

145. **2026-11-16, io (4070 Ti) Arkade run on GPU 0, pinned host output against a device buffer: the committed model number for the device buffer launch (3.71 ms) is right (3.70 measured), and the 20 to 24 ms base cost of Arkade is the result write to pinned host memory.** Run by Jack on io with ARK_GPU=0 (60 SMs), 5 runs per cell, kernel span by the global timer, 100,000 points and queries, radius 0.005, KN 16. Device buffer, full program: 3.700 ms median (3.642 to 3.779) against 3.71 predicted (3.667 floor plus 0.040 hand off, committed before the run, model/predictions_arkade_devout.md): log error -0.003. Scaling the triton measurement by SM count (4.65) would have been 26 percent too high. Device buffer, empty intersection program: 0.254 ms (0.250 to 0.257) against 0.04 predicted: a fixed part of about 0.21 ms is missing (triton 0.131 against 0.017). The committed failure bounds (full outside 3.0 to 5.0 ms, empty above 0.5 ms) did not trigger. Pinned host output: full 24.1 ms (23.4 to 24.6), empty 20.7 ms (20.0 to 20.8); the old values were 25.7 and 20.8 ms. So pinned minus device is 20.4 ms (full) and 20.4 ms (empty), the same for both programs; 12.8 MB in that time is about 0.6 GB/s. Callback cycles per call by the probe (f0 cycles over f0 calls, median): full 86,900 (device) against 336,700 (pinned); empty 233 (device) against 18,000 (pinned). The empty device launch is dominated by warp start: the last of 3,125 warps starts 0.20 ms after the first on io (on triton all started within 0.01 ms). CUDA events on io read 1.054 ms for the empty launch against 0.254 by the timer (rounded up as on triton, Finding 144) and are within 3 percent of the timer for the full device launch. Run history: a first run of the same script, whose GPU was not recorded, gave noisier pinned times (full 22.3 to 26.0, empty 21.3 to 29.4 ms) and device times within 1 percent of these; it is not used. The script's default is GPU 0 (ARK_GPU unset gives 0), so the cause of that noise is not known (io's GPU 0 also drives the display). Data: notes/q17/arkade_io_*.txt (the GPU 0 run).

146. **2026-11-16, Arkade's own device code has no atomics: the 84,000 cycles per call and the 0.131 ms empty program are not atomic contention.** Read the intersection program, bounds program, ray generation program and host code of Arkade (sample01 knn, in /dev/shm, third party, not in git) and counted atom and red instructions in the compiled PTX of the full and the empty builds: 9 in each, all of them our own probes (cbclk.h: two atomic adds per callback call on the warp's own slot, four atomic min or max per warp on its own slot, no address shared between warps). There is no closest hit or any hit program; the intersection program updates the query's own payload (a 16 entry list of 8 bytes, 128 bytes, reached through a pointer) and a recorder counter at the query's own index. Atomics per call from Arkade: zero. So the planned contended atomic microbenchmark (device and pinned memory) was not run, its condition was false. What the code does have is the same pattern as Finding 138: a scan of the 16 entry list for the largest distance, where each step compares against the entry found so far, then up to one update; the list lives behind the payload pointer, so it is a chain of dependent loads from memory under load. That is the leading candidate for the 84,000 cycles per call (the register heap of Finding 138 removed 4.0 times of the same cost in kNN), but it was not tested on Arkade here. The probes' own cost: the empty program is 232 cycles per call on io with two probe atomics and two clock reads, so at most that.

147. **2026-11-16, triton: a throwaway session on a different tiny BVH before the cold traversal does nothing, and with the L1 warm loads it stays far from the second session cost (1,363 against 782 cycles).** Harness: build_patch --decoy (the first session of two runs on a second one triangle GAS, hit by the same ray, with its own instance node copy; the timed second session is the target), host --decoy; stage 0 first, then one launch, then 30 launches, one warp, no fault, results equal to OptiX in every run. The instance node of OptiX is only an identity transform with no GAS pointer, and OptiX writes it at the same address for every launch, so a copy was made for the decoy. Median cycles of the timed session, one triangle / 144 triangles (ray misses) / 1,000 triangles: cold in a fresh launch (L2 warm) 1,595 / 1,263 / 1,541; after the throwaway session 1,596 / 1,263 / 1,540 (no change); L1 warm loads of the target BVH alone (all four sectors of every line) 1,331 / 1,265 / 1,294; L1 warm loads plus the throwaway session 1,363 / 1,263 / 1,546 (the 1,000 triangle case is bimodal, 1,291 to 1,551, as is the L1 only case); second session of the same BVH 782 / 679 / 721. With the L2 flushed (256 MB read before the launch): cold 2,527 / 1,879 / 2,576, after the throwaway session 2,529 / 1,879 / 2,574, L1 warm loads 1,916 / 1,589 / 1,870, L1 warm loads plus throwaway 1,917 / 1,587 / 1,872. So the code, the instruction side and the RT core state a different session warms are not part of the second session's saving; the saving needs state that only a session on the same nodes fills (the unit side state of nodes and primitives that TTUCCTL removes, Finding 140), of which L1 holds about half.

148. **2026-11-16, triton: the L2 flush gap after warm loads is the OptiX instance node line, not the TLB; the loss of the L1 gain after a flush is tied to the BVH being evicted from L2 and is still unexplained.** (a) TLB, plain CUDA probe (one thread, L2 only loads): a load that hits L2 takes 366 cycles; after touching one line in each of 2,048 or 16,384 2 MB pages (4 or 32 GB) it takes 375 or 377; after the 256 MB sweep the first load of a line (L2 miss) takes 678 and another line of the same page right after takes 672, so the sweep and the page touching cost at most about 10 cycles of translation for ordinary loads (a second run showed 758 against 672, but a control that touched the page first gave the same 751, so that is a placement effect, not the TLB). With the traversal unit: touching 16,384 pages before the launch (L2 warm) gives 1,703 (one triangle) and 1,565 (1,000 triangles) against 1,595 and 1,541 cold; with the L2 flush on top 2,653 and 2,563 against 2,527 and 2,576. Touching a buffer in the BVH's 2 MB chunk after the flush (no BVH line) gives 2,605 and 2,551: no recovery. (b) The gap after warm loads: after the flush, loading every GAS line (L2 or L1) gives 1,913 to 1,916 for one triangle, 320 above the L2 warm cold start (1,595). Adding a load of the OptiX instance node line (address A, not inside the GAS, so the earlier warm loads never touched it) gives 1,589: the gap is one DRAM miss (L2 miss minus hit in the probe is 306 cycles), the instance node line. (c) The L1 gain after the flush: with the GAS lines and the node line loaded the traversal is 1,589 whether the loads allocate in L1 or not, so the L1 gain is gone; it is also gone when the lines are first brought to L2 with non L1 loads and then to L1 with L1 loads (1,595), so it does not depend on whether the L1 fill came from DRAM. Flush size sweep, L1 warm loads of GAS and node, one triangle: 16, 64, 90 MB flush 1,338 / 1,336 / 1,336 (gain kept, cold control 1,589 to 1,601, BVH still in L2), 110 and 160 MB 1,589 / 1,589 (gain gone, cold control 2,532 and 2,546, BVH evicted). The gain is lost exactly when the flush exceeds the L2 size (96 MB), so what is lost is something tied to lines that were evicted and refilled, not translation and not the L1 fill path. Not explained, and not tested: which property of a refilled L2 line differs (partition or slice placement, sector state). Also not explained: with an intermediate kernel that only touches 16,384 pages and the L2 warm, the L1 plus node warm traversal is 1,136, 195 cycles below the 1,331 without it. Data and builds: notes/q18/.

149. **2026-11-16, mean value analysis with a traversal unit station and a hand off station, frozen first and tested on the register heap kNN and on Arkade with a device buffer: the two added stations change nothing material, the issue queue alone does the work, kNN is under predicted in all eight launches (error -0.28 to -0.74) and Arkade is close only because its floor is huge.** Form committed first (model/q18/FORM_mva2.md), predictions committed before the comparison (model/predictions_mva.md, f8cb260), no constant changed afterwards. Stations per TPC (two SMs): issue per SM with demand f = the floor of Finding 131 (multiplier 1, not the post hoc 2 of Finding 143); the traversal unit per TPC with demand 32 x lane efficiency / 0.14 lanes per cycle (the saturated rate of short coherent queries with both SMs of a TPC, Finding 105: 98 to 170 cycles per warp call); the hand off path per SM with demand 129 cycles per warp call, which is the highest call rate any non target launch (RTNN, RT-BarnesHut) reached, an upper bound of capacity because the hand off path has no saturation measurement of its own (BVH_3 is a one warp latency, 875 cycles); everything else a delay (trace window cycles per call of the one warp per SM launch minus the stations). Bard Schweitzer approximation, two chains, continuous warps in flight. Predicted: loaded callback latency per call and launch time from throughput, no fixed term, no miss term, 2.5 GHz. Error = log(predicted / measured). Register heap kNN rounds 1 to 4 (4070 Ti): loaded latency -0.57 / -0.43 / -0.36 / -0.38 (issue queue only: -0.57 / -0.43 / -0.34 / -0.28), launch time -0.75 / -0.53 / -0.38 / -0.21 (issue only -0.76 / -0.54 / -0.39 / -0.30); rms 0.44 and 0.51 against 0.42 and 0.53 without the added stations. Original kNN: latency -0.64 to -0.74, launch time -0.66 to -0.95 (rms 0.71 and 0.82). Arkade device buffer, full program: io latency -0.13, launch time +0.02 (3.76 predicted against 3.70 ms); triton latency -0.10, launch time -0.21 (1.59 against 1.96 ms; its floor and unloaded values are io's per call values). Utilisation of the traversal unit station was 0.31 / 0.38 / 0.56 / 0.85 in rounds 1 to 4 of the register heap, so it did become busy in the large rounds, and it moved the round 4 latency down by 0.10 and the launch time up by 0.09, no net gain; the hand off station stayed under 0.32. The speedup of the register heap over the original is predicted 3.3 / 3.2 / 3.4 / 2.4 against 4.0 / 4.3 / 5.1 / 3.75 measured, the right order but 20 to 40 percent low. Written expectations: wrong on Arkade (I expected 3 times too low; the issue station at demand f is saturated at 97 percent, so the network just reproduces the floor of Finding 131, which is also why Arkade looks right: it is not a test of the stations); wrong on the original kNN magnitude (expected 3 times low, it is 2 times); the traversal unit became the busy station in rounds 3 and 4 as expected but without effect; the written failure rule (any register heap latency error beyond 0.5) fired in round 1. Reading: the saturated rates give small demands next to the issue floor, so the stations are idle or nearly so and cannot supply the missing 20 to 50 percent of the kNN latency; a missing term must be memory system queueing of the dependent loads, which none of these stations models. Honest status of the test: the targets were measured before the form was written, the guard is only that nothing was tuned. Files: model/q18/mva2.py, model/results_mva2.md.

150. **2026-11-16, triton: the second session saving of a traversal is flat (about 60 percent of the cold time) up to a BVH of about 100 KB, then falls and is gone at about 260 to 520 KB, well above both the L1 data size (about 90 KB for a cyclic scan) and the 475 line (60 KB) fit of Finding 123; TTUCCTL removes it at every size.** One warp, one block, one launch process, 30 launches, stage 0 first, then one stage 1 launch, then 30; every run exit 0, no fault, GPU 1 idle. Scene par:N:s (a +z ray that enters every box and hits nothing, so one ray's working set is the whole BVH: nodes plus triangle data), N from 4 to 65,536 triangles; timed session in a fresh launch with a warm L2 (single), the second session of the same ray (twice), and a second session after TTUCCTL.IVALL (cctl). Size = the GAS bytes of the OptiX build (nodes fetched by the ray from the CPU replay: 1 at N 4, 33 at N 1,024, 140 at N 4,096, 335 at N 8,192). Saved fraction (1 minus second over first): 0.53 to 0.64 from 18 lines to 1,059 lines (2 KB to 132 KB) with no trend (0.62 at 68 KB, 0.54 at 132 KB); then 0.39 at 164 KB, 0.23 at 199 KB, 0.08 at 231 KB, 0.23 at 263 KB (the BVH shape changes with N, so it is not monotone), 0.18 / 0.13 / 0.05 at 327 / 394 / 458 KB, 0.04 at 525 KB, 0.03 at 1 MB, 0.00 at 4 MB. In cycles: 1,024 triangles 100,590 against 38,229; 2,048 176,427 against 81,418; 4,096 370,750 against 285,521; 8,192 791,081 against 761,133. TTUCCTL: cctl over single is 0.999 to 1.007 at all 22 sizes, so the saving is removed at every size, also the small saving left at large sizes. A plain CUDA probe (notes/q19/l1_probe.cu, one thread, all four sectors of every 128 byte line, cyclic, L1 allocating loads) gives 63 cycles per line up to 80 KB, 167 at 96 KB and L2 time (327) from 112 KB, so the L1 data capacity for a cyclic scan in this launch is 80 to 96 KB (the L1 is a sharp cliff; the loads that skip L1 are 311). So the saving does not disappear at the L1 size: it starts to fall just above it (about 100 to 120 KB) but a quarter of it is left at 200 to 260 KB and a tenth at 400 KB, as if the traversal order (nearest first, so upper nodes are reused and the scan is not strictly cyclic) or a second store beyond L1 kept part of the working set; not separated here. It is also far from the 475 lines of Finding 123: the saving is unchanged at 545 to 942 lines of GAS, so that fit (random rays, reuse distance in node fetches of many rays) does not describe this capacity; the two measure different things (the fit is a miss probability against reuse distance per node fetch with lanes merged, this is a one ray cyclic scan of all lines). Multi warp attempt, not used: with 3 or more warps in the block (32 distinct rays per warp on grid scenes) the second session window comes out as long or longer than the first (twice over single 1.0 at 3 and 4 warps, 1.4 at 8 warps, 3.3 at 12 warps on 1,000 triangles) and cctl is no better, because the warps queue on the shared unit and are not aligned (no barrier between the sessions); that is a queueing effect, not a capacity measurement, and the node working set of those launches was only 6 to 115 nodes. Limits: the size includes triangle data and the header, so the line counts are upper bounds on lines fetched; only one SM and one warp; the unit side state (Finding 140) and the L1 cannot be told apart in the saving; L2 warm, not flushed. Data and scripts: notes/q19/.

151. **2026-11-16, triton, Arkade with a device buffer: the distance function in double precision is the whole cost (1.97 ms against 0.18 ms with a float distance, same neighbours); the register heap changes nothing, and the launch's fixed 0.13 ms is 0.01 ms warp start plus 0.08 ms of result writes plus 0.04 ms of traversal.** All numbers are the GPU global timer span of the kernel (first warp start to last warp end), 100,000 points and queries, radius 0.005, 5 runs per cell, device buffer (DEVOUT), GPU 1 idle, Arkade is third party code kept in /dev/shm (our changes are only in a diff in notes/q19). (a) Register heap (the 16 entry list of 8 bytes is loaded into registers with independent loads, the scan of the largest distance runs on constant indices, only the replaced entry is stored; same strict greater than rule): the 12,800,000 output bytes are identical to the original; time 1.97 against 1.97 ms (span medians 1.980 and 1.985), callback cycles per call 83,700 against 83,100. The leading candidate of Finding 146 (the list scan behind the payload pointer) is wrong for Arkade; the same pattern that gave 4.0 times in kNN costs nothing here. (c) Where the time goes: returning right after the sphere load (level 3) 0.137 ms (empty program 0.128); returning right after the distance (level 1) 1.96 ms; so everything is in the distance computation, which for the L2 norm is std::pow(abs(float), int) in double precision (NORM 2), at 1 over 64 of the FP32 rate on this chip. Removing the per call probes (clock reads and two atomics), the candidate counter, or both changes nothing (1.96, 1.97, 1.96 ms). Distance as float pow (powf) 0.189 ms and as a float square 0.182 ms; neighbours: 0 of 1,600,000 entries differ in index, largest relative distance difference 1.2e-7. So 10.8 times faster for the same answer (and at 3,200 queries 0.33 against 0.057 ms, at 32 queries 0.176 against 0.041 ms). This reverses Finding 136, whose float test used the pinned output (24 ms of which 20 ms is the write, Finding 145), and confirms Finding 132's explanation that was withdrawn there; the callback floor of Finding 131 (which included double precision at 19 cycles per instruction) was right about what dominates. (b) Zero ray launches (3,125 warps, one wave): no traceRay and no result writes 0.0092 to 0.0102 ms; no traceRay but the result writes 0.087 ms (12.8 MB, 150 GB/s of a 960 GB/s peak: short, latency bound stores); traceRay with the empty intersection program 0.128 ms; so the fixed part is 0.010 launch and warp start, 0.077 writes, 0.041 traversal with calls that do nothing. At 3,200 queries (100 warps): no trace 0.000 to 0.001, writes 0.003, empty 0.032; at 32 queries (one warp) empty 0.025 ms (one warp's traversal latency of the empty program, about 59,000 cycles at 2.4 GHz: a cold BVH walk through L2 and DRAM, not a throughput cost). The io run of the same script (notes/q17/arkade_io2.sh, device buffer only) is for Jack to run: it tests whether io's 0.254 ms empty launch (against 0.04 predicted, Finding 145) has the same parts, and whether the float distance gives the same 10 times there (prediction: the full program 3.70 ms falls to 0.3 to 0.4 ms, because the floor of Finding 131 without double precision instructions is about a tenth). Limits: triton only; one dataset (a1e5) and one radius; the NORM 2 case only; the float square is only the same answer to 1.2e-7 for this data; no ncu on triton. Data: notes/q19/arkade_triton_*.txt.

152. **2026-11-16, mean value analysis with separate issue, L1, L2 and DRAM stations (the last try): the kill rule fired (launch time error rms 0.88 over the 8 kNN launches, limit 0.3); MVA is dropped as a model of the callback side.** Form, kill rule and constants were committed first (model/q19/FORM_mva3.md), the predictions before the comparison (model/predictions_mva3.md, cf88bcb), nothing changed afterwards. Stations per SM: issue (instructions x 0.25), L1 (callback tag requests and local accesses x 1.00 plus the RT unit's node fetches from the CPU replay, 1 tag each, lane sharing 1.1), L2 (callback L1 misses x 1.90 plus node fetch misses, 4 sectors each, replay share beyond 480 lines), DRAM (launch L2 misses from the same ncu run x 9.52 cycles, the 504 GB/s specification shared over 60 SMs); the traversal unit and hand off stations of Finding 149 unchanged. The per call load counts are the ncu source counts of queue 13, not BITTU: BITTU does not instrument OptiX programs (Finding 118). Result, log error of the launch time (predicted over measured), register heap build rounds 1 to 4: -0.55 / -0.49 / -0.48 / -0.26; original build -1.27 / -1.16 / -1.16 / -1.03; rms 0.880. The same inputs with the single station of Finding 149: rms 0.681 (so the split made it worse, not better). Loaded callback latency rms 0.846 (split) against 0.589 (single). Speedup of the register heap over the original, rounds 1 to 4: measured 4.0 / 4.3 / 5.1 / 3.75, split 1.9 / 2.2 / 2.5 / 1.7, single station 3.3 / 3.2 / 3.4 / 2.4. Written expectations: the kill rule would fire (right); the result within 10 percent of Finding 149 (wrong: the split lowered the original build's launch time by a further 30 to 40 percent, because splitting one saturated server into four in series cuts the queue at each); node fetches small next to the callback's loads (wrong in rounds 1 and 2: 73 and 35 fetches per call against 366 and 243 L1 demand, because those rounds have few callback calls per query); DRAM demand small (wrong in round 1 and 2, 742 and 290 cycles per call, because the launch level L2 misses are divided over very few calls). Reading: the missing 20 to 70 percent is not capacity at L1, L2 or DRAM as these stations describe them; the original build's loaded cost (a chain of dependent loads, Finding 138) is a latency chain that a capacity station network cannot represent unless it carries the chain as a delay that grows with load, which would be a fitted term. Decision: MVA is not used in the paper's model; Findings 143, 149 and this one are reported as a negative result: a closed network with stations built from saturation throughputs does not reproduce loaded callback latency or launch time. Files: model/q19/mva3.py, model/results_mva3.md.

153. **2026-11-16, open items (as of the end of queue 19; none is being worked on).** From Finding 148: (a) which property of an L2 line that was evicted and refilled makes the L1 gain disappear (placement in a slice or partition, or sector state), after the L2 flush exceeds the L2 size (96 MB); (b) why an intermediate kernel that only touches 16,384 pages with the L2 warm lowers the L1 plus node warm traversal by 195 cycles (1,136 against 1,331); (c) whether the instance node line is also the missing piece in the second session (a controlled test of that line alone in the second session). From Finding 142 and 147: (d) why the 144 triangle miss ray shows no gain from L1 warm loads before the first session but 280 cycles after TTUCCTL; (e) what the unit side state is that only a session on the same nodes fills, and which store beyond the L1 keeps part of it (Finding 150: the saving falls above the L1 size but a quarter is left at 200 to 260 KB). From Finding 150: a clean multi warp capacity test needs a barrier between the two sessions (the unit queues warps), a change to the harness. From Finding 151: the io run of the zero ray and float distance tests (notes/q17/arkade_io2.sh) is not done; whether the float distance speeds up the full program on io by the same 10 times; why a launch of one empty warp takes 0.025 ms. From earlier: ncu on triton (RmProfilingAdminOnly 1), tethys, IAS random rays and many warps, TTUCCTL in the IAS path.
