154. **2026-11-16, io (4070 Ti), Arkade with a device buffer: the double precision distance is the whole cost here too (3.70 ms against 0.36 ms with a float square, 10 times), the register heap changes nothing (3.74 ms), and the neighbours are identical.** Run by Jack on io with notes/q17/arkade_io2.sh (GPU 0, device buffer, 100,000 points and queries, radius 0.005, 5 runs per cell, GPU global timer span, medians; data notes/q19/arkade_io_*.txt). Variants in one line each: lvl1 returns right after the distance (3.73 ms, so the cost is in the distance), lvl2 returns after the list scan (3.72), lvl3 returns right after the sphere load (0.27), empty is the empty intersection program (0.25); np removes the per call clock probes (3.73), nc removes the candidate counter (3.72), npnc both (3.74), npncf both plus the float square (0.34); nt1 has no traceRay but keeps the result writes (0.15), nt2 neither (0.017). Smaller launches: 3,200 queries base 0.32, float square 0.054, empty 0.030; 32 queries 0.176, 0.042, 0.026. Triton (F151) 1.97 / 1.97 / 0.18 / 0.19 ms; io is 1.9 times slower on the original (60 SMs against 142, double precision at the same 1/64 rate) and 2.0 times slower on the float builds. 155. **2026-11-16, triton, sibling L1 test: a plain block resident on the TPC sibling SM removes about two thirds of the second session saving, but NOT through its thrash: zero passes, 4 passes and 16 passes, with fast or slow flag polling, give the same second session time; the same block on an SM of another TPC changes nothing.** Harness (new, all non TTU parts checked in stage 0 first, check_seq ALL PASS in both stages; build_patch --sibling, setup3 --sibling SM:KB:PASSES[:POLLNS], check_seq B15 sibling rule): one TTU warp (one block, smfilter, SM 0 by the placement rule, probe_placement 0 mismatches); after its first session closes it stores flag1 and spins on flag2 (LDG STRONG.SYS loop, one backward branch outside any session); a plain CUDA block (384 threads) on the target SM waits for flag1, then streams ordinary cached loads (ld.ca) over its own 4 MB buffer PASSES times, then sets flag2; the TTU warp then runs the timed second session. Both are co-resident (the plain block is launched first and the host waits until it is resident). Scene par:N:s, N 1,024 / 2,048 / 3,072 / 4,096 = 68 / 132 / 199 / 263 KB of GAS, 5 repetitions x 30 launches per cell in rotating order, medians, GPU 1 idle, every run exit 0, exactly one TTU block ran in every launch, no sibling timeout. Cycles of the timed session, single (fresh launch, no second session) / twice (no flags) / flag0 (block on SM 1, 0 passes) / sib4 (SM 1, 4 passes) / ctl4 (SM 20, 4 passes) / sib16 / ctl16 / flag0p, sib4p, ctl4p (same with the waiting block polling every 5 us): 68 KB 100,740 / 38,231 / 73,951 / 74,704 / 39,311 / 74,098 / 39,087 / 75,008, 75,012, 39,180; 132 KB 176,397 / 81,014 / 135,821 / 136,113 / 77,623 / 135,924 / 77,589 / 135,880, 135,865, 77,658; 199 KB 309,155 / 237,880 / 281,912 / 281,197 / 237,481 / 281,668 / 237,059 / 281,279, 281,778, 237,674; 263 KB 370,448 / 285,323 / 344,388 / 344,235 / 284,929 / 343,912 / 284,405 / 344,856, 344,585, 285,713. Saved fraction (1 minus second over single): no sibling or another TPC 0.62 / 0.54 / 0.23 / 0.23 (the F150 values), sibling SM 0.26 / 0.23 / 0.09 / 0.07 for every thrash volume and polling rate. So the sibling does remove part of the saving where the control does not, but the cause is the presence of the sibling block (a waiting block, a handshake, or its traffic during the first session), not the volume of its cached loads: thrash adds nothing. What the data cannot separate: effect during the first session (the waiting block's polling or its start) against between the sessions; the control block does the same handshake on another TPC, so the handshake itself is not the cause. What remains after the sibling (0.26 / 0.23) is about 40 percent of the saving at 68 and 132 KB, and 30 to 40 percent at 199 and 263 KB. Placement side result (plain CUDA, then TTU harness): a TTU block never lands on an SM whose TPC sibling holds a 1,024 thread block of another kernel (no TTU block ran in 142 or 284 block launches); with the sibling block at 384 threads or fewer it lands normally; the first test of the harness with a 1,024 thread plain block therefore ran no warp and was discarded. Node cache capacity curve (F150) is confirmed as a per SM or per TPC effect only in the sense above: the saving is a state that a block on the sibling SM disturbs without touching memory heavily. Data notes/q20/sib_matrix.json, script notes/q20/sib_matrix.py. (Interpretation corrected by Finding 157: the cause is the fence in the sibling block, not its presence.)

156. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, TrueKNN predicted first (calibration on 100,000 points only, no refitting): rounds and active sets right, total launch time 0.53 times (200,000 points) and 0.85 times (400,000 points) the measured value, per round errors up to a factor 4; the callback cost of one program is NOT one number.** Program: vani-nag/OWLRayTracing s01-trueknn (Apache 2.0 file, README adds a non commercial notice: ambiguous), third party code kept in /dev/shm/tk, built on the newer OWL of the Arkade tree because its own OWL fails on CUDA 12.8 and OptiX 8 (INVALID_PTX); our changes: frame buffer in device memory (the original uses managed memory read by the host every round), refit flags, clock64 probes in the intersection program (Finding 133 type) and a global timer span per launch; one launch per round, uniform random 3D points in the unit cube, k = 16, start radius 0.004, radius doubling, refit between rounds. Constants from the 100,000 point set A (5 runs, identical to 1 percent): hardware warp calls over the exact warp maximum box hit count 1.031, 14,815 callback cycles per warp call pooled over the rounds, total over callback warp cycles 1.287, 12.0 warps in flight per SM. Committed first (model/predictions_trueknn.md, e2f4b00), then measured (model/results_trueknn.md): 200,000 points predicted 2.62 ms against 4.96 (log error -0.64; rounds 1 to 5: -1.46 / -0.52 / -0.79 / -0.52 / -1.31), 400,000 points 8.84 against 10.39 (-0.16; -1.26 / -0.78 / -0.80 / +0.11). Rounds: 5 and 4 predicted, 5 and 4 measured. Active queries per round: 200,000 / 200,000 / 200,000 / 199,790 / 164 predicted against 200,000 / 200,000 / 200,000 / 199,649 / 132; 400,000 case round 4 307,525 against 284,121 (-8 percent): the exact box rule is close, as F132 said. Warp calls are under predicted by a factor 1.28 to 1.47 in every round of both sets (A was fitted to 1.031 of the exact count, but the ratio grows with the set size; the hardware leaf slot overreach is not constant). The cost per call measured in the held out runs is 60,600 / 24,200 / 35,000 / 26,400 / 2,310 cycles (200,000 points) and 48,100 / 32,600 / 35,600 / 14,000 (400,000): it differs 4 to 26 times between rounds, so a per program pooled number (14,815) makes the cheap rounds (many warps, few calls, short latency) and the last small round wrong; the total looks better than the rounds only because the big rounds dominate and the errors have opposite signs for the 400,000 set. Success rule fixed before (total within a factor 1.5 and equal number of rounds): passed for 400,000 points (1.18), failed for 200,000 (1.89). Reading: the callback cost has to be measured per round regime (per call latency depends on how many warps are in flight and on the queue behind the heap, Findings 133 and 138), not once per program; with one number this model ranks the rounds right but the absolute error is 0.5 to 2 times. A fourth set was not run.

157. **2026-11-16, triton, sibling fence test: a plain block on the TPC sibling SM that only waits (on the global timer, or by polling with relaxed loads, no fence) leaves the second session saving intact; one fence.acq_rel.gpu in that block removes about two thirds of it, exactly like the F155 block. So F155's "presence of the sibling block" was wrong: the cause is the memory fence, which on this chip compiles to a memory barrier plus an L1 data cache invalidate (CCTL.IVALL) of the SM that runs it.** Same F155 setup (one TTU warp on SM 0, two sessions of the same ray, scene par:N:s at 68 KB (N 1,024) and 132 KB (N 2,048) of GAS, 5 repetitions x 30 launches per cell in rotating order, medians, GPU 1 idle, stage 0 first then one stage 1 launch per arm, every run exit 0, exactly one TTU block per launch, no sibling timeout; the TTU kernel, one build, is the same in all arms). The TTU warp's own barrier, read from the compiled kernel: after the first session it stores flag1 (a strong system scope store), then spins on flag2 with a strong system scope load, a compare and one backward branch; no memory barrier and no cache invalidate anywhere in the TTU kernel. New plain kernel (setup3 --sibling ...:MODE, a block of 384 threads, only thread 0 acts, one block barrier at the start and end as in F155): (a) waits 8 ms on the global timer with no loads or stores, then stores flag2; (b) polls flag1 with relaxed gpu scope loads and stores flag2 relaxed, no fence; (c) as (b) plus exactly one acquire release gpu scope fence before the flag2 store; (d) the F155 kernel (0 passes, and 4 passes), which contains system scope fences after its ready store, after its flag2 store and a gpu scope fence at the end. Compiled: (c) fence = MEMBAR.ALL.GPU, ERRBAR, CCTL.IVALL; (d) three such triples (SC.SYS, SC.GPU, SC.SYS); (a) and (b) have none (their only other memory operations are the start ticket atomic and the ready store, before the TTU launch, identical in all arms). Saved fraction (1 minus second session over single) at 68 / 132 KB: no sibling 0.62 / 0.54; (a) sibling SM 0.61 / 0.49, control SM 0.61 / 0.52; (b) 0.61 / 0.49 and 0.61 / 0.50; (c) 0.27 / 0.22 against control SM 0.61 / 0.54; (d) 0.27 / 0.23 (4 passes 0.26 / 0.23) against control 0.61 / 0.56. Cycles of the timed session at 68 KB: single 100,695, no sibling 38,228, (a) 39,411, (b) 39,359, (c) 73,914, (d) 73,940. In (a) the TTU launch took 5.9 ms by events (it waited for the timer block), so the sibling block was resident through the first session and the whole gap and the long gap on a control SM costs nothing. Cases that lose the saving: (c) and (d); not (a) and (b). At 132 KB (a) and (b) are a little below no sibling (0.49 to 0.52 against 0.54, inside the spread of the five repetition medians 78,000 to 92,700 cycles; not separated). Not separated: whether the effect needs the invalidate or the barrier part of the fence, whether a fence before the first session (in (d) only) adds anything, and why a fence on the sibling SM, which has its own L1, reaches the TTU SM (shared traversal unit side state of the TPC is the candidate). Data notes/q20/sib_fence.log, sib_matrix.json.

158. **2026-11-16, triton, TrueKNN SECOND ATTEMPT (F156 stays as reported): a rule built from the 100,000 point set alone, launch time of a round proportional to the candidates (box hits) the intersection program sees, fixes rounds 1 to 3 (log error -0.63 to +0.21, F156 -1.46 to -0.52) but over predicts the heavy round 4 by 1.9 times (200,000) and 3.0 times (400,000), so the totals are 1.56 times and 2.27 times the measured (log error +0.44 and +0.82; F156 was -0.64 and -0.16). Not better than the first attempt overall.** What was tried on A (100,000 points, 5 runs, per round callback probe): callback cycles per warp call across rounds 1 to 5 are 72,800 / 15,000 / 30,600 / 36,300 / 2,800. Three one constant rules compared in sample on the per round launch time of A (constant = A total over feature total): warp calls (F156's feature) rms log error 1.00, expected heap insertions (n up to 16, then 16 (1 + ln(n / 16)), random order) 0.60, candidates 0.38, so candidates was chosen before any prediction. The rule: T = 6.3e-7 ms x candidates = 224 SM cycles per candidate; candidates = sum over active queries of the exact Chebyshev box count including self (CPU, as F156), active rule and stop rule unchanged. Facts behind it: in rounds 1 to 4 of A about 11 to 12 callbacks per SM are in flight (callback cycles over SM times span), in round 5 4.2 (few active lanes), so the cost per call is mostly queueing behind the others; the SM time per call grows with the active lanes per call (11.5, 13, 17 lanes in rounds 2, 3, 4; 2.2 in round 5), about 160 to 300 cycles per candidate, round 1 (first launch, cold) 450. Radius itself, heap fill and active warps were not separable on five points (active warps are 3,125 in rounds 1 to 4). Committed first (model/predictions_trueknn2.md, 7ede120; the point files of F156 were gone, so features use regenerated uniform points, round 1 calls 5,792 against F156's 5,879; one raw 200,000 point run file had been printed before the rule was chosen, and the F156 tables were known, so held out only in the sense that no number from them entered the rule), then compared (model/results_trueknn2.md). Per round log error (predicted over measured): 200,000 points rounds 1 to 5 -0.63 / +0.13 / +0.11 / +0.67 / -3.26; 400,000 points rounds 1 to 4 -0.51 / -0.08 / +0.21 / +1.10; rounds predicted 5 and 5 (measured 5), and 5 against 4 (round 5 had one query predicted, the measured set ended after round 4). What failed: (1) round 4 at larger sizes: measured cost per candidate falls from 0.53 ns (A, 26 candidates per query) to 0.32 (200,000, 51 per query) and 0.21 ns (400,000, 99 per query), so a per call fixed part exists that A cannot show (its candidates per call lie between 11 and 17 except the 2.2 of round 5); a rule with calls and candidates as two terms would be a third attempt, not done; (2) tail rounds: 132 queries left take 0.37 ms against 0.014 predicted, a lone warp chain of about 130 candidates per query is latency bound (about 2.8 microseconds per candidate), not throughput bound, which no throughput rule gives. The success rule set before (total within 1.5 times, rounds equal) was met for neither set (1.56 and 2.27 times; F156 met it for the 400,000 set only).
159. **2026-11-16, triton, fence decomposition between the two sessions (predictions committed first, aaabc8c): the loss of the second session saving is a property of the gpu scope fence (memory barrier, error barrier and an L1 invalidate), not of the barrier alone, and it adds up over the two SMs of the TPC: a gpu fence on the TTU's own SM removes about 58 percent of the saving, the same as one on the sibling SM, both together remove all of it.** Same setup as F157 (one TTU warp on SM 0, two sessions of the same ray, scene par:N:s at 68 KB (N 1,024) and 132 KB (N 2,048), 5 repetitions x 30 launches per cell in rotating order, medians, stage 0 check first, every run exit 0, exactly one TTU block per launch, no timeout). Saved fraction (1 minus second session over single session) at 68 / 132 KB: no fence 0.62 / 0.54 (sibling polling without a fence 0.61 / 0.50, control SM 0.61 / 0.50); (e) fence.acq_rel.cta on the sibling 0.61 / 0.49 (predicted no loss: held); gpu scope fence on the sibling 0.26 / 0.23 (F157 value reproduced); (f) gpu scope fence on the TTU's own SM only 0.26 / 0.25 (predicted about half, 0.31 / 0.27 with range 0.22 to 0.40 / 0.19 to 0.35: held); (g) gpu scope fences on both SMs 0.00 / 0.00, the second session as slow as a fresh first session (100,104 against 100,429 cycles at 68 KB; 175,791 against 176,148 at 132 KB; predicted at most 0.12 / 0.10: held, and in every repetition). All three predictions fell inside their ranges. Compiled forms (read from the SASS): the cta scope fence is a single memory barrier at cta scope; the gpu scope fence is a gpu scope memory barrier, an error barrier and an L1 invalidate (CCTL.IVALL). So a barrier at cta scope costs nothing, and the effect needs the gpu scope form; not separated: gpu barrier against the L1 invalidate (no PTX for a bare invalidate). Reading under the hypothesis of Jack (node lines spread over both L1s of the TPC): fits, each fence removes about 58 percent and both remove everything (the two losses overlap only slightly). Alternatives not excluded: two separate pieces of per SM state, not both L1; and a change of who executes the fence: (f) and (g) use a fence executed by the TTU warp itself (new build option, three plain instructions between the sessions, outside any session, checked by check_seq), because a plain block placed first on the TTU's SM keeps the TTU block off that SM (0 of 3 launches ran, also with 142 blocks), while the same block on the sibling SM lets it run. (h) TTUCCTL alone on the sibling was NOT run: it needs a second TTU block executing a TTUCCTL outside any session, a sequence type that is not validated. Files model/predictions_fence.md, model/results_fence.md; data notes/q21/fence2_stage1_20261116T133352.json (private).

Constants from A: warp calls over exact warp max box count 1.044, warp cycles over callback cycles 1.485, 12.8 warps in flight per SM. Model of F156 with its pooled callback cost replaced by the measured cost per call of each round (cycles per call at 200,000 points: 72,600 / 47,000 / 37,600 / 26,800 / 2,580; at 400,000: 70,900 / 40,100 / 35,600 / 13,900 / 4,010). Round 5 (142 and 1 active queries) measured 0.55 and 0.60 ms against 0.02 and 0.0002 predicted: a latency bound chain, not throughput (log error -3.3 and -8.2), which dominates the per round rms (1.5 and 3.7). Why the totals look better than the model is: hardware warp calls are again under predicted by 1.3 to 1.4 times in the big rounds (17,986 against 13,822 predicted at round 1 of 200,000; 2,285,571 against 1,608,174 at round 4 of 400,000), and the warps in flight per SM are 15.7 to 17.9 in the held sizes against 12.8 pooled from A; with the measured call count the same model is 1.3 to 1.5 times too high (+0.28 and +0.41). The two errors cancel. Active sets: round 4 predicted 199,766 and 307,795 against 199,604 and 284,365 measured (400,000: +8 percent, as F156). Rounds 5 and 5 predicted and measured. Rebuild check on A against the F156 build (medians of 5): rounds 4 and 5 span equal within 6 percent (1.447 and 0.757 ms against 1.384 and 0.680), rounds 1 to 3 are 1.4 to 3.3 times slower in the rebuild (0.222 / 0.215 / 0.373 against 0.131 / 0.066 / 0.266 ms; callback cycles per call round 2 51,600 against 15,000), cause not determined (refit build flags, points and call order differ). So the cheap rounds depend on the build, not only on the program; it also means F156 and F158 were measured on a build that is not this one. Checked on 2026-11-16 without a GPU run: the work is the same (callback calls in rounds 1 to 5 equal within 2 percent between the builds, active sets equal); only the callback cycles per call differ, in rounds 1 to 3 (round 2 by 3.5 times) and not in rounds 4 and 5; the run cadence was the same in both (runs about half a second apart, same GPU), so a cold or warm GPU state does not explain it; the third party build directory of F156 and its patches were never saved and are gone, so no source or binary comparison is possible; the F156 and rebuild point files differ (seeds), same distribution. What would separate the causes: rebuilds that change one thing at a time (refit build flags, frame buffer placement, point order) on the new build. 161. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, TTUCCTL.IVALL executed alone by the TPC sibling SM removes the whole second session saving (prediction missed): saved fraction 0.00 at 68 KB and 0.00 at 132 KB, against 0.62 and 0.54 with nothing between the sessions and 0.26 and 0.23 for a gpu scope fence on the sibling.** Setup as F157 and F159 (one TTU warp on SM 0, two sessions of the same ray, scenes of 68 and 132 KB, 5 repetitions of 30 launches per cell, medians, rotating arm order). New: between the sessions a second block on the sibling SM (SM 1) executes exactly one TTUCCTL.IVALL and nothing else (no session on that block, no memory barrier), then tells the TTU warp it is done through a flag. Cycles of the second session: 100,370 (68 KB) and 176,191 (132 KB) against 100,545 and 176,043 for a single session, so the second session is as slow as a fresh first one; the sibling that only polls (no TTUCCTL) keeps 0.61 and 0.49. Every run exit 0, exactly one block with a session per launch, no timeout, no fault. The prediction (committed first, 966b726) was 0.27 and 0.24 (accept 0.15 to 0.40 and 0.13 to 0.36), the same as the sibling gpu fence; it was wrong, and the outcome the rule set before the run assigned to this result is "the effect of TTUCCTL is TPC wide". What this shows: a bare TTUCCTL from the other SM, without a memory barrier, removes everything, while a gpu scope fence (barrier, error barrier, L1 invalidate) on one SM removes about 55 percent and needs both SMs to remove everything (F159). So the unit's own cache control reaches state that the L1 invalidate of a fence does not: a reading that fits all of F142, F150, F157, F159 and this is two layers, the L1 of each SM (cleared per SM by the fence) and node state of the traversal unit that is shared by the TPC (cleared by TTUCCTL from either SM). That reading is a hypothesis, not separated by this test: it does not say whether the shared state is in the unit, in a TPC level cache, or in both L1s reached through the unit. Not tested: TTUCCTL on the TTU's own SM between the sessions was F142's case in a different build; a TTUCCTL executed by a warp that has no session is a new sequence type that ran without fault here (one warp, 150 launches per cell). Files model/predictions_fence_h.md, model/results_fence_h.md; harness build_patch --sibccctl, setup3 --sibling ...:5, check_seq rules; data notes/q22 (private). Mistake found on the way: the first stage 0 build used a forward branch with the sign extension bits of a backward branch (illegal instruction before any TTU code); fixed (bra_fwd) and a check_seq rule added.
162. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, TTUCCTL.IVALL executed by the TTU warp itself between the two sessions removes the whole second session saving, the same as on the sibling SM (prediction held): saved fraction 0.00 at 68 KB and 0.00 at 132 KB, against 0.62 and 0.54 with nothing between the sessions and 0.61 and 0.50 with a sibling that only polls.** Setup as F157, F159 and F161 (one TTU warp on SM 0, two sessions of the same ray, scenes of 68 and 132 KB, 5 repetitions of 30 launches per cell, medians, rotating arm order), in the sibling test build with one new option: after its first session the TTU warp executes exactly one TTUCCTL.IVALL (outside any session, no memory barrier), then spins on the flag as before; the plain block on SM 1 only polls. Cycles of the second session 100,344 (68 KB) and 176,159 (132 KB) against 100,556 and 176,072 for a single session, so the second session is as slow as a fresh first one. Every run exit 0, exactly one block with a session per launch, no timeout, no fault. The prediction (committed first, 7c16a38) was 0.00 and 0.00 (accept at most 0.10 and 0.08): held. What this adds to F161: TTUCCTL removes the whole saving whichever SM of the TPC issues it, so its effect is symmetric over the two SMs, while the gpu scope fence of F159 removes about 55 percent from either SM and needs both. The two layer reading of F161 (L1 per SM, cleared per SM by the fence; node state of the traversal unit shared by the TPC, cleared by TTUCCTL from either SM) is unchanged and still a hypothesis. The caption of m4 is updated (F162 point added). Not tested: TTUCCTL on the own SM with the sibling's L1 held warm by ordinary loads. Files model/predictions_fence_i.md, model/results_fence_i.md; harness build_patch --ownccctl, check_seq B15 rules; data notes/q22 (private).
163. **2026-11-16, the two fixed costs are one: about 740 cycles is the fixed cost of a query with every line in L1 (F25), the 934 of F67 is that plus roughly one L2 line per query that the F67 fit does not count as a node.** Checked from the data, no new GPU run. F25 (same ray traced twice in one kernel, second timed): 780 / 830 / 870 cycles at 1 / 2 / 3 internal levels, so 43 per level and 740 at zero levels, with the OptiX instance node, the GAS nodes and the triangle data all in L1. F67 (60 scenes, one warp, one query that visits every node and leaf, a fresh launch each time so L2 warm and L1 cold, 5 launches with the first dropped): refitted from the stored table, latency = 934 (standard error 50) + 343 (158) per node + 113 (17) per leaf + 52 (2) per triangle, rms 287; at the one triangle scene the fit gives 1,441 against 1,591 measured. So the two numbers answer different questions: 740 is the cost of the unit's own work and the SM instructions around it when nothing has to be fetched; 934 is an intercept of a regression whose other terms count only nodes, leaves and triangles of the GAS. The 194 cycle gap (934 minus 740, error about 50) is the size of one more L2 fetch less the 43 warm level: the OptiX instance node is read by every query (F148: its line costs 320 cycles from L2) and is not in the node count; 740 plus 295 (L2 round trip of the chase) minus 43 is 992, within 1.2 standard errors of 934. Cross check without any fit: one triangle scene is 780 cycles warm (F25) and 1,591 to 1,640 with L1 cold and L2 warm (F20, F22, F67 table), a difference of 810 to 860 cycles, about three L2 lines (instance node, GAS root, leaf and triangle) at 270 to 295 cycles each. Which is right: both, for their state; the model's fixed cost t_fixed is 740 (L1 warm), and the cold L1 cost is the lines fetched at the cost of their cache level, with the instance node line as one of them. The brief's wording "about 740 (warm L1), 1,640 (L2 warm, L1 cold), 2,630 (cold)" is correct. Not tested: that the 194 is the instance node line and nothing else (a scene without an instance level does not exist in OptiX; F148 measured the line directly); the regression terms are correlated (node coefficient error 158), so 934 itself is only known to about plus or minus 100 in practice. The intro draft says 740 for L1 warm and explains the 934.
164. **2026-11-16, Mochi validation cannot start: the Mochi source is not available.** The earlier notes (F132) say Mochi built with OWL and ran 10 frames on a 100,000 particle file on the 4070 Ti, from a clone in /dev/shm on io; that clone is gone (/dev/shm is per machine), the home directory has no Mochi source (the OWLRayTracing tree in the home directory has an empty samples folder), and a web search finds the Mochi paper (Mandarapu, James, Kulkarni; Computer Graphics Forum 2024; arXiv 2402.14801) but no code repository. DATASETS.md already recorded that the earlier clone cannot be reproduced from the paper's links. So the 10 frame run is not reconfirmed here, no per frame BVH dumps exist, and no predictions were written. What is needed: the repository address or source archive from the authors (the Kulkarni group can supply it), then the order is build, 10 frames, per frame BVH dump, predictions committed, measurement. Not started, nothing run on a GPU.
165. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, Mochi (collision detection, 100,000 particles) validated: the replay predicts the intersection calls per query exactly while the particles are a falling lattice (frames 0 to 4: 1.082 predicted, 1.075 measured), but under predicts them more and more once the particles pile up and the refit tree degrades (frame 9: 6.4 predicted, 12.9 measured), and the launch time is above the lower bound as stated.** This replaces F164 (which said the source could not be found: the repository is github MDurgaKeerthi/Mochi-DCD-on-RT, cloned to ~/Mochi-DCD-on-RT outside the repo, never committed). Program: s23-3Dparticlesim, one point ray per particle (tmax 1e-16) against one box per particle (centre -/+ 2 radius, custom primitive), the intersection program computes the distance and for an overlapping pair does 6 atomic adds; the tree is built once and refit in each of 400 substeps per frame; builds and runs 10 frames (unpatched: 2.2 s, refit 1,328 ms, launches 285 ms by host timers). Predictions were committed first (286faf2) from a dump run (run A1) of the refit GAS and particles at the end of each frame, replayed on the CPU (the F125 replay: hardware calls = 1.006 x primitives in entered leaf slots), then a recorder run (run B) counted the calls per query and timed every launch in the kernel. Runs are bit identical for frames 0 to 4 and diverge from frame 5 (float atomics), checked with a second dump run. Counts per query (measured / predicted): 1.075 / 1.082 in frames 0 to 4; 2.65 / 2.42, 3.71 / 3.11, 5.76 / 4.22, 8.46 / 5.40, 12.94 / 6.50 in frames 5 to 9; the replay of the measured run's own dump is as far off (-8.6 to -50 percent), so the miss is not the changed trajectory. Replay nodes per query rise from 6.25 (frames 0 to 4) to 253 (frame 9) as the refit tree degrades. Launch span (median of 400 launches, in kernel global timer): 0.099 to 0.106 ms in frames 0 to 4, then 0.099, 0.113, 0.139, 0.181, 0.239 ms in frames 5 to 9; the lower bound (RT part, F127 form, 142 SMs at 2.5 GHz assumed) was 0.034 to 0.041 ms, the committed bracket 0.034 to 0.17 ms for frames 0 to 4 (held), and the committed ratio statement frame 9 over frame 0 above 1.5 held (2.26). Lane efficiency 0.602 (exact in frames 0 to 4) and 0.446 to 0.544 measured against 0.406 to 0.501 predicted in frames 5 to 9. Not found: why the refit tree makes the hardware call the program more often than the replay says (replay slot assignment by box matching is ambiguous in a refit tree: not tested); the recorder's own overhead; whether the node count term matters (needs correct counts). Files model/predict_mochi.py, model/mochi_compare.py, model/predictions_mochi.md, model/results_mochi.md; patches and data private in notes/q24 (patch diff, GAS dump of run B, per query counts, launch spans).
166. **2026-11-16, Mochi count gap explained: the replay's box matching put primitives into the wrong leaf slots on the refit tree; with the true primitives per leaf slot the replay predicts the intersection calls per query within 1.4 to 2.9 percent in every frame (box matched: -8.6 to -50 percent) and the lane efficiency within 0.006.** Found by reading the BVH, not by fitting: in a custom primitive GAS the low 3 bits of a leaf slot's type byte are the number of primitives in the slot (0x41 is 1, 0x42 is 2; 0 would mean 8); over all 96,233 leaf slots of the 100,000 particle tree (92,466 with one, 3,767 with two) they add up to exactly the 100,000 primitives, so every primitive is in exactly one slot (no duplicated references in this tree), and the slot types and the topology are the same in every frame (only the boxes are refit). Test on run B's own dumps and measured counts (no new GPU run): the table of F165 rebuilt with these counts, same rays and boxes, measured against the per query calls counted in the intersection program: mean calls per query 1.075 measured and 1.082 replay in frames 0 to 4 (identical to the box matched result, where the assignment is unambiguous), and in frames 5 to 9 measured 2.651 / 3.713 / 5.764 / 8.464 / 12.941 against 2.614 / 3.619 / 5.598 / 8.231 / 12.612 (errors -1.4, -2.5, -2.9, -2.8, -2.5 percent), per query correlation 0.985 to 0.994, and no query has more replay calls than measured by over half a call; 4.6 to 27.5 percent of the queries have slightly more calls in hardware (residual, not separated: the hardware enters somewhat more leaf slots than the exact slab test of the replay, or rounds boxes outward). The success rule of F165 (5 percent in own replay) is met in every frame. Why box matching failed: as the particles pile up the refit boxes of sibling leaf slots overlap more and more, so the smallest slot box containing a primitive is often not its slot; for the lattice of frames 0 to 4 the match is unambiguous. This also means the F125 / RTNN replays (box matching, freshly built trees) may carry the same error in dense scenes; not rechecked. The primitive ids per slot are still not decoded (only the counts, enough for a point query count); the 64 bit entries (id, 0) seen in the section after the header look like the primitive index array in leaf order, not followed up. The node counts per query (6.3 to 253.8) do not depend on the assignment, so the node term could now be checked against time: with the true counts the F127 form gives 0.048 ms at frame 9 against 0.239 measured. Files model/mochi_leafcount.py, model/results_mochi_leafcount.md, model/replay/README.md; data notes/q24 (private). Also corrected in the committed predictions file: the column "exact box hits (incl. self)" is too high by 1 (note added there, table unchanged).
167. **2026-11-16, recheck of the RTNN replays (F125, F127) with the true primitives per leaf slot: no change, the box matched and the true count replays give the same count for every query in a freshly built tree, so the F125 to F127 numbers, fits and figures stand.** Rebuilt the patched RTNN on triton (OptiX only) and ran the 12 configurations of F127 (100,000 points radius 2, 4, 6 and 1,000,000 points radius 1, 2, 3, sorted and unsorted); to get the same grid sizing as the 4070 Ti the device memory RTNN reads was set to the 4070 Ti's 11.6 GB (at the 48 GB of this card the program builds a 1850 cubed grid and faults in a thrust scan: unrelated to the question, noted so nobody repeats it). Triton's trees equal io's (per query hardware calls identical for 99.7 percent of queries and the same mean, 54.241, for 100,000 points radius 4 sorted). Hardware calls per query (recorder) and replay (1.006 times the primitives in entered slots), box matched and true count identical: 7.97 against 7.96, 54.24 against 54.22, 9.84 against 9.82, 69.65 against 69.66 (within 0.2 percent, the same as F125), lane efficiency within 0.003; the radius 6 (100,000 points) and radius 3 (1,000,000 points) configurations are 76 and 117 percent above the recorder in both replays, which is the K = 50 early termination of F127, not the slot assignment. Slot counts: the slots hold 1 to 6 boxes in these trees (100,000 points radius 4: 66,206 of 1, 14,684 of 2, 1,403 of 3, 53 of 4, 1 of 5; none of 8), the sum equals the primitive count. So box matching is exact on freshly built trees and wrong only on a refit tree whose boxes have been stretched by motion (F166); the warning in model/replay/README.md now says exactly that. Nothing in F125 to F127, the model refit or the figures needs to be redone. Files model/rtnn_leafcount.py, model/results_rtnn_leafcount.md; patch recipe notes/q27 (private); dumps (424 MB) deleted, regenerable.
168. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, Mochi time gap with the callback cost measured and fed in (not a prediction): the callbacks are only 7 to 23 percent of the warp time and the model stays 2 to 3 times below the measured launch span, so the missing piece is not the callback term but the traversal itself (a node fetch chain of about 400 cycles per node of the warp's maximum, plus launch ramp and queueing).** Form committed first (model/FORM_mochi_time.md). Clock64 probes in the intersection program and raygen (per warp cycles and calls) on the full launch of the last substep of each frame, and on an extra launch of 4,544 queries (one warp per SM) at the same state with the forces sent to a scratch buffer so that the run is not disturbed; spans with probes equal the spans without within 0.006 ms. Model: F127 form on the true counts of F166 plus measured latency times warp calls over SMs times implied warps in flight plus 875 cycles per warp call (F137): 0.047 ms (frame 0) to 0.082 ms (frame 9) with the loaded latency, 0.038 to 0.075 with the unloaded one, against measured spans of 0.100 and 0.246 ms: log errors -0.75 to -1.1 (loaded), -0.97 to -1.19 (unloaded); the success rule (within 0.22 for frames 0 to 4 and 9) failed. Measured: callbacks 11 percent of the summed warp time at frame 0, 23 percent at frame 5, 7 percent at frame 9 (loaded 1,100 to 5,000 cycles per warp call, unloaded 700 to 780); traversal window 73 to 92 percent; one warp per SM trace window 10,136 cycles at frame 0 and 121,644 at frame 9, with 380 to 430 cycles per node of the warp's maximum node count over a fixed 12,000 to 20,000 cycles (frames 5, 7, 9); loaded warps take 7.0 times (frame 0) and 3.6 times (frame 9) the one warp value, implied warps in flight 6.9 rising to 15.8. Ruled out: callbacks, the hand off term (0.002 to 0.014 ms), probe overhead, the clock assumption, the counts (F166). Not explained: the launch at frame 0 costs 355 cycles per query per SM against the 110 of the F127 form (fitted on 10 to 228 candidates per query) and 27 times the unit capacity bound of F105 (9,200 cycles); whether it is launch ramp, queueing in the traversal unit or the memory system is untested. Files model/mochi_time.py, model/FORM_mochi_time.md, model/results_mochi_time.md; data and patches private in notes/q27.
169. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, loaded traversal test (OptiX only): the Mochi time gap is neither launch ramp nor queueing in the traversal unit, because a plain loaded launch of 100,000 point queries with 1 candidate each takes only 12 to 17 microseconds, 6 to 8 times less than Mochi's 100 microseconds.** Form, predictions and decision rule committed first (model/FORM_loaded_traversal.md). A custom primitive tree of 100,000 disjoint boxes, one point query per box (1.03 intersection calls per query), query count from 32 to 1.6 million, lattice order and random order, 20 timed launches each. Result by the rule: not separated (the intercept share of the 100,000 launch is 0.34 and 0.32, between the 0.25 and 0.5 limits; warp latency grows only 1.3 to 1.65 times from one warp per SM to 100,000, where queueing needed 2). What it shows: the span is a straight line, about 4.1 microseconds (one warp latency, 9,100 to 10,600 cycles) plus 0.079 ns per query (12.6 queries per ns over the GPU, about half the 26 per ns unit bound of F105; random order 5.4 microseconds plus 0.110 ns, 9.1 per ns); warps in flight saturate at 13 of 22 per SM; warp latency stays near 11,000 to 13,500 cycles at every load, unlike Mochi's loaded warps (7.0 times the one warp value, F168). A launch of 100,000 queries costs 28 to 42 cycles per query per SM here against 355 for Mochi. The F127 form (110 cycles) is 2.8 times too high for this 1 candidate lattice, as F127 itself says for the smallest radius. So the loaded traversal of simple point queries needs no ramp or queueing term; the Mochi excess has to come from what Mochi has and this test does not: its refitted updatable tree (node count and quality), its much heavier raygen and intersection program (force gathers and writes), the launch sequence, or its query positions. None of these was run. Files ttu/bench/Load_1, model/loaded_traversal.py, model/results_loaded_traversal.md; raw data private in notes/q28.
170. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, refit arm (OptiX only): a refitted tree that has lost its spatial order is the slow case, up to 134 times slower than a fresh tree at the same positions and queries, but it does not explain Mochi's 100 microsecond launch at frame 0, where the calls per query are 1.08.** Form, predictions and decision rule committed first (model/FORM_refit_arm.md, with an addendum written between the two runs). The 100,000 box lattice of F169 with three trees at every step, same 100,000 point queries: R built with the update flag once and refitted at every step, U a fresh build with the update flag, F a fresh build. Motion 1, a squeeze along z (boxes overlap more and more, order preserved): R within 1.09 of F everywhere, 13 microseconds at 1.065 calls per query rising to 36 at 4.2; this motion cannot degrade a refit, so it only rules out overlap alone. Motion 2, a random scramble of up to f cells per particle (density constant, a refit tree loses its order): R equals F at f 0, then 1.1, 1.4, 1.7, 4.6, 23 and 134 times the fresh tree at f = 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 (104, 528 and 2,891 microseconds at f = 4, 8, 16), while the fresh trees stay at 20 to 23 microseconds and the update flag alone changes nothing. The refitted tree makes 4.1, 13.3 and 53.7 calls per query where the fresh tree makes 1.5, 1.4 and 1.3 (overlapping leaf boxes). By the rule set beforehand tree quality is part of the answer (R / F at least 2); the size prediction (1.5 to 4 times at f = 8 and 16) missed by a large factor, and the prediction that the call counts agree was wrong. It fits Mochi's growth over frames 5 to 9 (0.100 to 0.246 ms, calls 1.08 to 12.9, refit tree) in size, not in mechanism proven. It does NOT explain frames 0 to 4: a refit tree at 1.065 calls per query takes 13 microseconds here, Mochi 100. Remaining candidates for that gap (not run): Mochi's heavier raygen and intersection program, its launch sequence, how its frame 0 tree is built, its query positions. Range limit recorded: the F127 form (110 cycles per query per SM) is 2.8 times too high at 1 candidate (F169) and cannot see a degraded refit tree; do not use it below about 2 candidates per query or on a refit tree. Files ttu/bench/Load_1 (load2.cu), model/refit_arm.py, model/results_refit_arm.md; raw data private in notes/q29.
171. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, program arm (OptiX only): Mochi's own raygen and intersection program, and the instance level it traces through, are ruled out as the cause of the 100 microsecond launch at frames 0 to 4: the full replica takes 11.3 microseconds for 100,000 queries.** Form, predictions, decision rule and two source checks committed first (model/FORM_program_arm.md). The real Mochi dataset in Mochi's Morton order (calls per query 1.075, exactly Mochi's measured frame 0 value), the GAS built with the update flag only as Mochi does, 100,000 point queries, two programs (trivial, or Mochi's with the particle loads from global memory, the payload pointer to a local copy of the query particle, the distance, the collision test and the atomics) times two structures (GAS only, or an instance level over it as Mochi traces). Spans 11.3 (trivial, GAS), 12.3 (Mochi program, GAS), 10.2 (trivial, instance level), 11.3 microseconds (Mochi program, instance level), all within one 1.024 microsecond timer step of each other; warp latency 13,000 to 19,000 cycles against 78,000 for Mochi's loaded warps. The predictions for the Mochi program arms (15 to 35 and 20 to 55 microseconds) were too high. Checks: the 100 microseconds in F168 is the trace launch only (global timer span from raygen start to end; refit and the update kernel are outside it). Mochi's frame 0 tree: Morton sorted particles, one custom primitive GAS with the update flag only, one instance group over it with the update flag, traced through the instance group, after 200 warmup substeps with a refit of both before frame 0 and a refit of both before every launch (read from the source, not checked against a dumped GAS). With F169 (a loaded traversal) and F170 (a refit chain on a lattice, a fresh tree) the remaining candidates for frames 0 to 4 are the launch sequence (the update kernel, two refits and the host synchronisation between launches change the unit's and the caches' state at the start of a launch, cf. F159 to F162, and the clock state), the refit chain on the real moving particles, and the query positions after the fall; none run. 172. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, clock, node count, flags and the between launch sequence (OptiX only): none of them explains the 100 microsecond Mochi launch at frame 0; the replica with Mochi's program, instance level, update flag and the whole update and refit sequence before every launch takes 11.3 microseconds, the same as launches back to back.** Form and predictions committed first (model/FORM_sequence_arm.md). Clock: nvidia-smi sampled every 20 ms during unpatched Mochi: 2,700 MHz in 498 of 589 samples during the run, 2,505 to 2,700 in all (max 3,105), and inside the launch the longest warp of frame 0 (242,321 cycles) fills the 100.4 microsecond span, so the clock is at least 2.41 GHz; the 2.5 GHz of F168 was fine. Nodes: a replay of the replica's own GAS gives 6.25 nodes, 1.00 leaf slots and 1.08 primitives per query and lane efficiency 0.602, identical to Mochi's frame 0, so the replica does the same traversal work. Flags: OWL passes the build flags through unchanged (GAS and instance group: ALLOW_UPDATE only), the replica builds the same. Sequence: update kernel and synchronisation, update kernel plus bounds kernel plus refit of the GAS and of the instance group plus synchronisations, with 200 warmup iterations and 400 timed launches: 11.26 microseconds in all three cases (the predictions for the sequence, 20 to 100, missed low). What the data shows instead: Mochi's per warp raygen time at frame 0 is highly skewed (10th percentile 17,000, median 40,000, 90th 189,000, max 242,000 cycles, growing with the warp index) while the replica's warps are alike (15,700 to 22,200); the excess is a tail of long warps, as if groups of warps waited for the same event. Remaining candidates, none run: the OWL pipeline and stack configuration, the SBT record reads, anything specific to the OWL process. Files ttu/bench/Load_1 (load3.cu SEQ and WARM, load3_seq_run.py), model/results_sequence_arm.md; raw data private in notes/q31.
173. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, last Mochi arm (OptiX only): none of the six differences between Mochi's OWL setup and the replica produces the 100 microsecond launch or the tail of slow warps, alone or together; the gap stays unexplained.** Form, differences table and predictions committed first (model/FORM_options_arm.md). Read from the source: the module options (optimisation level 3, minimal debug information), exception flags, payload count, motion blur and the single level instancing flag are the same in OWL and the replica; six things differ: attribute count (2 against 0), the sphere primitive flag, trace depth in the link options (2 against 1), stack sizes (OWL sets 2,048 three times with depth 2 and never computes them; the replica uses the OptiX default, which the API cannot read), program data in the SBT records (OWL) against the launch parameters, and the PTX target (Mochi's PTX is built for the oldest architecture, the replica's for sm_89, with different flags). Each was flipped into the replica alone (100,000 queries, 400 timed launches after 200 warmups, two rounds, rotated order, all exit 0), then all together: spans 11.3 to 12.3 microseconds (one timer step), per warp times narrow (90th percentile 1.13 to 1.15 times the median), calls per query 1.075, against 100 microseconds and 4.7 times for Mochi. The prediction (none of them explains it, spans within 15 percent of the baseline) held. Consequence for the paper: the time model is scoped to plain OptiX launches (F169, F171), Mochi's count result (F165, F166) is kept, and the Mochi time gap is reported as unexplained. Files ttu/bench/Load_1 (load3.cu FLIP, load3_dev.cu SBTDATA, load4_run.py), model/results_options_arm.md; raw private in notes/q32.
174. **2026-11-16, triton GPU 1, 1 MB of ordinary loads through the TTU's own SM between the two sessions removes about half of the second session saving beyond what the same wait removes: the node state the traversal unit reuses is in the L1 of its own SM (own SM half of the question; the sibling SM is still open).** Predictions committed first (model/predictions_node_state.md). A plain role block on the same SM as the single working TTU block (grid of 142 blocks, placement checked with plain CUDA first; a single block does not land beside a plain block, a grid does) polls the flag set after the first session and then either streams 1 MB with ordinary cached loads (70 microseconds) or waits 70 microseconds with no loads. Saved fraction of the second session at 68 and 132 KB of BVH: nothing between 0.61 and 0.57; 1 MB stream 0.29 and 0.27; same wait without loads 0.53 and 0.42 (5 repetitions of 30 launches, medians, every run exit 0, one TTU block). The prediction for the hypothesis that the node lines are in L1 (about 0.26) held; the prediction for a separate structure (stream within 0.08 of the wait) missed (0.24 and 0.15 apart). The wait alone costs part of the saving at 132 KB, which is why the comparison is against the wait. Test (i) (TTUCCTL on one SM, reload on the other) was not run: its probe, a plain block reloading 32 KB after a possible invalidate, did not distinguish a fence from no fence in stage 0. Two earlier designs failed before this one (a TTU warp loop that faults in stage 0 for reasons not found, and a plain block launched after the TTU kernel that never landed on the SM and hung); no TTU fault in any run. 175. **2026-11-16, node state summary (no new run): the traversal unit's node state is "node state in the TPC's L1s": node lines in the two L1s of the TPC, about half in each, ordinary loads cannot create them, a gpu scope fence or a stream of ordinary loads clears one SM's half, TTUCCTL from either SM clears both, capacity about 130 KB.** Put together from F147 (ordinary loads do not reach the second session cost), F150 (capacity), F159 and F174 (one SM's half cleared by a fence or by 1 MB of ordinary loads, about 0.26 to 0.29 of the saving left against 0.5 without loads), F161 and F162 (TTUCCTL from the sibling or the own SM removes everything). The sibling reload probe that F174 planned was not needed and is dropped: F161 with F174 already show TTUCCTL is TPC wide. The name is not "L0": nothing shows a separate cache level. Figure m4 now carries the F174 arms.
176. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090) GPU 1, core set against the predictions committed before any run (model/predictions_4090.md, results in model/results_4090.md): per SM cycle costs carry over from triton almost unchanged, the TPC scaling holds, and the misses are the L2 capacity ramp (later and wider than modelled) and one written absolute throughput number.** Detected: 128 SMs, 64 TPCs, 72 MB L2, 384 bit memory, driver 580.178.04 (already validated on io); block placement rule held (0 mismatches); the OptiX traversal group read back from our own launch is byte identical to the earlier dump. Matched: L1 hit 41 cycles, L2 hit 285 to 295 cycles up to 70 MB, DRAM level 620 cycles (6 percent under the predicted 658, inside 10 percent); one triangle warm 1,630, cold 2,627, second session 782 cycles; depth sweep warm within 3.3 percent and cold within 4.3 percent at all seven sizes; one SM throughput at 1 to 20 warps within 1 percent of the prediction and a steady state of 0.143 to 0.148 rays per cycle; TPC siblings 1.02 to 1.05 times one SM and different TPCs 2.0 times on long queries; long query whole GPU scaling 63.5 times at one SM per TPC (99 percent) and 64.6 times with both SMs of every TPC; second session costs next to a working sibling 782 / 827 / 867 cycles and 1,676 to 2,335 on another TPC; L1 about 90 KB falling to about 20 KB with the shared memory reservation. Missed: the capacity ramp (at 72, 74 and 76 MB the latency is 354, 451 and 572 cycles against 425, 554 and 658 predicted: hits still occur at 76 MB, so the ramp starts later and is wider than the model, not refitted), and the absolute throughput of the long query scene (0.0178 rays per cycle per SM against 0.10 written: the prediction used the wrong scene's rate, triton measures the same 0.0178, so it is an error in the prediction and not a difference between boards). Not measured: the Memory_2 refetch fraction and the 50 percent split of node fetches between the TPC's two L1s (ncu cannot open /tmp/nsight-compute-lock, owned by another user, reported to the administrators), the short query sibling version, and the IAS group on this driver. The tri1 whole GPU numbers were not predicted. System libnvoptix is absent on tethys, so the extracted copy of the matching 580.178.04 library was used (as documented for this case).
177. **2026-11-16, F176 bookkeeping (no new run): the L2 ramp miss is a model limitation, the Throughput_6 absolute rate was a prediction writing error, and ncu honors TMPDIR for its lock file.** (1) The L2 capacity term (hit 1 up to 0.97 times the L2 size, 0 from 1.05 times, F110) was fitted on one chip, the 4070 Ti. On the 4090 the latency at 72, 74 and 76 MB is 354, 451 and 572 cycles against 425, 554 and 658: the ramp starts later and is wider. It is recorded as a limit of the model, valid for the 4070 Ti only, not refitted; the triton ramp (88 to 104 MB) is still untested. A cause (replacement policy or L2 partitioning) is not known; Cache_2 is planned for it. (2) The long query rate of 0.10 rays per cycle per SM in the 4090 predictions was the wrong scene's number; triton and the 4090 both measure 0.0178. It is a writing error in the prediction, not a difference between boards and not a refit; the prediction file keeps its original text with a dated erratum below it. (3) From the Nsight Compute 2025.1.1 documentation (the copy under our CUDA 12.8): the lock file is TMPDIR/nsight-compute-lock, where the directory is the first of TMPDIR, TMP, TEMP, TEMPDIR that is set, else /tmp; the documented workaround for a lock file owned by another user is to set TMPDIR to a directory the current user can write, provided no other ncu instance is running on the machine. So the Memory_2 refetch fraction and the 50 percent L1 split can be tried on tethys with TMPDIR set to a directory we own (for example one under /dev/shm), after checking nvidia-smi for other profiling processes, because our own lock then no longer excludes theirs. Not tested (no GPU run this session). The administrators are only needed if that fails, for the admin only profiling setting on triton, and for the missing system OptiX library on tethys (optional, the extracted copy works). Coverage matrix, rankings and the harness gap test are in Phase 10 of this file.
178. **2026-11-16, HG_1, tethys GPU 1, one warp, harness against OptiX on the same rays (predictions committed first, model/predictions_hg1.md; results model/results_hg1.md): the harness constants for the query itself (fixed cost, node, leaf, triangle) represent OptiX exactly, but OptiX adds a constant of about 4,000 cycles per optixTrace call at one warp (plus about 1,200 on the first call of a launch) that the harness does not have, so by the reading rule the one warp gap is over 20 percent (3.1 on one triangle, 1.23 on random rays).** One block, one warp, 32 rays, 100 launches per scene, L2 warm and L1 cold; the harness loop (whole ray, clock before the first group and after the resume loop) against clock probes around optixTrace in a raygen of our own; every harness lane equals OptiX; spread under 0.5 percent per scene; four OptiX series agree within 1 percent. Harness loop: 2,211 cycles on one triangle to 3,149 at 20,736 triangles, 19,596 on 32 random rays of the 100,000 soup (1.9 rounds, 3 at most). OptiX trace: 6,856 to 7,743, 24,215 on the soup. OptiX minus harness loop is 4,528 to 4,663 cycles in all eight scenes: no slope in nodes (the OptiX cost grows 924 from one to 20,736 triangles, the harness group 926) and none that could be separated in rounds (one multi round scene). Tracing the same ray again inside the raygen (three traces) gives 4,750 warm on one triangle, equal in call 2 and 3 to the cycle, and the warm cost differs from the harness warm second session (782, F176) by 3,968 to 3,970 in every one round scene, so the constant is per call and independent of the scene. The first call costs about 1,200 more (cold code, not separated from other first call effects). ncu: the HG_1 raygen launch executes 1,143 warp instructions, the harness loop and group about 100, so the constant is about 3.8 cycles per extra instruction (a lone warp cannot hide the issue latency of OptiX's own instructions); a raygen with fewer payload values (709 instructions) has a smaller constant (about 2,600), so the number belongs to the program around the trace. Harness loop overhead (its own reload after every query) is about 580 per round. Reading: the model's per query and per node constants carry over to OptiX unchanged; an OptiX launch of one warp is dominated by a per call term that other warps hide, so one warp comparisons of whole rays say nothing about the 1.6 times whole GPU gap, which stays a concurrency question (next: Sched_1, Sched_2, Unit_1). Predictions missed: harness one triangle 1,640 (measured 2,211: the reload term was not counted), random rays 7,500 (19,596), OptiX trace 1,900 (6,856); the form (one additive constant, no slopes) held, its size was 18 times larger than predicted; the second trace test (trace 2 below 3,000 with 70 percent) missed (4,750); the rotated ray arm did not give unseen data (the other lanes had already traced those rays). Not done: cold L2 variant, OptiX queries per ray by replay, a probe cost measurement, 4 to 28 warps for OptiX.
179. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090) Memory_2 now measured: the node cache holds a 60 KB footprint (at most 3 percent refetched) and half of a 94 KB footprint, as on triton and io; the ncu lock file problem is solved by an own TMPDIR, no ECN request needed.** ncu failed on tethys with /tmp/nsight-compute-lock owned by another user although the file is world writable (the likely cause is the kernel's protected regular file rule for sticky directories; /proc/sys/fs/protected_regular could not be read, so not verified). With TMPDIR set to a directory we own (under /dev/shm), after checking that no other ncu runs, a plain kernel and then the whole Memory_2 run (15 footprints, 2 ray pools, 2 L1 sizes, 6.5 minutes, no fault) worked. Refetch share of the second session with the full L1: 0.00 to 0.03 up to a 60 KB footprint, 0.50 at 94 KB, 0.61 at 174 KB, 0.73 at 539 KB, 1.01 at 719 KB; with the L1 reduced to about 20 KB every footprint is refetched 1.2 to 5.5 times. Prediction 9 of model/predictions_4090.md (found again below about 60 KB, half lost at about 100 KB) holds; details model/results_4090.md addendum. The item for ECN about the ncu lock file can be dropped for tethys; the triton setting (counters admin only) is separate and still open.
180. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090), ray flags (predictions committed first, model/predictions_flags.md, results model/results_flags.md): terminate on first hit shortens the unit's work on random rays but not a lone warp's time, an any hit call costs about 1,565 cycles of latency at one warp but only about 450 cycles of throughput per warp, and back face culling is nearly free.** OptiX only, a new raygen with clock probes, one warp (32 rays, 100 launches) and launches of 262,144 rays with ncu. Terminate on first hit: on 64 parallel planes no effect (the nearest plane is already the first hit found), on 32 random rays of the 100,000 soup the one warp trace falls 1.8 percent, the unit's L1 requests per ray fall 21 percent (80.9 to 64.0), kernel time per ray 12 percent, SM instructions per ray and hit fraction are identical; this corrects F56, which said the flag changes nothing in the unit. Any hit program that ignores every hit (16 per ray): one warp trace 28,439 warm against 3,401 without it, so 1,565 cycles per call (222 SM instructions, 7 cycles per instruction), a prediction of 850 (from the custom primitive figure of F49) missed; at the whole GPU rate the 16 calls add 0.66 ns per ray, about 450 SM cycles per call per warp. The ray flag DISABLE_ANYHIT, the geometry flag at build and no any hit program are the same within 1.5 percent; ENFORCE_ANYHIT equals the default. Cull back: +5 percent cold, +1.7 percent warm, 4 more L1 fill sectors, the skipped plane is not returned; cull front equals no flag when the nearest plane is back facing. Cull both flags at once is documented as mutually exclusive: the configuration was invalid (result not used). Prediction misses: terminate on first hit at one warp (predicted 5 to 30 percent), any hit cost per call, both culls. Not done: instance flags, our own TTU sequence with any of these flags.
181. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090), masked out instances are almost free: 63 instances rejected by the visibility mask add 4 L1 fill sectors and 43 cycles (257 cold) over a scene with only the one visible instance, and they are cheaper than visible ones.** IAS of 64 instances of one triangle GAS along the ray, one warp: one instance scene 4,632 cold and 2,282 warm cycles (54 fill sectors per 32 ray launch), 63 masked out and the farthest visible 4,889 and 2,325 (58 sectors), 64 visible 5,616 and 2,539 (66 sectors), ray mask 255 with the nearest visible the same as 64 visible. The prediction that masked instances would cost at least the interior IAS nodes (60 to 130 sectors) and that each might cost an instance node line (250 to 400 sectors) both missed far above the measured 4. So whole subtrees of masked instances are rejected without being read (an interior node mask summary would explain it; not decoded). Limit: the masked block was contiguous by position, a lone masked instance between visible ones was not tested. Consequence for the model: per class filters by mask cost nothing per rejected block; the cost sits in the visible instances.
182. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090), tmax prunes strongly but not in proportion to the radius: on the 100,000 soup the unit's L1 requests per ray go from 21 at tmax 0 to 81 unlimited, half of the unlimited value is reached just below tmax 0.1, and a zero length ray still costs 21 requests and 37 percent of the time.** 262,144 random rays, origin uniform in the scene cube, tmax 0 to 2 and unlimited (scene edge 2, triangle edge 0.065). RT unit L1 requests per ray: 20.7 / 22.9 / 25.2 / 31.5 / 41.8 / 56.8 / 75.2 / 80.2 / 80.7 / 80.8 at tmax 0 / 0.01 / 0.02 / 0.05 / 0.1 / 0.2 / 0.5 / 1 / 2 / unlimited; time per ray 0.188 / 0.188 / 0.219 / 0.246 / 0.289 / 0.371 / 0.469 / 0.496 / 0.507 / 0.508 ns; hit fraction 0 / 0.038 / 0.075 / 0.175 / 0.314 / 0.509 / 0.754 / 0.823 / 0.829 / 0.829 (corrected 2026-11-17: the first version of this series was taken from the 32 ray probe launch, not the 262,144 ray launch; the requests and times were right). A radius of 0.05 saves 61 percent of the requests and 52 percent of the time against unlimited; beyond tmax 1 nothing changes (within 1 percent). Against the predictions: right at tmax 0 to 0.02 and in the time ratios, 30 to 36 percent too high between 0.05 and 0.2 (45 / 65 / 80 predicted), saturation 81 not 90, hit fraction too high (0.9 predicted, 0.83 measured). One warp (32 rays): trace falls to 0.39 (cold) and 0.51 (warm) of the unlimited one at tmax 0.05, below the predicted 0.5 to 0.8 on the cold side. Consequence: the saving of a radius limit is large but not proportional to the radius; a zero length ray is not free (F44).

183. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090), where ray flags, mask, tmin and tmax enter the traversal unit's stores: they are run time values built in the raygen, never in the traversal code (predictions committed first, model/predictions_flagenc.md; our own OptiX launches, no TTU code of ours).** Fifteen raygen variants (each of the eight OptiX ray flags alone, terminate plus disable closest hit, three masks, tmax 0.05 and 2, tmin 0.1) and the base twice (17 reads) were read with memdump (64 KB of live code per process, one process each, no fault). The traversal function and the TTU group are byte identical in all 17 reads (the base twice and 15 variants), so a flag or mask does not select a different traversal code. The raygen packs the flags, the mask and a constant 1 into one register word (flags in bits 16 and up, mask in bits 8 to 15), and tmax and tmin are plain float registers set by single move instructions; nothing else in the raygen changes. The computed store words (hand transcription of the traversal code, not yet checked on the GPU) put terminate on first hit, back and front cull and the mask into the first word of the two ray flag stores (the mask also in the third word), and the any hit disable, any hit enforce, cull opaque and cull non opaque flags into the low byte of the second word; disable closest hit alone changes no word and matters only together with terminate on first hit (it selects the path where the shadow ray bit is set and two stores are predicated off). tmax and tmin are in the ray origin and direction stores, not in these. Prediction misses: I predicted that the four any hit and opaque flags would not change a store word (they do, in the computed words); unexplained and not predicted: in 10 of the 15 variants (terminate, all four cull flags, all masks, tmax 2, tmin 0.1) the raygen sits 0x1400 bytes higher and the surrounding code differs, in the others it does not, and this is unrelated to the content (tmax 0.05 and 2 differ). Consequence: a TTU sequence of ours can set flags and mask by changing those words only, which is not yet run (Flags_2 would be the first real check, usual TTU rules).
184. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090), masked out instances between visible ones: a lone masked instance costs nothing measurable, but half of all instances masked in alternation still pull the same cache lines as visible ones (one warp), and at whole GPU throughput they cost only 2 percent (predictions committed first, model/predictions_masklone.md, results model/results_masklone.md; OptiX only).** One IAS of 256 instances along the ray, rays that pass through every instance's box and miss the triangle, ray mask 1. Every 16th instance masked (16 lone masked ones) against the same 16 removed: one warp 1.4 percent (cold) and 2.2 percent (warm) faster, L1 fill sectors 785 against 777, 262,144 rays 0.6 percent slower, so no cost beyond the difference in tree shape (the two trees differ by 9 percent in the unit's L1 requests). Odd instances masked against only the even instances present (128 visible each): the fill sectors equal the all visible scene (785 against 417 for the even only tree, 2.9 sectors per instance either way), one warp time +50 percent cold and +13 percent warm over the even only scene (all visible: +95 percent), 262,144 rays +2 percent (all visible: +95 percent), the unit's L1 requests per ray higher than for all visible. So a masked instance next to visible ones is not free in cache lines when most of its neighbours are visible and masked ones alternate, but it costs almost no traversal time at throughput; it is free when it can be skipped with the entry of a node that is read anyway or when whole blocks are masked (F181). Predictions missed: the cost of a visible instance (2.9 sectors, predicted 5 to 13), the interleaved case within 3 percent of the even only scene (missed at one warp, held at throughput), the 6 percent saving for 16 absent instances (1 and 3 percent). Not separated: IAS nodes against instance nodes among the extra fills, a masked instance near the root versus a leaf, sibling masks other than 1 in 16 and 1 in 2. The rule written for the test (lone masked cost over visible cost) had no resolution at 16 of 256, so the lone case is concluded from the time and fill differences to the removed case only.
185. **2026-11-16, model check, no GPU run and no refit: the performance model has no term for OptiX's per call SM overhead (about 4,000 cycles at one warp, F178); adding one of 9 to 28 cycles per ray (an assumed range, not measured at saturation) makes the existing OptiX speedup validations worse, and tmax is already in the replay.** The harness fitted constants are for the query only, and the overhead appears only indirectly in the RTNN refit's fixed part. Added as an equal additive term to both orders on the 4070 Ti bounce ray prediction (F113): the rms log error of the six speedups goes from 0.087 to 0.101 or 0.129 (100,000 triangles) and from 0.103 to 0.123 or 0.157 (3 million), because the Morton prediction gets closer (1.18 to 1.15 or 1.11 against 1.10) but the deep oracle orders, already too low, fall further. The random ray speedups (F100, F117), already too small, shrink further by an estimated 0.02 to 0.06 in log. For RTNN absolute times (F127) the term is 1 to 21 percent of the cycles and is absorbed by a refit of the fixed part; for 1 candidate point queries (F169) it would make the 2.8 times too high prediction 3.0 to 3.6 times unless the fixed part is lowered by the same amount. The replay already takes tmax per ray (the box test uses the interval from tmin to the nearest hit so far, started at tmax); it matches the recorder on the RTNN, kNN and Mochi rays that carry a short tmax, and was not run against F182. Details model/results_overhead_check.md. Not done: a measurement of the overhead at saturation (OptiX launch with an empty raygen against one without optixTrace), the replay on F182's tmax series.
186. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090), OptiX per call overhead at whole GPU load (predictions committed first, model/predictions_ovh.md, results model/results_ovh.md; OptiX only): at saturation optixTrace costs about 4 to 7 cycles per ray per SM more than the traversal unit alone on a one triangle scene (14.4 cycles per ray per SM against the harness unit's 7.1), about 6 percent of a random ray scene (124 cycles), so the per call overhead of F178 is mostly hidden at saturation, not on a trivial scene, and F185 (no per call term, no refit) stands.** A raygen of our own builds each ray from the launch index and traces it once or three times (no ray or result buffers), against a raygen with the same hash and no optixTrace in its code; sizes from 32 rays (one warp) to 8,388,608; one triangle (every ray hits) and the 100,000 soup. Warp instructions (ncu, 32 rays): the empty raygen 142, a call adds 503 (first) and 592 (each further one), so the issue bound of a call is 4.6 cycles per ray per SM. Per ray cost with the empty raygen removed is flat from about 64 warps per SM launched on (11.0 to 12.3 cycles on one triangle); one more warm call costs 13.2 cycles per ray per SM at saturation (7.1 unit plus about 6, the issue bound and a little). At one warp the same raygen is cheaper than F178's (first call 4,988 cycles, one more warm call 3,425; predicted 6,000 to 7,500 and 4,300 to 5,100, both missed low: F178's raygen also loaded rays and stored results), and the extra call's latency grows with load (3,425 at one warp to 6,386 saturated) because the issue slots are shared. Reading rule: o_sat (14.4 minus 7.1 = 7.3, or 4 if the unit runs at the ramp rate) is below the assumed 9 to 28, so the F185 conclusion holds: adding it would move the F113 rms log error from 0.087 to about 0.098 (interpolated from F185, not recomputed). Where it matters: scenes where the unit is almost idle (OptiX about two times the harness there). Other predictions missed: empty raygen 3.1 cycles per ray (under 1.5 predicted), one extra call 13.2 (7 to 12), soup 124 (130 to 190), total instructions per optixTrace raygen 675 (900 to 1,300). Not done: an OptiX ray set equal to the harness one, other scene sizes, a fixed SM clock.
187. **2026-11-16, tethys (RTX 4090), tmax replay check against F182 (predictions committed first, model/predictions_tmax_replay.md, results model/results_tmax_replay.md; CPU replay plus one OptiX build for the inputs): the replay carries the right shape of the tmax sweep only if nodes and leaf slots are weighted (about 2 and 10 requests each); counted equally it is 17 to 49 percent too high for tmax up to 0.1 (three triangle edges).** The replay (model/replay, nearest first, tmax per ray) on the same 100,000 soup GAS and the same 262,144 rays gives 12.9 nodes and 5.2 leaf slots entered per ray unlimited (predicted 45 to 100 requests worth: the measured 80.8 requests per ray are sectors, 4.5 per counted element). Unweighted time and request ratios match from tmax 0.2 up (within 7 percent, 3 percent for time) and miss below (replay ratio 0.38 against 0.26 at tmax 0, 0.51 against 0.39 at 0.05). The replay hit fraction is within 0.02 of the measured one at all ten values (0.01 low: its box matching misses the nearest hit in about 3.5 percent of rays). CORRECTION 2026-11-17: an earlier version of this finding reported the hardware hit fraction above exact geometry at short tmax; that came from a wrong hit fraction series in F182 (32 ray probe values, corrected there), and the hardware equals exact geometry on all 262,144 rays (F189). A two parameter weighting (2.2 per node, 9.9 per leaf slot) fits all ten measured request values within 3 percent, fitted afterwards on the same series, not a validation. Consequence: tmax was already in the replay (F185), the replay is usable for radius queries only with weights per type; not tested on RTNN, kNN or Mochi radii.

Every build passes check_seq (exactly one open, one macro fuse with the exact count, one close, no other TTU instruction) and the stage 0 builds (no TTU instruction) ran without error on one warp with both rays (hit and back faces), the stored registers equal the loaded words. 189. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), the hardware hit decision equals exact geometry on every ray at short tmax: the "extra hardware hits" of the first F187 text do not exist (prediction model/predictions_hwhits.md, result model/results_hwhits.md; OptiX plus CPU brute force).** F182's hit fraction series was a transcription error (values from the 32 ray probe launch; the 262,144 ray launch gives 0 / 0.038 / 0.075 / 0.175 / 0.314 / 0.509 / 0.754 / 0.823 / 0.829 / 0.829); F182, F187 and model/results_flags.md are corrected, the requests and times of F182 were right. Checked on all 262,144 rays at tmax 0.02, 0.1, 0.2 and 1 against a double precision brute force over the 100,000 triangles: hit decision, distance (1e-5 relative) and primitive agree on every ray, no extra and no missed hits, so there is no precision effect at the tmax limit at these scales. The replay's hit fraction is 0.01 low because its box matching assignment misses the nearest hit in about 3.5 percent of rays (F191 measures it: 0.4 percent of rays differ in the hit decision and 2.4 percent of hit rays in the primitive, not 3.5 percent; exact with the mode 1 assignment). 190. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), Flags_2 stage 1 (approved, 23 runs, no fault): our own TTU sequence with the computed flag words gives the OptiX outcome for terminate, cull back, cull front, cull opaque, cull non opaque and enforce any hit, but the zero ray mask did not reject the ray, and disable any hit changes the returned words (predictions committed first, model/predictions_flags2.md, results model/results_flags2.md).** The face rules match OptiX (cull back drops the ray that meets the back face, cull front the other; a culled ray returns the same all ones result words as a miss); cull non opaque misses a non opaque triangle, cull opaque does not; terminate and enforce any hit change nothing visible on one triangle. Masks 1, 15 and 128 hit (they intersect the instance mask 255, uninformative); mask 0 HIT where OptiX misses, so the ray mask words as computed do not make the unit reject against the identity instance (another word carries it, or OptiX rejects a zero mask before the unit): the mask words are not confirmed, run stopped there as instructed (mask 0 back ray not run). Disable any hit returns the same hit, primitive, u and v, but the distance word with its sign bit clear (+1.0 instead of -1.0) and a zero in the 64 bit pair that otherwise holds a non zero address like value: the unit appears to commit the hit as opaque without a candidate record.
