191. **2026-11-17, tethys, replay fix (CPU only, model/replay mode 1): the replay's nearest hit, hit decision and distance now equal the double precision brute force on every ray, by putting a triangle that no leaf slot contains into every leaf slot it overlaps.** The 10.5 percent of the 100,000 soup triangles that the builder split (no leaf slot box contains them) were assigned to one guessed slot; the nearest hit was then wrong or missing in 962 of the 262,144 rays (hit fraction 0.825 against 0.829, 2.4 percent of hits with the wrong primitive). With every overlapping slot (exact triangle and box test, 134,489 pairs instead of 101,582) the hit decision, primitive and distance agree on all 262,144 rays, and the hit fractions of the tmax sweep equal the measured series (0.038, 0.075, 0.175, 0.314, 0.509, 0.754, 0.823, 0.829). Cost: triangles tested per ray rise from 9.2 to 12.0 (a superset; the triangle count of the replay is not to be used), nodes and leaf slots entered fall by 1.4 and 2.3 percent (12.91 to 12.73 and 5.23 to 5.11), so the tmax comparison of F187 moves by 1 to 2.5 points at short tmax (replay 3 points higher than before at tmax 0; the conclusion and the weights stand). Earlier replay results (F125 to F167) were made with the old assignment and are not redone; for custom primitive trees the box assignment stays as it was (F166, F167). Mode 1 is opt in (ASSIGN_MODE=1).
192. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), the zero ray mask: OptiX rejects it in software before the traversal unit, the ray mask is otherwise only handed to the unit, and the instance mask test follows the documented AND (predictions committed first, model/predictions_mask_ias.md, results model/results_mask_ias.md).** Reading the dumped traversal code: the ray mask is used for the words handed to the unit and for tests that it is not zero (three places, one per traversal variant) guarding the call into the traversal body together with a test for a null handle; nothing in software combines it with an instance mask. With mask zero the call is skipped and the trace returns a miss without a single TTU instruction, which explains why our own sequence in F190 hit with mask zero (it has no such test and the unit did not reject a zero mask against the identity instance of a single GAS pipeline). OptiX only, one instance of visibility mask 0x0F in an IAS, 262,144 rays inside the triangle: ray masks 0xF0 and 0x10 miss, 0x01, 0x0F and 0xFF hit, 0x00 misses (every ray the same, hit fraction 0 or 1); an instance of 0xFF with ray mask 0x00 misses. All predictions held. The unit's own AND against the instance mask (child slot byte, F111) is therefore the only place it can happen for non zero masks; confirming it on the unit needs our own IAS sequence, which was prepared and then run (F195; my first text here said no IAS TTU sequence had ever run, which was wrong: F139 ran the same group on triton). The branch polarity of the software test is read from the control flow, not run in isolation.
193. **2026-11-17, existing data only: the DISABLE_ANYHIT result words fit "the distance sign marks a candidate that may need an any hit shader, and the pair is the hit instance word", with two gaps that no existing run closes.** Across the 23 Flags_2 stage 1 results, the sign of the distance word is set in every hit except the two with any hit disabled (positive distance), and the 64 bit pair is, in all 17 hits with any hit allowed, exactly the instance word we stored for the unit (so it is a pointer to the hit instance node, not a record the unit builds), and zero in the two disabled hits and the four misses. The leaf slot type byte has a bit that means "any hit may run" (clear for geometry built opaque, F111 notes), which is where the unit would take the same decision for opaque geometry. Not settled (opaque geometry was run later: F196 gives the same words; force opaque instance and a running any hit program remain): opaque geometry or a force opaque instance with default ray flags was never run with our sequence (the same sequence, only the build flag changes: the run that would separate "ray flag" from "needs any hit"), and a closest hit still needs the instance for its shader record while the pair is zero in the disabled case. Noted in notes/protocol.md (private).
194. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), Unit_1: at saturation the traversal unit's rate is a triangle rate, about 0.67 cycles per triangle (1.3 to 1.5 triangles per cycle) with a node costing about 1.3 cycles and a leaf slot nothing, not the step latencies divided by one capacity (predictions committed first, model/predictions_unit1.md, results model/results_unit1.md).** One SM, 32 warps, every lane the same ray that hits nothing, scenes of 1 to 4,096 stacked or overlapping triangles (1 to 140 nodes, 1 to 1,399 leaf slots entered per ray, GAS 2.3 to 269 KB), the checked Throughput_1 sequence, 22 stage 1 runs of 30 launches, no fault, no hang. Cycles per ray per SM (launch span over 1,024 rays): 8.8 (one triangle), 56 (64), 193 (256), 761 (1,024), 3,122 (4,096); a fit over the ten scenes that fit in the node state is 4.7 + 1.3 per node + 0.06 per leaf slot + 0.67 per triangle (largest residual 23 percent). The F67 step costs (343 per node, 113 per leaf, 52 per triangle) are therefore not rates: the overlap is 77 for triangles, 260 for nodes and unlimited for slots, so a single in flight capacity (142 or the 117 to 193 lanes of one warp latency over rate, which falls with query size) does not describe the unit; it is a triangle rate with a small node term. The overlapping scenes (more nodes and slots at the same triangles) cost only 15 percent more than the stacked ones, against 40 percent expected from latency steps. One warp latency stays on the F67 line within 15 percent for the stacked scenes up to 256 triangles (0.97 to 1.10) and falls to 0.87 at 4,096 and 0.83 on the overlapping ones. At 269 KB, past the 130 KB node state, the rate is the same 0.67 to 0.76 per triangle: no memory bound regime for coherent queries. CAVEAT (F197): every lane ran the same ray, and distinct rays cost 4.5 to 5.9 times more per ray, so these are rates for identical lanes. Not measured: misses in flight (needs distinct lines per lane and wide nodes, a real Unit_3), mixed rays in a lane group, queries that hit, triangles per slot above 2.9. Predictions held for H_T (triangle rate) within 25 percent, the fit intercept and triangle cost, the latency line and the no memory bound regime; missed for the latency over capacity hypothesis, the node and slot costs and the overlapping scene ratio; one prediction was typed wrongly (ratio of latency to 32 times the rate; the quantity meant, 117 to 193, held).
195. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), the unit itself does the ray mask AND instance mask test in the instance level (IAS) group, and rejects a zero ray mask there (predictions committed first, model/predictions_ias_opaque.md, results model/results_ias_opaque.md; approved by Jack).** The IAS traversal group of F139 (same group, same checked builds) with an instance of visibility mask 0x0F and the mask words of the first query computed for ray masks 0F, FF, 01, F0, 10, 00 (only those words and the host's instance mask change), one warp, one launch each, no fault, GPU idle after, stage 0 first: ray masks 0F, FF and 01 return the hit of F139 (u -0.25, v 0.5, distance word with the sign set, non zero instance pair), ray masks F0, 10 and 00 return the miss encoding (all result words ones, pair zero). All predictions held. So the unit takes the ray mask from the first query words and ANDs it with the instance's byte in the IAS child slot (F111); with a zero mask it rejects the ray itself in the IAS case, whereas in the single GAS case the identity instance had no such test (F190 mask 0 hit) and OptiX's software test (F192) is the only rejection. Correction of my own earlier text in F192 and the status files: they said no IAS sequence had ever run, which contradicted F139.

L2 to L1 sectors per ray beyond the setup baseline: 2.5, 16, 41, 59 (0.6 to 14.7 lines of 128 bytes): fills grow 20 times, time 1.5 times, so one SM is not memory bound here and the unit sustains at least 47 lines in flight (lower bound, Little with 285 cycles at 1,000,000): misses in flight are NOT determined, only bounded below. CORRECTION (F199): the one warp result (2.7 to 3.3 times the F67 line, lanes not served in parallel) was the warp waiting for its slowest lane, since the 32 rays had different counts; with equal counts the one warp latency of 32 distinct rays is on the F67 line. The rate result (4.5 to 5.9 times) stands.
198. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), model audit, no GPU run and no refit: every throughput and sharing constant of the model comes from distinct rays, every latency and unit rate constant from identical lanes, and the model's lane sharing term does not predict the 4.5 to 5.9 times of F197 (script model/audit_lane_sharing.py, table model/results_audit_lane_sharing.md).** From identical lanes (one ray in every lane): the F67 per node, slot and triangle costs, the 740 cycle fixed cost, the one SM rates of Throughput_1, the unit capacity and sibling gain (short and long queries), the F194 rate fit. From distinct rays: the Memory_4 fit (unit term, L1 and fill terms, queueing term, warps 4 to 16, about two queries per ray), the F123 sharing form (OptiX launches on the 4070 Ti), the RTNN refit of the fixed part. No constant links the two: the unit term fitted on distinct rays is applied to identical lanes. The F123 sharing term divides only the miss cost by the number of lane fetches per warp fetch, so it cannot change the unit term. On the F197 configurations (measured fills as the miss input, the best case for the model) the Memory_4 model predicts distinct over identical of 1.7 to 2.8 and the sharing form 1.05 to 2.1, against 4.5 to 5.9 measured; even with every fetch a miss the sharing form reaches only 1.9 to 3.1. The error is on the identical side: both predict 58 to 67 cycles per ray for identical lanes where 12 to 20 are measured. Caveat: 32 warps is outside the fitted range (4 to 16). One warp: the Memory_4 model gives 2,700 to 14,000 cycles for 32 distinct rays against 6,576 to 19,165 measured (the F67 line is right only for identical lanes). 199. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), lane groups sharing a ray: the unit's rate for distinct rays is 4.3 to 5.9 times the identical lane rate, it falls to the identical lane rate when a warp holds few distinct rays (a knee that depends on scene size), and the one warp latency does not depend on sharing (predictions committed first, model/predictions_lane_groups.md with a design addendum; results model/results_lane_groups.md).** One SM, 32 warps (rate) and 1 warp (latency), the checked Unit_1 sequence (no new TTU code), four soups of 1,000 to 1,000,000 triangles, rays that hit nothing (one query each), groups of 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 or 32 consecutive lanes sharing a ray, 104 stage 1 runs in two designs, no fault. The first design did not keep the counts per ray fixed (the first rays of each size differ a lot between group sizes), so it cannot test the question; the second uses 32 rays of one nodes, slots, triangles class per size. Second design, cycles per ray per SM, g = 1 to 32 lanes per ray: 1,000: 63.5 18.6 11.8 10.5 10.9 12.7; 10,000: 77.9 76.4 22.9 16.1 13.5 13.1; 100,000: 85.9 76.3 44.0 21.1 15.6 18.0; 1,000,000: 86.6 81.5 64.7 28.3 22.3 20.2. The cost stays high until a warp holds few enough distinct rays (the knee: between 32 and 16 distinct rays at 1,000, 16 and 8 at 10,000, 8 and 4 at 100,000 and 1,000,000), then sits at the identical lane floor; with two distinct rays per warp it equals the 32 lane floor within 15 percent at every size. The existing model fails: its prediction is a smooth fall to a floor three to five times too high (Memory_4 model within 30 percent at none of the groups, the F123 sharing form at 0 to 2 of 4); my own hypothesis that cost is linear in distinct rays per warp also fails its rule (2 of 4 sizes within 30 percent, needed 3), the real shape is a step. What the knee limits (distinct nodes, lines or rays, unit state or cache) is not determined; distinct node requests per warp near 100 to 190 fit by eye on four scenes only. One warp latency of 32 distinct rays is the same for every group size (within 7 percent) and on the F67 line for the counts (0.97 to 1.07); in the first design it was the F67 line of the slowest lane (0.76 to 1.03) and not of the mean counts (1.2 to 3.4 times), so the 2.7 to 3.3 times of F197 at one warp was a slowest lane effect and is withdrawn as a result about lane parallelism. The rate result of F197 reproduces with equal counts (4.3 to 5.9 times). Not done: warp counts other than 32 (every warp ran the same 32 rays), distinct rays on a shared path, hitting queries. CORRECTION (F201): in the first design the 1,024 ray file was the whole launch (each warp its own 32 rays), so the quoted chunk 0 counts were wrong and its counts per group size were within about 15 percent of each other; its group size 32 case (one ray per warp, 32 distinct rays across the SM) costs 1.1 to 2.2 times the second design's.
200. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), knee map: H (per ray cost depends on the distinct node lines a warp requests per step against a capacity) is wrong as a warp independent rule, but the knee tracks the mean distinct lines per step (about 10) when the unit is the bottleneck, which needs 16 or more warps; at 4 and 8 warps sharing does nothing (predictions committed first, model/predictions_knee.md; results model/results_knee.md).** Design B rays of F199 (same counts per ray), 4 soups of 1,000 to 1,000,000 triangles, groups of 1 to 32 lanes per ray, 4, 8, 16 and 32 warps, 96 stage 1 runs, no fault. Capacity calibrated on one configuration (100,000 triangles, 32 warps). Predictions: the primary form (maximum distinct lines per step, which is 32 over the group size at every size) gave 5 of 15 configurations within a factor 2 and cannot follow the size trend (knee measured 1.5 to 4.3 lanes per ray at 32 warps, predicted 3.4 everywhere); the mean per step 7 of 15; the whole traversal count 7 of 15 with the right trend; times the warp count 14 and 11 of 15 but the first has no size trend. No form passes the rules set beforehand. Measured: at 4 and 8 warps cost per ray is flat in the group size (within 10 percent), 4 warps run at 1.1 to 1.2 times one warp latency (latency bound); the penalty and the knee exist at 16 and 32 warps, with similar knees (0.75 to 0.97 times) rising with scene size. At the knee the mean distinct node lines per step is about 10 (sd 0.32 in log2, post hoc, 8 points, three statistics tried), the best of the statistics, and it follows the size trend. So H holds in part (a capacity of about 10 lines per step, mean over steps) and only when the unit is the bottleneck. Not determined: a warp count between 8 and 16 (F201: the knee is already there at 12), what the capacity is (queue, merge window, bank), distinct rays on a shared path; all warps run the same rays. The performance model range line (notes) now states it is validated for distinct, workload like rays and not for lanes sharing work (F198).
201. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), knee blind test: the hypothesis "knee at about 10 mean distinct node lines per step per warp, unit bound regime from 16 warps" locates the knee on two new scene sizes but is wrong about the regime and is not a cost law (predictions committed first, model/predictions_knee2.md; results model/results_knee2.md; unit thread closed).** Two new sizes (30,000 and 300,000 triangles), 12 warps on six sizes, and a shifted variant (the same distinct rays with half of each group of lanes starting one tree level later, using the harness start node words; the checked sequence, no new TTU code), 120 stage 1 runs, no fault. New sizes at 16 and 32 warps: the knee is within a factor 2 of the prediction in all four (measured 2.4 to 4.1 lanes per ray against 2.7 and 2.8). 12 warps: predicted no knee, measured a knee at five of six sizes, so the unit bound regime starts at 12 warps or lower (flat at 8 in F200). Shifted variant: with eight distinct rays per warp and the shifted lanes (double the distinct lines per step) the cost is as high as for two lanes per ray (75 to 78 against 38 to 55 unshifted), with four distinct rays it is as low as unshifted (20 to 27): six of twelve scored configurations within 25 percent of the hypothesis, five closer to it than to a distinct rays rule (needed nine): not held. At the same mean of 7.1 lines per step an unshifted and a shifted configuration differ 1.5 to 2 times, so both distinct rays and lines per step matter. Closing text in notes/performance_model.md: the model has no term for this; a table of measured ladders is the only description (one SM, no hit rays of 5 to 15 nodes). CORRECTION of my own earlier text (F199, F200): the harness gives each warp its own 32 rays of a file, so in the first design of F199 the whole 1,024 ray file was in the launch and its group size 32 case had 32 distinct rays across the SM (1.1 to 2.2 times the second design's group size 32, which gives every warp the same 32 rays); my sentence that only chunk 0 was timed and its counts were wrong. The unit cost depends on distinct rays across warps too.
202. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), Cache_1 and Cache_2 (plain CUDA pointer chase, no TTU; predictions committed first, model/predictions_cache.md; results model/results_cache.md): L1 sector 32 bytes, tag line 128 bytes, effective L1 capacity about 96 KB with a soft edge, no way count found in L1 or L2, and no near and far L2 split but a graded per line latency.** L1: a sequential cyclic scan with 4, 8, 16, 32 byte stride misses 1/8, 1/4, 1/2, all of the time (sector 32 bytes); the capacity edge is the same at strides 32, 64 and 128 (about 100 KB where half miss), so the tag line is 128 bytes, and at stride 256 it is at twice the footprint, the same number of lines, so power of two strides lose nothing to set conflicts. The edge is soft: no miss to 88 KB, half at 100 to 104 KB, 85 to 89 percent over a wide band and all misses from 124 KB, not the sharp edge of a plain LRU; the effective capacity is about 96 KB of the 128 KB. Associativity could not be measured: up to 128 addresses at strides from 128 bytes to 1 MB never conflict (more than 128 ways or a hashed set index), and 600 to 1,100 lines at any stride show the knee at the capacity. Replacement therefore not determined. L2: no conflict knee for 160 addresses at strides up to 2 MB; the per line hit latency over 16 MB is 291 to 352 cycles (median 319), every line within 15 percent of the median, reproducible per line (r 0.95 between two access orders) and independent of the address range: several levels 6 to 11 cycles apart (a graded spread of 17 percent, slice distance), not two modes. A timing artifact (1 in 8 accesses at 575 to 620 cycles, tied to the position in the access order, not the line) was found and excluded from the statistics. Predictions: held for the capacity range, the tag line, hashed or no clean associativity, the L2 spread and no L2 conflict knee; missed for the sharp edge, the stride 256 wording and a clean way count; replacement not testable. Not done: node state (needs custom BVHs), L2 replacement and the triton ramp, other GPUs.
203. **2026-11-17, triton (RTX 6000 Ada), Cache_1 and Cache_2 repeated from F202 (plain CUDA pointer chase, no TTU; results model/results_cache_triton.md): every L1 and L2 result of the 4090 repeats within a few percent on the second AD102 board.** Same 32 byte L1 sector, same 128 byte tag line, the same soft L1 capacity edge (no miss to 88 KB, half way at about 97 to 104 KB, full miss from 124 to 128 KB, the same footprint at strides 32, 64 and 128 and about twice the footprint at stride 256, i.e. the same number of lines), no L1 way count found (no conflict for up to 128 addresses at any stride from 128 bytes to 1 MB), no L2 conflict knee (up to 160 addresses, strides 4 KB to 2 MB), and a graded per line L2 hit latency of 294 to 366 cycles (median 325, all lines within 15 percent of it, reproducible per line between two access orders at r 0.96 over about 100,000 lines, no dependence on the address). Differences are small: L1 hit 39.9 against 40, L2 hit about 2 percent slower (292 against 285 on a 1 MB scan, 325 against 319 median per line). The 1 in 8 timing artifact found in F202 is present here at exactly 12.5 percent in both orders, so it belongs to the program on both chips. Not measured: L2 replacement and the ramp beyond the cache size, node state, io (the 4070 Ti run is prepared as a script, not run).
204. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), spheres built in, custom and tessellated (OptiX only, no TTU code; predictions model/predictions_spheres.md committed first with a labeled addendum, results model/results_spheres.md): the built in sphere runs on the SM and costs the same as a custom primitive; tessellated spheres (20 and 80 triangles) gave no speedup on random rays.** Four representations of the same point sets: built in spheres (S), custom primitives with our ray sphere program (C), and inscribed icospheres (T0 20, T1 80 triangles per sphere). Controlled line scene (D1): N spheres along an axis ray, every box crossed, no hit. S: one warp 1,341 cycles per candidate, saturation 3.45 cycles per ray per SM; C: 1,226 and 3.00; ncu: 215 to 245 (S) and 190 to 230 (C) warp instructions per extra candidate, so the built in sphere is an SM program on Ada (the Feature_8 hypothesis), not in the RT core, and not faster than a custom primitive (0 to 15 percent slower per candidate here, 3 to 16 percent faster on random rays). The triangle arms use no SM instructions per sphere. Their cost per sphere depends on how many triangle boxes the ray reaches: with the axis 1.27 r from the centres 0.04 and 0.38 cycles (saturation) because the tree is over triangles and no box reaches the ray (my first design gave them no work; stated, predictions for them missed), with the axis 1.018 r away (near miss, second design, predicted first) 4.5 (T0) and 7.2 (T1) against 2.8 to 3.1 for C and S at saturation, and 540 and 850 against 1,180 to 1,310 cycles at one warp. Random rays on the point set (D2, r 0.02, 0.04, 0.08): cycles per ray per SM S 84, 98, 222; C 86, 103, 263; T0 84, 110, 282; T1 100, 118, 249; tessellation does not pay (T0 0.98 to 1.07 times C), loses 7 percent of the hits at r 0.02 (median hit distance error 0.2 r for T0, 0.06 r for T1) and needs 25 to 100 times the BVH memory (24.8 and 99.4 MB against 0.7 to 1.0 MB). Prediction scoring: SM residency, the ncu ranges and the one warp slopes of S and C held; every saturation slope was too high by 2 to 5 times (I used 450 cycles per warp call, F180; the measured warp call costs about 96 SM cycles when the 32 lanes call together), the intercepts and the D2 ordering and growth with radius missed. Caveats: one run per cell; residency read from counts and times, not from the OptiX code; lanes that diverge in the tree pay more per call (not isolated); inscribed tessellation only; BVHs below the L2; the 4090 only.
205. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), held out graphics scenes, F123 form with no refit (predictions model/predictions_heldout.md committed before any ordered launch was timed; results model/results_heldout.md): pooled error 0.091 over 18 points on three scenes never used before, against 0.120 for the Memory_4 form; Sponza 0.083 and Conference 0.014 pass the 0.12 rule, Rungholt 0.132 fails it.** Crytek Sponza (262,266 triangles, BVH 16 MB), Conference Room (124,619, 8 MB), Rungholt (6.7 million, 416 MB, 5.8 times the L2), 8.4 million random rays each, six orders (Morton, oracle depth 1 to full) against the random order, no wrong result in 21 launches. Measured gains: Sponza 1.04 to 1.08, Conference 1.05 to 1.07, Rungholt 1.29 to 1.68. The F123 form over predicts Sponza by 0.08 (all six orders, same sign) and gets Conference right (the Memory_4 form wrongly predicts 1.20 for Morton, measured 1.06); on Rungholt it under predicts all five oracle orders (mean signed -0.124) while keeping the order of the orders and the Morton gain (1.33 against 1.37); the form has no DRAM term, the likely cause, not tested. My written expectation that Conference would fail was wrong. Caveats: on Sponza and Conference the measured spread is only 0.06, so 0.083 is above what a constant 1.0 would score there (0.062); random rays only (camera and bounce rays not tested here); parameters fitted on the 4070 Ti and applied to the 4090 through SM count and L2 size only; one run per order, 10 launches each.
206. **2026-11-17, framing records, no new run: sphere to triangle is a negative Paper 2 result so far; the 8 percent is a speedup error, not an absolute time error; 875 cycles is a one warp latency.** (1) Sphere to triangle (Feature_8, F204): on random rays the built in sphere runs on the SM and costs the same as a custom primitive, and tessellated spheres (20 and 80 triangles) are no faster (0.98 to 1.07 times the custom primitive), lose 7 percent of hits at the smallest radius and need 25 to 100 times the BVH memory. The rule "replace spheres by triangles" is therefore NOT supported on random rays on the 4090. Triangles can cost more or less per sphere depending on how many triangle boxes a ray reaches (far misses almost free, near misses 4.5 to 7.2 cycles against 3 at saturation); a kNN style workload and Blackwell hardware spheres are untested and could still change this. (2) The 0.08 of F123 and 0.09 of F205 are rms log errors of predicted speedup over the natural ray order. Against a model that always predicts no change the score is: held out F123 sets 0.094, 0.074, 0.072 against 0.702, 0.244, 0.318 (pooled 0.080 against 0.467); F205 Sponza 0.083 against 0.062 (worse than no change, the gain there is only 1.04 to 1.08), Conference 0.014 against 0.064, Rungholt 0.132 against 0.421 (pooled 0.091 against 0.248). No finding validates the absolute traversal time of a whole workload to better than 16 to 89 percent on the whole GPU (Throughput_6) or a log error of 0.4 to 0.6 for custom primitives (F127); absolute time is validated within a few percent only for latency chains and one SM throughput (F176), and within 11 to 19 percent for one SM held out scenes (Memory_4). (3) The 875 cycles per custom primitive candidate (F49) is the latency one warp waits; at saturation a warp call costs about 96 SM cycles and a candidate 3.0 cycles per ray per SM (custom) or 3.45 (built in sphere), about 9 times less. Details in notes/performance_model.md (private).
207. **2026-11-17, triton (RTX 6000 Ada), prior works (RTNN, TrueKNN, Arkade) on datasets we have, predictions committed first (model/priorworks/predictions_priorworks.md, commit 7b0a6c4; results model/priorworks/results_priorworks.md): the model misses by 1.7 to 2.5 times on RTNN, by 0.6 to 160 times on TrueKNN and by 3.8 to 7.7 times on the fixed Arkade, and on this build the original versions cost 5 to 6,000 times the device buffer versions (F216 softened this; F218 reinstates it as a statement about this system only: the two builds return identical neighbours and time the same thing, but the papers' times do not show the same cost, so it is not a claim about the papers' program, and the cause, paging of the managed buffer, was not isolated).** Programs built outside git (RTNN, the OWL port of TrueKNN, Arkade with the earlier patches), one GPU, OptiX only, three runs per case, no fault. The papers state no radius, K or query count for RTNN, no k for Arkade's main results and no radius code for Arkade, so those were chosen and written down before any run (the only RTNN radius mentioned, 0.1 on a 131 wide Buddha, scaled to each file; k 50; 10,000 queries; the radius for Arkade is the first doubling at which 99 percent of the queries have 50 neighbours). Datasets used: Stanford bunny (362,272 points) and dragon (3.6M) for RTNN; 3DRoad (434,874), Porto first 1M points, uniform 1M for TrueKNN; Gowalla (1,256,693 unique places against 1,270,969 in the paper), Glove 3D (1,193,514, PCA of the 25 d Twitter vectors, our inference) and Manuscript (2,155,617) for Arkade. RTNN (no fix applies: its output buffer is already in device memory and its distance is float): measured over predicted 2.5, 2.2, 2.5 and 1.7, all four above the prediction, three of four outside the factor 2 rule. (F220: the measured time is the program's search compute time, which includes allocating and filling the output buffer on the host side of the launch; against the launch time itself all four rows are inside the factor 2.) TrueKNN rounds were predicted exactly in all four cases (13, 13, 20, 5) but time was not: fixed version launch time over prediction 3.1 (road, k 5), 162 (road, k 659: the rule has no term for the cost of keeping k sorted neighbours, which at k 659 is the whole cost), 91 (Porto: twenty rounds, many with one active query that sees up to all points in its box) and 0.56 (uniform), so only one of four passes. Original TrueKNN (managed memory frame buffer): the launches themselves are 58 to 6,200 times slower than with a device buffer (24 s against 17.6 ms on 3DRoad k 5; 58 s against 9 ms on uniform 1M), (likely the GPU paging the managed buffer in from the host, not tested); k 659 on the original did not finish in 300 s. Arkade, event time of the launch: fixed over predicted 3.8, 4.0 and 7.7, original (pinned output, double precision distance) over predicted 1.3, 1.7 and 3.2; original over fixed is 4.7 to 5.8 against 11 predicted from one uniform 2D set, and the neighbour sets are identical in all 30,000 queries. CORRECTION (F216): the Arkade rows used the L2 norm and raw latitude and longitude for Gowalla, which are not what the paper's tables use, so they cannot be compared with the paper. The throughput form works for the original better than for the fixed version; the fixed version is limited by something the model does not have at these launch sizes. Not tested: the papers' own other datasets (Cali OSM, Kitti 4M, Randnet, Gbif, Millennium, KITTI frames, 3DIono, Mochi meshes are not available), Arkade with other norms, and TrueKNN with k about the square root of the size at 1M points. CLOVER (a different method, no prediction) builds with CMake 3.27 or newer and runs its own test; its test code returns success before checking any result.
208. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), held out scenes part 2, camera and bounce rays on Sponza, Conference and Rungholt, F123 form with no refit, against the baseline "predict 1.0" (predictions model/predictions_scenes.md committed before any ordered launch was timed; results model/results_scenes.md): pooled error 0.061 over 42 points against 0.087 for no change and 0.088 for the Memory_4 form; every set passes the 0.12 rule, but the model beats no change clearly on only one set.** Camera rays (4096 x 2048, pixel order is the natural order) and one bounce from their closest hits, 8.4 million rays per set, 100 percent hits, seven orders (shuffled, Morton, oracle depth 1 to full) against the pixel order, 10 launches each, 0 wrong results. Errors (F123 / no change): Conference camera 0.086 / 0.091, bounce 0.055 / 0.061; Sponza camera 0.051 / 0.065, bounce 0.031 / 0.021 (worse than no change: measured gains 1.00 to 1.02, predicted 1.05); Rungholt camera 0.053 / 0.070, bounce 0.075 / 0.153 (the one set where sorting pays, measured 1.03 to 1.24). Camera: pooled 0.066 / 0.076 (Memory_4 0.081), bounce 0.057 / 0.096 (Memory_4 0.096). Where it is wrong: shuffling a coherent camera order costs 0.79 to 0.84 times (Conference, Sponza, Rungholt) and the form predicts 0.95 to 0.99 (log error +0.13 to +0.22, the largest errors); Rungholt bounce under predicts the deep oracle orders (measured 1.24, predicted 1.10, error growing with depth from -0.047 to -0.125, same sign as the random rays of F205); Morton of the ray direction loses 4 percent on camera rays where the model says nothing. On the sorted order points of the five easy sets the measured speedups are 0.96 to 1.08, so a constant 1.0 is nearly as good there: the model's advantage comes from the shuffled orders and Rungholt bounce. Speedup only, not absolute time; one camera and one bounce per scene; parameters from the 4070 Ti applied to the 4090 through SM count and L2 size; the pixel order of camera rays is already coherent, so the gain side is weakly tested.
209. **2026-11-17, tethys, Rungholt cause test: the replay's DRAM traffic feature does NOT explain the Rungholt under prediction of F205, and the feature is not a usable traffic estimate (CPU only on F205 data, plan and pass rule committed before the comparison: model/predictions_rungholt_dram.md; results model/results_rungholt_dram.md).** The residual (measured over predicted speedup, log) is +0.12, +0.16, +0.17, +0.16, +0.11 for oracle depth 1 to full and +0.03 for Morton: it rises with depth and falls again at the full order, while the DRAM traffic reduction keeps growing, so the rank correlation is -0.10 and one slope through the origin leaves 0.119 leave one out error (rule: at most 0.06); the order index and the L1 miss reduction do no better (0.089 and 0.107). Parameter free check: bytes per launch implied by the feature divided by the DRAM bandwidth is LONGER than the measured launch for five of six orders (1.06 to 1.41 times), which is impossible, because the feature counts every SM's cold and overflow node fetches as DRAM although SMs share lines through the 72 MB L2. My expectation (floor 10 to 40 percent of the launch) was wrong for that reason. Verdict: "missing DRAM term" is not supported by this feature; whether DRAM plays a role is untested (would need measured DRAM bytes per order, for example ncu on the 4090, and a model with the true L2 hit share).
210. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), Sched_1, which ready warp issues (plain CUDA, no TTU; predictions model/predictions_sched.md committed first; results model/results_sched.md): warp id mod 4 picks the scheduler; two or three ready warps on one scheduler share it equally in strict alternation; with four or more stall free warps the three highest warp ids run first and the others starve until they finish; ties after the block barrier go to the highest id for three or more warps.** One block of up to 32 warps, clock read after each segment of FMAs, 40 launches per configuration. One warp alone issues one FMA per cycle (40.0 cycles per 32 FMA segment including a 9 cycle clock read and store; 4 cycles per FMA on a dependent chain). Warps 0 and 1 do not slow each other, warps 0 and 4 do (70 cycles). Four warps (ids 0,4,8,12) on one scheduler: 12, 8, 4 take about 105 cycles per segment and finish together after about 6,700 cycles, warp 0 makes almost no progress until then (first segment end at 7,300 cycles); eight warps run in groups of three by descending id. With dependent chains (a stalled warp every 4 cycles) four warps all run (1.11 times alone) and eight warps cost 2.0 to 2.3 times, no starvation; with 8 FMAs per segment starvation is small; with 64 FMAs per segment the rotating group is two warps. Predictions: scheduler map, one FMA per cycle and the dependent chain results held; "lowest id first" (missed: highest id for three or more warps), "greedy runs of the same warp" (missed: alternation) and the absolute segment times missed. Not found: the mechanism (a priority by warp id with a small rotating group fits, the group size changes with code length). One code shape, SM 0 only, no stalls from memory.
211. **2026-11-17, tethys, Sched_2 (priority of warps returning from the RT core): the first design was invalid and nothing is concluded.** The checked Unit_1 TTU builds (one TTU warp, 5 repetitions of 30 launches, stage 0 and stage 1, with and without the harness ALU compute kernel, no fault) gave 3,586 and 3,590 cycles (stage 1) and 80 and 80 (stage 0), but the harness reports no compute iterations in any measured launch after the first, and the first launch waited 24 seconds, the compute kernel's own watchdog: the one block TTU kernel did not start beside the persistent compute kernel (same with a smaller compute grid; cause not found), so the "with compute" arms had no load. A design with ready ALU warps in the same block or a launch order that lets the TTU block start is needed. The extra delay of +4 cycles is noise, not a measured priority.
212. **2026-11-17, tethys, knee term against the camera shuffle penalty (CPU only, existing data, no refit; prediction committed first: model/predictions_knee_shuffle.md, commit df8d010; script model/predict_knee_shuffle.py): adding the F201 capacity (10 distinct node lines per warp step) to the F123 form cuts the camera shuffle error from 0.162 to 0.094 (mean absolute log error, three scenes), misses my 0.08 pass line, and ruins the bounce sets (0.019 to 0.423).** Form fixed before its numbers were computed, no free parameter: h = mean distinct node lines per warp step = 32 over the replay's raw to deduplicated line ratio (an upper bound, every lane active); K(h) = max(1, h / 10); predicted speedup of the shuffled order = F123 prediction x K(natural) / K(shuffled). Disclosure: the measured shuffled speedups (F208: camera 0.789, 0.843, 0.839) were known to me before the form was fixed. Camera: h natural 2.1, 1.7, 1.2 (below the capacity) and shuffled 11.6, 13.1, 10.6 (just above), so the form predicts 0.853, 0.739, 0.901 against measured 0.789, 0.843, 0.839 (F123 0.987, 0.964, 0.954): log errors +0.078, -0.132, +0.072 against +0.223, +0.134, +0.128; closer on two of three scenes, Sponza now over penalised. Bounce: natural orders are already at 10 to 14 lines per step and shuffled 18 to 19, so the form gives 0.551, 0.754, 0.513 against measured 0.975, 0.962, 0.808 (F123 0.945, 0.947, 0.817): worse on all three, as I expected (no saturation above the knee). Expectations: closer than F123 on at least two of three camera sets and below F123's mean error HELD (probability 65 percent); pass at 0.08 MISSED (0.094); bounce worse on all three HELD. Reading: the knee locates why coherent camera order is cheap (a warp's lanes share nodes) and roughly sizes the penalty of destroying it, but as a multiplicative term over the whole launch it is not a usable model term (overshoots when lines per step are already above the capacity). Caveats: capacity from one SM, unit bound, no hit rays (F200, F201); applied to whole GPU launches of real scenes; lines per step counts every lane active; not tested with lane efficiency.
213. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), Rungholt DRAM bytes per ray order with ncu (OptiX only; predictions model/predictions_rungholt_ncu.md committed first, commit 7c89b00; results model/results_rungholt_ncu.md): the natural order is DRAM bandwidth bound (85.8 percent of peak), sorting cuts DRAM reads 2.45 times at once and then not at all across oracle depths, and measured DRAM bytes do not explain the F205 residual as a depth trend.** Same 8.4 million random rays and seven orders as F205 (rebuilt, equal hit fraction, triangle count and GAS size), one ncu launch per order. DRAM read / write MB and ncu ms: natural 985 / 270 / 1.488; oracle depth 1, 2, 3, 4, full 401 / 269 / 1.147, 401 / 268 / 1.005, 402 / 268 / 0.934, 402 / 268 / 0.890, 402 / 268 / 0.884; Morton 510 / 269 / 1.081; ncu durations equal the F205 times within 1 percent. 538 MB of every launch is ray traffic (268 MB of ray reads, 268 to 270 MB of results written) that neither the replay feature nor the model has: 0.53 ms at the full 1,008 GB/s. The F209 feature over estimates the bytes (measured read over feature 0.27 to 0.70). Expectations: read below the feature, natural ratio 0.3 to 0.8, bandwidth fraction 0.25 to 0.85, natural over full 1.5 to 4 HELD; my 35 percent case "at least 60 percent of peak = bandwidth bound" HAPPENED; monotone fall with depth MISSED (flat 401 to 402 MB from depth 1); the F209 rule with measured bytes FAILED (Spearman 0.10 against 0.8, Morton residual -0.075 against 0.04): the leave one out error of 0.028 is an artifact of a constant regressor (log reduction 0.897 to 0.898 for all five oracle orders). Reading, marked as such: DRAM traffic explains the STEP between natural and sorted orders (584 MB, about 0.58 ms of bandwidth time, the direction of the residual 0.11 to 0.17) but not how the gain grows with depth (time falls 1.147 to 0.884 ms at constant DRAM reads while the L2 hit rate falls from 90.9 to 72.1 percent), and not Morton (510 MB). No model with a DRAM term and the ray traffic was fitted (a separate labeled step). Caveats: one launch per order (no noise estimate), ncu serialisation, 4090 only.
214. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), Sched_2 redone with ALU warps in the TTU block (build_patch --aluwarps; predictions model/predictions_sched2.md, commits a9b2e7e and 8e02eac, before any stage 1 launch; results model/results_sched2.md): ready ALU warps on the TTU warp's own scheduler lengthen its 3,569 cycle query window by about 110 to 150 cycles per ALU warp for the lowest id and 46 to 60 for the highest id, with no starvation and no return priority; ALU warps on the other schedulers do nothing.** One block on one SM, one TTU warp (checked Unit_1 sequence, one TTUOPEN, TTUMACROFUSE 17, one TTULD.CLOSE) and W-1 ALU warps of 192 independent FFMA per loop iteration that start 480 cycles after it; smoke gate passed before any timed launch (ncu FFMA warp instructions 108,340 against 108,288 expected, ALU windows overlap the whole TTU window); 9 arms x 5 x 30 launches, no fault. Porto cause found in the data: round 20 has one active query with about 995,000 candidates and takes 1,036 of the 1,069 ms, one lane working through its candidates one after another at about 1.04 microseconds (about 2,600 cycles) each; a latency chain, which a throughput form cannot have. 217. **2026-11-17, tethys (RTX 4090), bandwidth bound (time at least bytes per launch over measured DRAM bandwidth), scored alone and combined with the current model on the Rungholt orders, the F205 and F208 scenes and the prior works rows (predictions committed first: model/predictions_bound_bw.md, commit f9e48b7; script model/bound_bw.py, results model/results_bound_bw.md): with the replay's node bytes the bound is not a bound (above the measured time in every Rungholt cell) and makes the prediction worse; nowhere does a valid bound bind; the model's misses are not a bandwidth effect.** Plain CUDA copy of 268 MB in and 268 MB out with the L2 flushed: 901 GB/s (89 percent of the 1,008 GB/s peak, prediction 850 to 930 held), read only 787, write only 934. Bytes per launch = node traffic (the replay DRAM feature of F209) + 32 B per ray read + 32 B per ray written. Model time of an order = measured natural time over the F123 speedup. Errors are the rms log error of the speedup over the natural order. Rungholt random orders: F123 0.132, bound alone 0.153, max of the two 0.153 (the bound is 1.4 to 2.1 times the measured time in all six orders, because the replay feature over estimates DRAM bytes, F213); pooled F205 0.091 / 0.102 / 0.101; pooled F208 0.061 / 0.126 / 0.079. The bound binds only in Rungholt cells (10 of 60), all of them invalid. Scenes that fit in L2 have only the ray traffic (537 MB, 0.60 ms), equal to predicting no change for the speedup, and it is 0.60 to 0.89 of the measured time, so it is a real floor that is nearly reached on camera ray sets (Conference camera 0.89) but it never exceeds the model's time. Prior works rows (device buffer versions, queries and results only, no node traffic, so a lower bound; triton bandwidth assumed as 960 GB/s times the 4090 copy fraction, not measured there): the bound is 0.00 to 0.29 of the measured time and 0.00 to 0.73 of the F207 prediction, it binds in 0 of 11 rows (prediction held); TrueKNN k 659 moves at least 24.8 GB, 29 ms of bandwidth against 87 s measured, so F207's misses are not a bandwidth effect. Diagnostic in sample (labeled, with the DRAM bytes ncu measured in F213): the bound is valid (0.64 to 0.94 of the measured time; the natural Rungholt order runs at 94 percent of the copy bandwidth), binds in 0 of 6 orders, max form equals F123 (0.132), bound alone 0.214. Written expectations: bandwidth, prior works, Rungholt validity and "max form does not help" held; the Conference camera bound fraction (0.89 against my 0.55 to 0.8) and the "pooled change under 0.005" parts missed; the pass line for "useful model term" failed. Reading: the idea is sound for the natural Rungholt launch but needs the DRAM bytes of the actual launch, which the replay's cache model does not give. Caveats: one bandwidth run on one GPU (a streaming rate; random node reads may reach less); TrueKNN 8 bytes per neighbour assumed; the F205 and F208 times were known before the predictions.

221. **2026-11-17, triton, CLOVER bitonic: the wrong neighbours are real (not our checker), the cause is one line, and a one line fix gives zero wrong queries at every K tried; warpwise and hubs are right at K up to 128.** A second checker rules out the failure modes of the first: ties (a returned point counts as right if tied with the true K-th distance within 1e-6 relative), duplicate points (0 in both data sets, counted), repeated or out of range indices (0), and its tolerance. At K = 32 on 20,000 uniform points 0 queries have a boundary tie and all 20,000 are wrong (recall of the true top 32 is 0.13); on 20,000 bunny points the wrong queries are 19,880 (K 32), 18,959 (K 64), 15,841 (K 128), 9,920 (K 256), of which only 265, 167, 191 and 63 have a boundary tie. A second implementation (python, scipy kd tree, no shared code) gives the same 19,880, 18,959 and 15,841. Right (0 wrong) at K 8, 31, 33, 96, 100, 160, 192 on both sets. Cause, found by reading the source and then confirmed by a patch: the pruning threshold is read from lane K mod 32 of the last round's register array, which is the K-th best element only by luck; when K is a multiple of 32 and the template rounds equal K over 32 (K = 32, 64, 128, 256) it is lane 0 of the last round, element K minus 32, so good candidates are skipped; at K = 96 the switch uses 4 rounds, at 160 and 192 8 rounds, and the same expression reads a later element, a looser threshold that is only slower. The reading also gave 192 right and 256 wrong, which the runs matched (that prediction was made in the session before the runs and was not committed). With the threshold read from element K minus 1 (our patch, tested only, not the authors' code) bitonic has 0 wrong queries at K 31, 32, 33, 64, 96, 128, 160, 192 and 256 on both sets. warpwise and hubs: 0 wrong at K 32, 64, 128 (hubs also 256); warpwise at K 256 returns garbage on both sets (cause not looked at). The CLOVER mesh runs use K 128, so bitonic there skipped candidates; whether the paper's timings are affected is not known. A note for the authors is in notes/CLOVER_bitonic_note.md (not sent). Baseline for any CLOVER comparison: warpwise and hubs.

226. **2026-11-17, triton (RTX 6000 Ada), the dependent global loads inside the Arkade callback do not slow down at the paper's launch size (10,000 queries) but do from about 6 warps per SM and under a DRAM heavy neighbour; the 2.5 to 3.2 times per call gap of F220 is not load (predictions committed first, 00bd6b5; results model/priorworks/results_arkade_callback_loads.md).** Float build, device buffer, k 50, clock64 inside the intersection program around (a) the load of the primitive data (depends on the primitive index the hardware hands over) and (b) the scan of the 50 result slots on the payload (49 dependent compare loads), Manuscript and Gowalla, 32 to 160,000 queries (1 to 12 warps per SM), without and with a pointer chase kernel (a separate stream, one chain per thread over 1 GiB; 18 blocks or 144 blocks of 128 threads) started after the warm up launch, 3 runs each, 120 runs, no fault. Unloaded, one warp: (a) about 500 cycles, (b) about 3,400 (about 70 cycles per dependent load), one whole warp call about 4,000 to 4,300 cycles, of which (a) + (b) are 72 to 87 percent. At 10,000 queries (3.3 warps per SM, the paper's size) the whole call is 1.15 (Manuscript) and 1.03 (Gowalla) times the single warp value; at 40,000 queries (6 to 7 warps per SM) 1.4 to 2.1 times; at 160,000 (12 warps per SM) 2.2 to 2.6 times, almost all in (b) (2.4 to 2.8 times; (a) 1.0 to 1.6). With the light chase (18 blocks) (a) is 2.0 to 2.7 times at up to 10,000 queries and (b) 1.05 to 1.2 times (same size run without the chase); with the saturating chase (144 blocks, DRAM full, chase 6,300 cycles per step) (a) is 9 to 12 times and (b) 1.4 to 10 times the run without the chase. Written predictions: (a) at 32 queries (250 to 500), 10,000 queries and the light chase held; (b) 800 to 2,000 cycles missed (3,400), (a) + (b) share 30 to 60 percent missed (72 to 87), whole call growth at 10,000 queries 1.3 to 3 times missed (1.03 to 1.15), the chase effect on (b) under 20 percent held only for the light chase. Answer: loaded growth of the callback exists (and could matter for much larger launches or with other kernels beside it) but at 10,000 queries the float call is already 4,000 cycles unloaded, mostly the scan of the payload, which is what F220's per call factor against the model measured (the model's unloaded constant was too low for the float call, not the load). Caveats: the probes are inside the numbers (the build without the segment probes has warp calls 0.84 to 1.14 of the probe build, no direction); one GPU; float build only; the chase is a crude load; why a payload load costs 70 cycles was not isolated.
227. **2026-11-17, triton (RTX 6000 Ada), a non RT baseline for the prior works rows: CLOVER (linear scan kNN on the GPU) timed on the same datasets, and its pruned variant (hubs) is as fast as or faster than the RT methods in most of the rows, while the plain linear scan (warpwise) is 30 to 600 times slower (predictions committed first, f890048; results model/priorworks/results_clover_vs_rt.md).** CLOVER warpwise, hubs and bitonic (stock and with our one line patch from F221) on 3DRoad, Porto first 1M and uniform 1M at k 5 and bunny and dragon at k 50, every point a query, CLOVER's own timer, 1,000 sampled queries checked against a CPU brute force: 0 wrong in every run and variant (stock bitonic is right at these k). Times: warpwise 7.0 s (road), 37.8 s (Porto), 38.8 s (uniform), 6.1 s (bunny), about 627 s (dragon, from 100,000 queries); hubs 34, 94, 90, 31 and 410 ms; bitonic 322, 2,407, 2,708, 360 ms and 32.5 s. Against the RT rows: TrueKNN (k 5) is 82, 29 and 597 times faster than warpwise by wall time, but hubs is 2.5 times faster than TrueKNN's wall time on road and 13.6 times on Porto, and TrueKNN is only 1.4 times faster by wall on uniform (its launches alone are 1.9 and 9.6 times faster than hubs on road and uniform, 11 times slower on Porto), and 3.4 to 7.9 percent of its queries have a wrong slot while CLOVER's were all right. RTNN at a radius with 50 neighbours (checked with a CPU scan): launch 1.0 ms (bunny) and 7.6 ms (dragon) against hubs 31 and 410 ms, but its query sort and geometry build make the total on the device 35.5 and 60 ms, so it is 1.15 times slower than hubs on bunny and 6.8 times faster on dragon; whether its radius answers are the 50 nearest was not checked. So where RT wins here: dense surface data at 3.6M points and launch time alone; where it loses: clustered 2D data with a long round tail (Porto), small inputs, and when the answer has to be exact. Misses: warpwise 1.4 to 1.5 times above the interval I wrote, hubs 200 to 430 times faster than warpwise (I wrote 1.5 to 50), bitonic 12 to 22 times faster than warpwise (I wrote within 3 times), dragon hubs 12 times below the interval. Observation: CLOVER bitonic and hubs crash with an illegal memory access when the queries are a random subset of the points (warpwise does not); every point in order works. Caveats: CLOVER spans exclude host copies, RTNN and TrueKNN are not the same problem (radius bounded, box stop rule), one GPU, one run for the slow cells.


228. **2026-11-17, tethys (4090, OptiX only, predictions model/predictions_phase10b.md first plus two addenda, results model/results_phase10b.md), opacity micromaps: the hardware resolves transparent and opaque micro triangles without calling the any hit program, but this does not make traversal faster here.** Random triangle soups (100,000 and 1,000,000), 8.4 million random rays, a disc shaped alpha hole per triangle with exactly classified micro triangles. Any hit invocations against plain any hit: 0.55, 0.14, 0.04, 0.01 at subdivision levels 2, 4, 6, 8 (the unknown area), and the results (hit, primitive, t) are identical to plain any hit on every ray. Time over plain any hit is 1.02 to 1.10 at every level (2 state micromaps with no program at all: 0.78, 0.93, 1.02, 1.11 and wrong on 34, 12, 3, 0.8 percent of rays); opaque geometry is 0.63 of plain any hit. Adding 256 times more math to the any hit program changed nothing (the program is hidden behind the unit), so there is no gain; ncu: the micromap path removes all SM instructions but its memory traffic equals the any hit's. GAS +23 percent. Timing needed a new design: separate processes differ by 3 to 14 percent from memory placement alone (within a process 0.2 percent), so variants were built in one process and launched round robin, ratios over 8 processes. Most time predictions missed (listed in the results file). Not tested: an any hit with real memory loads.
229. **2026-11-17, tethys (4090, our own TTU kernel beside a plain persistent kernel, both directions, 5 repetitions, every RT record checked), concurrent kernels: memory streaming on the TPC sibling SMs costs random ray traversal 6.7 times more than streaming from other TPCs (which has twice the blocks), and the order flips for coherent rays.** RT on 32 SMs; compute on the odd SMs below (siblings), or on SMs 64 to 127 (other TPCs, twice the blocks), or on all odd SMs with RT on 64 SMs. Random rays, streaming a 1 GB buffer: RT runs at 0.022 of its alone speed with sibling compute (0.022 also at G 64, as on triton in F101), but 0.147 with compute on other TPCs. Coherent rays: 0.464 with siblings, 0.277 with other TPCs, and the compute side then loses more beside a sibling (0.56 against 0.75). The "L1" kernel (32 KB windows, 4 blocks, spills) slows RT beside other TPCs too (0.72 random, 0.76 coherent), siblings 0.37 and 0.84. The ALU kernel leaves compute untouched and makes RT about 7 percent faster in every placement (clock or power state suspected, not tested). Compute side with streaming: 0.86 to 0.95 beside random rays. Conclusion: the RT core's memory port is a per TPC resource on top of the chip wide L2 and DRAM path; several predictions missed (listed in the results file).
230. **2026-11-17, tethys (4090, OptiX only), partial warps: traversal cost per traced ray with 1, 8, 16 and 32 active lanes per warp.** 2,097,152 traced rays in every case (the launch is 32 over A times larger), identical hits. 16 active lanes cost 1.03 to 1.05 times a full warp per traced ray, 8 lanes 1.15 to 1.16, one lane per warp 5.1 to 5.4 (a one lane warp finishes about 5.9 times faster than a full warp, not 32 times); ncu: SM warp instructions grow 30 times at one lane for 5 times the time, sectors stay constant. Consecutive lanes or every n-th lane: the same within 6 percent. All my cost predictions were too high (1.4 to 2.1, 2.5 to 4.2, 10 to 32). Baseline cell noisy (12 percent).
231. **2026-11-17, tethys (4090, five third party style programs rebuilt with a lane histogram probe, synthetic data, 3 repeats), divergence profile: callbacks run with 7 to 19 of 32 lanes active, every callback is its own handoff, and 22 to 48 percent of warp time is spent at 8 or fewer lanes.** Mean lanes per intersection callback: RTNN 12.0 and 13.6 (radius 0.025, 0.05), own kNN 11.5, Arkade fixed 10.3 and 11.3, TrueKNN 7.1 (100,000 points) and 12.7 (400,000), Mochi frame 0 19.2 (56 percent of its callbacks at full width). Callbacks with 8 or fewer lanes: 44 to 79 percent. Warp time at 8 or fewer lanes (intervals from the previous callback exit, including the unit round trip): Arkade 0.45 to 0.48, RTNN 0.33 to 0.35, kNN 0.32, TrueKNN 0.22 to 0.30, Mochi 0.10. TrueKNN last round: 1.8 lanes. Callbacks per handoff 1.00 everywhere. Lane statistics identical across repeats; time shares within 1.2 percent; the lane probe costs 3 to 12 percent kernel time on Arkade and 23 to 28 percent on RTNN. Real datasets (triton only) not used. A first version that stamped the raygen end from one lane gave shares above 1 and was discarded.
232. **2026-11-17, tethys (4090, OptiX only), many instances per level: one instance level with up to 65,536 instances of 15 triangles costs no trace time against the flat GAS of the same triangles at similar coverage.** M 65,536 against flat: time 0.99 (hit fraction 0.88 against 0.92), L1 sectors +19 percent, memory 11 MB against 65 MB (shared GAS). One instance (same scene): time 1.00 but 33 percent fewer SM warp instructions than the single GAS pipeline (31.4 M against 47.0 M). The instance structure build takes 2.4 to 3.0 ms at every M from 1 to 65,536 (fixed, probably a first use cost, not separated). Design flaw: at small M the instanced scene covers less of the cube (hit fractions 0.56 to 0.83), so those times cannot be compared to flat. Predictions of a 3 to 12 times cost at 65,536 missed.
233. **2026-11-17, tethys (4090, OptiX only), BVH build time against build flags: one million triangles build in 1.9 ms.** None 1.89 ms (100,000: 0.44), prefer fast trace 1.19 times (1.11), prefer fast build 0.61 (0.72), allow update 0.72 (0.85), refit 0.17 of a build, compaction pass 7 percent of a build (size -25 percent), a tenfold larger scene takes 4.3 times as long. Process spread 0.1 to 9 percent. My predictions of 8 to 30 ms and 0.8 to 4 ms were 4 to 10 times too slow.
234. **2026-11-17, tethys, transform sequence for the unit prepared, NOT run (plan model/plan_xform1.md).** The instance level sequence of F139 and F195 is unchanged: only the instance leaf data changes. setup3 got an option for the instance matrix; the stage 0 and stage 1 builds pass the shape checker; five matrices (rotations, scale 2 with rotation, non uniform scale) times six rays agree between OptiX and a float64 CPU reference (30 of 30), and the register image words handed to the unit are identical to the identity case except two address words. Stage 1 needs approval.
235. **2026-11-17, triton, the current model scored in one table on every validation so far, synthetic and real, with the no change baseline where it applies (no new run, no refit; table model/results_model_table.md, script model/model_table.py): it predicts the speedup of a ray order better than predicting no change in 10 of 12 sets (7 of the 9 sets predicted blind), but absolute time only within 1.1 to 1.9 times on synthetic soups and 2 to 3.5 times (single rows up to 155 times) on the real programs.** "Current model" means the F123 form for speedups, the one SM fit with the sharing form for absolute traversal, and for custom primitive programs the max of the throughput form and the slowest warp (F219) with the measured callback cost per program and input (F216) as a second column; the two prior works forms were not merged into a new form for this table. Speedup sets (rms log error, model against predict 1.0): F123 random soup 0.094 against 0.702, Bistro 0.074 against 0.244, bounce soup 0.072 against 0.318 (held out of the fit, not blind); F205 Sponza 0.083 against 0.062 (worse), Conference 0.014 against 0.064, Rungholt 0.132 against 0.421 (blind); F208 camera Sponza 0.051 against 0.065, Conference 0.086 against 0.091, Rungholt 0.053 against 0.070, bounce Sponza 0.031 against 0.021 (worse), Conference 0.055 against 0.061, Rungholt 0.075 against 0.153 (blind). The two losses are the sets where the measured gain is under 10 percent. Absolute time: one SM held out groups 11 to 19 percent mean error (not blind), 4090 core set within 4.3 percent for latency and 1 percent for throughput (blind), whole GPU 16 to 85 percent (not counted as blind), RTNN soups 1.7 times (rms log 0.51), TrueKNN soups 1.2 to 1.9 times; real data: RTNN median 2.4 times by search compute and 1.3 times by launch time, TrueKNN median 2.0 times with the chain (k 659 155 times off) and 2.9 times with measured callbacks (Porto 128 times off), Arkade median 3.5 times with the F207 form and 3.4 with measured callbacks, Mochi 2.1 to 3.3 times under predicted. No form closes more than 5 of 14 real data rows within a factor 2. Not blind and so not counted: the F123 sets, the one SM groups, Throughput_6, the F219 and F216 forms (formed after the F207 measurements) and F220. Not in the table because the model makes no time prediction there: cache, scheduling, flag, mask, build flag, quantization and sphere measurements, HG_1, the unit and knee thread, the bandwidth bound. So the claim the data support is a speedup predictor plus an absolute estimate good to about a factor 2 to 4 for custom primitive launches once the program is calibrated, not an absolute time predictor.


236. **2026-11-17, triton, model v1 frozen: one combined form (per launch the larger of the throughput form with the measured callback cost of the program and input, and the slowest warp chain, per round for TrueKNN) scores a median factor 2.1 (median absolute log error 0.74) on the 14 real data rows and puts 6 of 14 inside a factor 2, against 5 of 14 for the best earlier form; it is not a blind result (form committed in eb22f98 before scoring, no constant new or refitted, but both parts were formed after the measurements and use calibration runs on the same inputs), and no further form changes will be made to it (model/priorworks/FORM_combined_v1.md, results_model_v1.md).** RTNN: median log error 0.69, 2 of 4 inside the factor (ratios 1.9 to 2.4, always under predicted; the chain never binds). TrueKNN: 0.43, 3 of 4 inside (0.71, 0.59, 1.29; Porto 0.46 just outside), where the callback form alone has 1.05 and the chain alone 0.69, so the combination is better than either part and closes TrueKNN k 659 (0.59) and Porto only through the chain (the callback form alone is 128 times off on Porto). Arkade: 1.10, 1 of 6 inside, all six under predicted by 1.7 to 3.4 times, so Arkade is the open row of the model: the cost per callback call of the program under load is not in the form. Later work compares new rows against this frozen form, not a new one.
